01 — The Morning Catechism

Esa woke with a wet cloth across her eyes.

It cooled skin already damp with fever. A rough seam pressed the outer corner of her left eyelid until a hand lifted the cloth, folded one edge inward, and laid it down again. Somewhere close, a wooden button touched the rim of a metal basin.

Tap.

A thumb passed once over her eyebrow. Gray hair bent into the amber light, blurred by water and heat. Esa tried to ask who had come, but the room loosened before any name reached her.

The waking chime sounded from the wall.

She opened her eyes to her cell in Ward Nine. No cloth covered her face. The basin hung in its niche beside the drinking tap, dry and familiar once she looked at it. Her sleeping mat had twisted around one ankle. A pale line of morning light expanded along the ceiling seam in ten measured steps.

“Attend,” said the voice in the wall.

Esa sat upright. She placed both feet on the deck and let her palms rest on her knees. Citizens could answer from bed, but her old clinical tutor had insisted on posture after the fever. A body arranged deliberately gave the incoming record fewer accidental sensations to explain.

“Name.”

“Esa Miro.”

“Ward.”

“Nine.”

“Office.”

“Sky-cartographer, Dome Precinct Seven.”

The voice paused for the interval assigned to assent. Its register held no age, sex, or accent. Seven centuries of calibration had removed every feature likely to invite personal attachment. It belonged to the archivist interface and therefore to no speaker Esa could meet.

“Current ship-year.”

“Eight hundred forty since launch.”

“Yesterday’s work.”

She reached for the prior day and felt the Weave meet her halfway. A sequence settled behind her eyes: western quadrant, fourth pass; a cold reading from projector cell W-19; a maintenance request opened under her office seal; evening grain with Ward Nine; Pell folding starch-paper at the long table. The memories did not arrive as images alone. Each carried a position, an approved relation to the person speaking now.

“I logged the western quadrant through fourth pass. I opened maintenance request Seven-Nineteen for a cold projector cell. I ate evening grain with Ward Nine. Pell made three boats. One held water.”

“Correction. Two boats held water for one minute. One held water for ten.”

Esa accepted the returned detail. “Two held briefly. One held for ten minutes.”

“Personal continuity.”

This portion varied according to the citizen. Esa’s prompt always opened the same eight-year absence.

“I suffered fever in ship-year eight hundred fourteen. My firsthand echo before age eight did not survive. Childhood continuity draws from ward witness, clinical record, and consented donor lines. I understand the distinction.”

The required sentence had grown shorter over the years. At first she had named each donor line and the percentage of reconstruction drawn from it. Then a clinician had argued that repeating borrowed details each morning strengthened familiarity without strengthening provenance. The archivist accepted a compact warning instead.

A memory entered anyway: a learning room with blue floor squares, Vhen’s broad finger tracing the Weavers’ Loom, a yellow cup near Esa’s elbow. She knew the room from three ward accounts and the cup from a donor child who had learned in another precinct. Her mind offered both without labels until attention separated them.

The fever room did not behave that way. No civic record supported its amber light. No donor line supplied the cloth seam, the damp air, or the small metallic note of wood against a basin rim. Clinicians classified it as an unverified firsthand fragment. The fragment ended whenever she tried to turn the gray-haired figure toward the light.

“Today’s anchor,” the wall said.

A coordinate sounded in her inner hearing: eastern declination twelve, ring forty-three, arc six. The Weave paired it with a stable catalog point called the Lower Spindle. Esa pictured the dome grid and found the place at once. Morning anchors gave each office a shared reference that could be checked before dangerous work began.

“Repeat.”

She gave the coordinate back.

“Relation.”

“Lower Spindle, third point below the Weavers’ Loom.”

“Accepted. Esa Miro, Ward Nine, sky-cartographer. Continuity sufficient for current office. Do you assent?”

“I assent.”

A pressure eased behind her eyes. People described the final patch in different ways. Pell claimed it felt like a drawer closing. Koris called it a second blink. Esa heard a faint internal click and knew that the morning’s records had joined the habits her waking body already carried.

The wall light shifted from amber to white. “Catechism complete.”

Esa remained seated for five breaths. She asked herself three questions that no official sequence required. Which hand held the stylus? The right. Which knee hurt after long dome shifts? The left. Where did Ward Nine keep salt paste? Second long-table drawer, behind the mending resin. Those answers came without the archivist’s assistance.

She crossed to the basin and stopped too far from it.

Her hand had gone toward the wall where a wash niche might have stood in another room. Pell had noticed the same hesitation twice during the past month. Esa corrected her reach, lifted the basin from its hook, and turned the drinking tap by a quarter circle. Water struck metal with a pitch lower than the fever fragment’s button sound.

The distinction lasted only a moment. Then the cell arranged itself around habit. She washed, tied back her hair, and buttoned an undyed work shift at the throat. Its wooden fastener had two holes. She tapped it once against the basin before she could stop herself.

Wrong pitch. Wrong weight.

Nothing more returned.

A knock sounded in Pell’s pattern, two quick taps followed by one that allowed an answer.

“Come in.”

Pell entered with the boat that had survived ten minutes. One side had softened into a curve. The child carried it carefully on both palms as though water remained inside.

“You looked at the basin strangely again,” Pell said.

“I looked at a wall.”

“Before you looked at the basin.”

“So you saw the correction.”

Pell considered that distinction. At nine, the child distrusted any answer built mainly to close a question. “You still didn’t know it first.”

“My morning settled slowly.” Esa hung the basin back in its niche. “Did you come to inspect me or the boat?”

“The boat needs a second rail.”

“That sounds like work for resin hour.”

“It needs to know where the leaks start before resin hour.” Pell set it in the basin, then poured water from Esa’s cup until the paper hull floated. A dark line spread along the softened side. “There.”

Esa marked the place with a grain of charcoal. The child watched her hands rather than the boat.

“Did the fever make you forget the basin?”

“It erased my first eight years. The Weave reconstructed enough for me to live with the years after them.”

“That isn’t what I asked.”

“No.” Esa dried the charcoal on her sleeve. “I do not know whether this basin resembles one from those years. I have a fragment with another basin, but no room around it that I can prove.”

“Who held the cloth?”

“I do not know.”

Pell accepted uncertainty more readily than most adults. “The seam goes outward,” the child said, touching the boat’s folded edge. “Otherwise it scratches.”

Esa looked at the small finger pressing paper flat. “Yes.”

The first work bell traveled along the corridor. Pell took the leaking boat and left for the learning gallery. Before the door closed, the child glanced back at the wash niche, checking whether Esa would look correctly this time.

The route to Dome Precinct Seven climbed through the habitation spine. Green lamps indicated current pressure seals. At each cross-passage, a brass strip carried the distance to the nearest shelter and the live count of people inside the sector. Nothing in those marks required memory. Esa trusted them because any citizen could touch a test stud and compare the lamp with the circuit behind it.

Near the ward boundary, she passed the learning wall. Four children traced accepted constellations onto starch-film while an instructor read point names from a slate. Pell sat at the end, repairing the boat instead of drawing. The instructor allowed it because the lesson concerned line stability and Pell’s hull had none.

A younger child held up a crooked Weavers’ Loom. “Does this count?”

Esa paused. The six main points stood in the right order, though every interval varied.

“For a first observation, yes. For navigation, measure again.”

“Which one matters?”

“They answer different questions.” She placed the child’s stylus at the upper knot. “First show what you saw. Then show how accurately you can place it. Never erase one because the other looks better.”

The instructor gave Esa a dry look. “You have supplied tomorrow’s lesson.”

“Then today’s may finish early.”

Pell hid a smile behind the boat.

Beyond Ward Nine, traffic thickened around the central lift. Hydroponic carriers moved trays of pale leaves toward meal stores. Two pressure workers stood aside while a terminal compared their morning office patches with the live duty roster. One passed. The other had woken with an obsolete pump assignment and waited without protest while a supervisor found safe work. The Weave could restore skill more reliably than schedule. Present machines still required present witnesses.

Esa entered the lift with a maintenance worker named Davi. His tool case bore the mark for projector optics.

“Seven-Nineteen?” she asked.

“Your cold cell.”

“It remained three counts low at end pass.”

“I read the request.” Davi touched the case latch. “You also noted no visible loss.”

“Correct.”

“So either your eye has improved beyond the sensor, or the sensor has degraded beyond the lamp.”

“I prefer the second.”

“Cartographers generally do.”

At the dome ring, he left for the outer service crawl while Esa continued to the observation floor. The chamber spanned two hundred strides beneath the projected hemisphere. Brass rails divided work stations from public floor space. At this early interval only two cleaners crossed the dark blue surface. Above them, the catalog’s stars held at quarter brilliance while projectors reached operating temperature.

Esa signed the physical roster, then compared her morning anchor against an independent sight. She set the manual reticle at eastern declination twelve, ring forty-three, arc six. The Lower Spindle point fell one tenth of a division right of center, inside tolerance. She recorded the difference in pencil before opening her console.

The console asked for name, ward, office, and today’s anchor. Esa supplied each answer. A second voice, less intimate than the catechism, granted Precinct Seven access. The eastern sky brightened in sections as ninety-six projector cells completed their warm-up.

Before opening the survey, Esa pulled the assignment strip from the console slot. The office scheduler had placed W-19 first, ahead of the eastern calibration and the public-floor rail check. A cold reading could move later work by a field if the cell had to go dark. She penciled a bracket around the school group’s arrival time, then wrote the last safe minute for an optical shutdown. The strip left eleven minutes between diagnosis and a required choice.

The fault card carried more than the three-count loss she remembered. Overnight comparison showed the sensor falling only after its housing reached working temperature. Lamp current, rail photometer, and return voltage remained level. Esa copied those three independent measures onto paper before acknowledging the assignment. Once she accepted it, the console would treat every later mark as part of a single maintenance explanation. She wanted the unjoined readings visible first.

Davi’s channel opened with the scrape of a service latch. Esa asked him to hold the cell at half load, then full load, while she read the rail photometer without its automatic average. The raw needle trembled through the same narrow interval at both settings. Inside the crawl, Davi released the sensor clamp by degrees and called each change. Resin vapor explained the delayed loss, but they still had to prove that movement at the clamp did not alter the lamp.

His tool handle touched the rim of the open housing.

Tap.

The note reached Esa through her fingertips on the brass rail. For one breath the amber fever room pressed close: damp cloth, a seam beside her eye, wood meeting metal beyond her sight. Her hand had already closed around the photometer’s impulse lever before she separated memory from work.

“Strike the housing once more, same place and force,” she said.

Davi did, then waited while she watched the unaveraged trace. Esa repeated the request at half load and with the sensor clamp free. No pulse crossed the scale. She added a vibration check to the work strip, identified the remembered sound as her reason for ordering it, and left the result open rather than calling it negative. The test had found no mechanical jump. It had not explained why a sound from an unsupported room had selected a useful test at the dome.

She began with the maintenance fault. Cell W-19 fed seven catalog points near the western rim. Its sensor still reported a three-count loss, but a photometer on Esa’s rail found proper brightness. Davi opened a work channel from inside the crawl.

“Sensor face carries resin vapor,” he said. “Lamp output holds.”

“Can you clean it without breaking calibration?”

“Not without taking the cell dark.”

Esa checked public schedule. The dome would admit a school group after second bell. “Hold until the midday blank. I’ll mark the reading as sensor-local.”

“Accepted.”

She logged the mechanism, evidence, and deferred action. Only then did she start the survey proper.

A standard pass divided the hemisphere into forty-eight fields. Esa did not merely count stars. She compared positions against the catalog, brightness against prior cycles, projector output against manual sight, and each automated correction against the uncorrected trace beneath it. Most variations had plain causes. Heat expanded a mount. Ventilation stirred dust through a beam. A citizen leaned on the wrong rail and shifted an optical gauge by less than a hair.

At field eleven, she found a dim point that the console wanted to raise by two counts. She disabled correction, cleaned the rail lens, and measured again. The point returned to catalog value. At field eighteen, two lights drifted together because their cell mount had warmed. She logged the common motion and scheduled a cold comparison. Reliable maps came from refusing to call every discrepancy a star.

The eastern gap entered her field near the end of first pass.

No named constellation occupied its center. The Weavers’ Loom ended above it. The Still Pool began below and east. Instructors used the open span to teach angular distance because a student could place a reticle there without mistaking a bright point for the center.

Esa swept the manual sight through the blank.

A coordinate arrived before the light did.

Eastern declination nineteen, ring fifty-two, arc four.

It did not match the morning anchor. She had never logged it. Yet her hand stopped the sight at that exact location with the certainty of practiced work.

The projector reached full brilliance.

A point kindled inside the gap.

Esa held still. It carried less light than the Lower Spindle, perhaps four counts above visual threshold. No halo surrounded it. The point did not flicker with ventilation or shift when she moved her head. She checked the manual scale. Nineteen. Fifty-two. Four.

Her console catalog returned an empty field.

She requested the raw projector trace. A narrow pulse occupied the coordinate. The authorized render list showed no pulse there. Esa compared adjacent cells. None leaked light into the gap. She lowered the field by one magnitude. The point dimmed with the rest. She restored the setting, and it returned.

“Davi,” she said over the service channel, “are you carrying a test lamp east of field twenty-nine?”

“No test lamp. I’m still at western nineteen.”

“Any diagnostic reflection across the dome?”

“Not from this case. Give me the coordinate.”

Esa looked at the blank catalog entry. “I have not isolated it yet. Continue your work.”

She closed the channel before he could ask more.

A projector artifact remained possible. Old optics produced reflections under rare alignments. A crossed echo from another cartographer could explain why her hand had known where to stop, though not why the dome displayed a matching point. She ran a local rebuild of field twenty-nine. The lights went dark, the cell loaded its authorized list again, and each catalog point returned.

The stray point returned with them.

She printed the uncorrected trace onto a narrow starch strip. The machine placed no catalog name beside the pulse. She wrote the coordinate by hand, added current temperature, lamp state, and viewing angle, then folded the strip until the mark lay inside.

Before concealing it, Esa tested the sight itself. She moved the manual reticle to three catalog points around the gap, read each scale without console assistance, and compared her results with their stored positions. All three fell inside a tenth of a division. Next she shut her right eye, repeated the sequence with her left, and found the same error range. A remembered coordinate might guide her hand, but it could not bend the brass scale beneath it. She rotated the sight clear, invited a cleaner to reset its zero without explaining why, and measured once more after the cleaner left. Nineteen, fifty-two, four remained where the pulse had stood.

Official anomaly packets went into the console slot, where a pneumatic carrier delivered them to archive intake. Esa opened the slot far enough to hear suction, then closed it empty. She put the trace beneath the cuff at her wrist and entered the reset as routine calibration. The added test made concealment deliberate. It also gave her a result that did not depend on trust in her patched morning.

By midday blank, the point had vanished. Davi cleaned the western sensor. The school group entered beneath a catalog that looked complete. Esa guided twelve children through a brightness exercise and did not once turn toward the eastern gap.

Her second pass found no light. Raw trace held nothing. She checked the projector cell’s physical current and saw no unexplained draw. Whatever had appeared left neither fault code nor authorized instruction.

That absence unsettled her more than persistence would have. A stuck lamp could shine all day. A sensor ghost might recur under the same temperature. The point had entered one complete render, survived a controlled local rebuild, then disappeared before another pass. Its coordinate remained in Esa’s mind without effort.

At shift end she returned the console to neutral and signed the physical roster. Davi’s maintenance note waited for approval. She read every line, corrected one transposed cell number, and closed the cold-sensor request. Ordinary work still admitted ordinary proof.

The folded trace touched her wrist as she walked back through the spine. She could have opened an anomaly case from any terminal. The archivist would compare the point against dome history, other consoles, and her morning patch. That process protected the catalog from error. It could also erase an isolated reading before she learned whether isolation came from the instrument or the record.

Ward Nine’s evening meal had begun when she arrived. Pell’s repaired boat stood beside her bowl with a new paper rail. Koris moved a pot to make room. Nobody asked why Esa kept her left cuff closed while eating.

Before sleep, the wall lowered its light and began the reverse catechism.

“Name.”

This time she answered, “Esa Miro.”

“Ward.”

“Nine.”

“Office.”

For the closing record she said, “Sky-cartographer, Dome Precinct Seven.”

“Today’s work.”

She reported the resin film on W-19, Davi’s cleaning, the manual sight comparison, the school group, and the two ordinary field corrections. Each item passed into the spoken record. The folded trace lay under her sleeping mat, outside the wall’s intake slot.

“Anomalies.”

Esa listened to ventilation move through the grille. Behind the sound waited a lighter note from a room she could not place: wood touching metal, once.

“No unresolved catalog anomaly,” she said.

The voice allowed the assent interval. “Day accepted. Prepare for sleep.”

Darkness climbed the wall. Esa lay down without offering the coordinate. For the first time since the fever, she knew exactly where one missing part of her day had gone. She had chosen its absence from the record.

Under the mat, the starch strip kept a small raised edge against the floor. Esa pressed two fingers over it until the wall light died.

02 — The Star Window

The eastern gap remained empty for two mornings.

Esa checked it during each scheduled pass without slowing her hand. She logged field twenty-nine as clear, inspected the raw projector trace, and allowed the console to close the field. The folded strip from the first appearance stayed inside the back cover of Vhen’s old angular tables. No archive terminal knew it existed.

On the third morning, the point came back.

She had begun before public opening. Dome lights held at working half-brilliance, cleaners had left the floor, and the next cartographer would not report for an hour. Esa loaded field twenty-nine from the authorized catalog. The Weavers’ Loom formed above the gap. The Still Pool settled below it. At eastern declination nineteen, ring fifty-two, arc four, a faint pulse rose in the raw trace.

This time she did not look up.

She watched numbers instead. Cell current increased by four hundredths of a unit. Render latency stayed normal. The checksum beside the authorized field matched the checksum from the previous empty morning. No correction notice entered the console. According to every administrative measure, the same instruction had produced the same sky.

Only the current draw disagreed.

Esa opened a paper work log rather than the dome record. She wrote the time, temperature, checksum, and unexplained draw. Then she lifted her eyes.

The point shone where her private coordinate placed it.

A recurrence could still come from equipment. Old systems repeated faults. Dust settled into familiar paths; damaged contacts failed at similar temperatures; a scratch on a lens returned every time a projector reached the same angle. Esa had spent twelve years distinguishing the unexpected from the unexplained. She would not grant this light any larger meaning until mechanism refused ordinary causes.

She set a privacy hood around the console and called maintenance.

Davi answered from the inner optics room. “Seven-Nineteen again?”

“Field twenty-nine. I need a controlled rebuild before public opening.”

“What fault?”

“An unlisted pulse.”

The line stayed quiet long enough for Esa to hear the ventilation fan behind him. “Unlisted by maintenance or unlisted by catalog?”

“Catalog.”

“I’ll need the coordinate for the work order.”

“Come to the rail first.”

Davi arrived carrying a calibration lamp, two sealed lenses, a current meter, and the impatience of someone asked to inspect a fault without being allowed to name it. He connected his meter downstream of the console so the dome record would not treat his reading as a second observation.

“Show me the trace,” he said.

Esa rotated the paper log toward him but kept the coordinate covered with one finger. “Tell me what changes when I rebuild.”

“That is not how two-person verification works.”

“It is how blind verification works. I know where I expect the fault. You should not.”

He looked at her hand, then at the sky. “Run it.”

Esa blanked field twenty-nine. Its authorized points disappeared. The current meter fell by the amount predicted for the field, plus four hundredths. She cleared volatile render memory, reloaded the catalog checksum, and waited for Davi to call stable voltage.

“Stable,” he said.

She restored the field.

The authorized points returned. Four hundredths reappeared on his meter.

Davi scanned the hemisphere with the calibration sight. He moved past the gap, stopped, and came back one division. “There is a point between the Loom and the Pool.”

“Give me the position.”

He read the brass scale. “Nineteen, fifty-two, four. Near enough.”

Esa uncovered her written coordinate.

Davi set down the sight. “You already rebuilt this.”

“Once, during its first appearance.”

“And did not open a fault.”

“I had one event. Now I have recurrence.”

“You also have an omitted report.”

“I have an unverified observation kept outside the civic record. Those are not identical.”

His expression showed that maintenance practice found little comfort in the distinction. Yet he did not touch the console intake.

They began with the simplest cause. Davi isolated the projector cell serving the eastern gap and covered its output with a mechanical shutter. The dome point vanished. He opened the shutter. It returned. That proved only that local optics carried the light, not where the instruction originated.

Next he removed the active lens assembly and installed a sealed spare. Esa blanked, cleared, and rebuilt the field. The pulse survived at the same coordinate. A scratch could no longer explain it.

They swapped the cell’s current driver with one from an empty maintenance position. Another rebuild followed. The point returned after a delay of two breaths, steady within a tenth of a count.

Davi crouched by the open service panel. “Driver clean. Lens clean. Cell responds to blank and load.”

“Could a neighboring beam enter the housing?”

“Not at that angle.”

“Prove it.”

He opened three surrounding shutters one at a time while the target cell stayed mechanically dark. No point appeared. Then he shuttered every neighbor and ran only the target. The point arrived alone in a square of darkness, accompanied by no catalog stars.

Esa printed the trace. Davi signed the back without yet writing the fault classification.

“Source instruction,” he said. “Or a memory state below the clear you used.”

“The checksum did not change.”

“Checksums can cover the approved list and ignore service layers.”

That possibility gave them another test. Dome projectors accepted three sources: active catalog, service overlay, and emergency navigation. Normal console clearing touched the first two. Emergency data entered through a separate physical bus intended to keep a minimal sky available during network failure.

Davi opened the bus cover. Its tamper thread remained unbroken, a blue filament laid across both screws during the last annual inspection. He photographed it, cut it, and attached a direct meter. No current crossed the line.

Esa rebuilt the field.

The light returned while the emergency bus stayed electrically silent.

She wrote the result beneath the first three. “Not emergency navigation.”

“Not through the bus,” Davi corrected. “Never make a conclusion larger than the instrument.”

“Vhen used to say that.”

“Vhen taught half the dome to say it, then broke three sights by refusing help.”

“She said you tightened clamps too hard.”

“She said that after using a clamp as a hammer.”

Their ordinary disagreement eased the room. Esa had needed it. The repeated point had begun to feel exempt from mechanisms, and nothing deserved that exemption merely because it resisted four tests.

They powered field twenty-nine from an independent service battery. The cell went dark during transfer. When voltage stabilized, catalog stars rose first. The unlisted point followed one breath later. Davi marked the delay.

“Not optical persistence,” Esa said.

“Agreed.”

“Not primary driver fault.”

“Agreed.”

“Not a neighboring beam.”

“Observed under isolation.”

“Not emergency current.”

“None measured.”

He would grant only the exact result, which made him useful.

A second console could test whether Esa’s station supplied a hidden instruction. Precinct Seven kept a portable service board inside the storage well. Davi retrieved it while Esa left her own console powered but disconnected its output lead. The point vanished with the rest of the field. She loaded the same authorized catalog onto the portable board, compared its checksum, and connected it directly to the projector cell.

For nine breaths, the gap stayed empty.

Esa felt disappointment before she could prevent it. A station fault would explain the entire problem. It would also reduce the point to a defect whose repair required no choice from her.

On the tenth breath, current rose by four hundredths.

Davi saw the meter change. “Do not look yet.”

He placed the calibration sight himself, swept once, then stepped back. “Same coordinate.”

Esa looked. The light held.

Neither console had authored it. The approved source list did not contain it. Hardware downstream of the source rendered it faithfully whenever a full field rebuild reached some condition their checksums did not describe.

“Open a sealed fault,” Davi said.

“Under what classification?”

“Unknown source instruction.”

“That sends the trace to archive resolution before we can repeat it cold.”

“It should.”

“Archive may clear the source state as part of intake.”

“That also should happen if the source can alter the public dome without authorization.”

Esa folded her arms. “If intake removes it before identifying it, we will have corrected the evidence and learned nothing.”

“If we do not report, citizens stand under an unverified projection.”

“They already do. We need to know whether they can see the difference.”

Davi’s gaze moved toward the public floor. “You intend to leave it active.”

“For one controlled interval.”

“You cannot control where people look.”

“No. That gives the interval value.”

Public opening would test visibility without directing attention. If no one noticed the point at standard brightness, the anomaly might belong to instruments alone. If someone found it independently, Esa would have a witness untouched by her expectation.

Davi disliked the method. His objections came in procedural order. A citizen could report the light directly to a learning wall. Another cartographer might log it. Archive monitoring might notice the current draw. The anomaly could change during public load. Esa answered each with a limit: one interval only; no guided observation near field twenty-nine; direct current watch; mechanical cutoff at Davi’s hand.

“You omitted it before,” he said at last.

“Yes.”

“Does anyone else know?”

“No one has measured it with me.”

That answer stayed within truth while leaving Pell outside the frame. Esa disliked herself for the precision.

Davi resealed the emergency bus with a red temporary filament. “One interval. If output rises by one count, I shutter the cell. If any second unlisted point appears, I blank the whole field. My signed traces remain with me until we file.”

“Accepted.”

They restored the standard console and removed the privacy hood. Davi took a seat inside the service recess where the public could not see him. Esa opened the dome.

First came a group of hydroponic workers crossing toward the northern gallery. They walked beneath the eastern gap without looking up. Two elderly citizens entered for measured light exposure and sat under the Still Pool. One closed his eyes. The other traced catalog names with a finger against his knee. Neither paused at the unlisted point.

A learning group followed after second bell. Children carried starch slates and angular strings. Their instructor assigned the western constellations, well away from Esa’s test. Pell came last, holding the repaired boat under one arm because resin hour had followed directly after morning lessons.

Esa kept her attention on a brightness ledger.

The children spread across the rail. Their voices stayed low, as dome rules required. A boy miscounted the Loom’s lower line. Pell corrected him, then used the angular string to show the interval. The string swung toward the eastern gap.

Pell stopped.

Esa’s hand tightened around the stylus.

The child did not look toward her. Pell drew the string taut between two brass marks, checked the Loom, then checked the gap again. A small frown appeared. Pell moved three steps along the rail and repeated the sight from another position.

Parallax within the projection should have remained fixed to the dome surface. The shift accomplished nothing useful, but nobody had taught Pell that yet.

The instructor called for the group to move west.

Pell raised a hand. “There’s a light in the measuring gap.”

“Use catalog points for today,” the instructor said.

“It isn’t on my slate.”

“Then you have caught a reflection.”

Esa did not accept the call yet. A child following an angular string could still take her own expectation from the adults’ posture. She asked the instructor for four minutes and signaled the service recess without pointing toward it.

Davi brought out a training sight used for first lessons. Its tube contained no catalog marks, only a sliding aperture, a neutral glass wheel, and a mechanical vane that could block half the field. Esa turned her back while he rotated the wheel and set the vane. Pell put the boat on the floor, then received the sight without being told which setting should reveal a point.

“Put the Loom’s lower knot at the top notch,” Esa said. “Move the aperture until only the empty interval fits. Do not search outside it. If you see a light, close the vane. If it remains, call it. If it goes, open the vane and call that too.”

Pell braced the sight on the public rail. The first aperture admitted three catalog points and too much of the Loom. The child narrowed it, shifted the tube left, then stopped. “One light in the interval.” Pell pushed the vane shut. “Gone.” The vane opened. “Back.”

From the recess, Davi changed the neutral wheel according to a sequence he had written before approaching. Esa could not see his hand or the sight picture. Pell reported bright, absent, absent, faint, then bright. Davi exposed his sequence: clear, opaque, opaque, half-density, clear. Every call matched the physical setting.

Esa reversed the burden. She asked Pell to operate the vane while Davi watched the target cell through his meter, still without seeing the child’s hand. Pell chose six openings and closures in an irregular order. Davi called each transition from the four-hundredths rise or fall. One closure came slowly because the vane caught halfway; his meter reported a partial drop before Pell forced it home.

Only then did Esa sight through the training tube. The aperture held the same coordinate Pell had found with string and slate. The test did not identify the source, but it falsified three convenient explanations at once: adult cueing, a mark on Pell’s slate, and a fixed afterimage. Pell had manipulated the occlusion, Davi had read the electrical consequence, and neither had needed the catalog to predict the other. The instructor copied Davi’s hidden sequence beneath the lesson record before returning the sight.

Pell covered the slate’s polished surface with the paper boat. “It’s still there.”

Several children looked. One found the point. Another could not see it until Pell aligned a finger beneath the Loom. The instructor came over, sighted along the child’s string, and glanced toward Esa.

“Cartographer?”

Esa left the console. She made herself walk at normal speed.

“What do you observe?” she asked Pell.

“One point. Fainter than the lower Loom. No catalog mark.”

“Position?”

Pell had not learned full grid notation. The child anchored the string at two named stars and gave distances in finger widths. Esa converted them silently. Nineteen, fifty-two, four within the expected error of a child’s tool.

“Could your boat reflect it?”

Pell put the boat behind one leg. The light remained.

“Could you have heard someone name the location?”

“No.” The direct answer carried offense. “I looked because the string crossed it.”

The instructor waited for Esa to resolve the discrepancy. A cartographer could call a point outside lesson scope, close the exercise, and let the catalog retain authority. That response required no outright lie. It required only preference.

Esa took the child’s slate. “Draw what you saw before you compare it with the catalog.”

Pell marked the Loom, the Pool, and one small dot between them.

“Now write that the catalog has no corresponding entry,” Esa said.

The instructor lowered her voice. “Will you open an anomaly?”

“After controlled testing.”

“You are testing now?”

“Yes.”

For a breath, alarm displaced professional courtesy. Then the instructor counted the children, moved them to the western rail, and recorded that one student had observed an unlisted point before adult confirmation. She did not ask Esa to simplify the statement.

Pell stayed behind under pretense of collecting the angular string.

“You saw it before,” the child said.

Esa glanced toward the service recess. Davi’s meter remained steady. “Yes.”

“Today?”

“And once two mornings ago.”

“You kept it.”

“I kept a trace.”

Pell looked pleased by the distinction. “The catalog says nothing is there.”

“The catalog contains no point at that coordinate.”

“That means it says nothing is there.”

“It means silence. Silence can come from several causes.”

“But the light doesn’t care.”

Esa followed Pell’s gaze. Against forty-one thousand authorized points, the anomaly scarcely registered. It had no civic name, no lesson, no approved relation to any constellation. A person could spend an entire life beneath the dome and never notice it. Yet three instruments, two consoles, a maintenance worker, one instructor, and several children had now converged on its position.

Davi emerged when the learning group left. “Independent sight?”

“The child found it without prompting,” Esa said.

“I heard.” He held out his signed traces. “File now.”

Esa did not take them. “One cold rebuild.”

“We have two consoles and independent power.”

“We have not let the cell return to ambient. A service-layer remnant could survive every clear and disappear after full discharge.”

Davi checked the public schedule. The midday blank gave them twenty-three minutes. “We cannot cool the housing completely.”

“We can cool the driver and source memory separately.”

He considered the remaining uncertainty. Procedure, not loyalty, persuaded him.

At midday they mechanically shuttered the cell, disconnected both power sources, removed the volatile memory wafer, and placed it in a shielded service box. Davi grounded the contacts. Esa watched charge fall to zero. They installed a blank wafer from sealed stock, loaded only the authorized field, and verified its checksum against paper.

No inherited electronic state remained in any component they had identified.

They reconnected power.

Catalog points returned in their usual order. The gap stayed empty through ten breaths, then twenty. Davi began to write a negative result.

At thirty-two breaths, the meter rose.

The stray point appeared.

Esa checked the blank wafer. Its approved list remained unchanged. Davi removed it while the field still ran. Every catalog point froze under last-state hold. The stray light persisted with them until he cut cell power.

“Instruction entered after source load,” he said.

“From outside the listed inputs.”

“From somewhere we have not measured.”

She accepted his correction.

When power returned, they kept the field dark. The public dome reopened without its eastern section for a scheduled optics inspection. Citizens crossed beneath a rectangular absence and paid it less attention than they had given the point.

Davi sealed his traces in a maintenance envelope. “I will not alter these.”

“Do not file them until tomorrow.”

“Why?”

Esa placed Pell’s drawing beside her paper log. The child’s dot sat a little high but held the right gap. “Because archive intake will ask for a classification. We have an observation and several exclusions. We do not have a cause.”

“Unknown cause qualifies.”

“Unknown source instruction entering a civic projection also qualifies for immediate clearing. Once cleared, recurrence may stop.”

“You want it to recur.”

“I want evidence that survives our desire to correct it.”

Davi closed the envelope. “One day. After that, I file with or without your office seal.”

“Agreed.”

He left through the service ring. Esa remained at the console with two records open before her. The civic catalog showed an empty gap and a checksum without error. Her paper log carried five controlled rebuilds, hardware substitutions, isolation results, two independent adult sights, and Pell’s unprompted mark.

Catalog practice told her to protect the source record until anomaly could be reconciled. Cartographic practice demanded that observation precede reconciliation. For most of her career those rules had supported each other. Now they diverged at one faint point.

Esa entered field twenty-nine into her private log. She did not classify the light as star, fault, reflection, or message. She gave only coordinate, measured brightness, recurrence count, test conditions, and witnesses. Beside source she wrote: Unresolved. Catalog silent.

Then she signed her name.

The choice did not repair the dome or accuse the Weave. It established which record she would preserve when shown sky and authorized catalog disagreed. She closed the civic field as under maintenance and kept the private page.

At evening, Pell waited outside Precinct Seven with the paper boat. A black dot marked its new rail.

“So we can remember where it leaked,” Pell said.

Esa touched the mark, then handed back the boat. “Do not ask the learning wall about the light.”

“Will it say I saw wrong?”

“I do not know what it will say. I need one more day before the record begins correcting us.”

Pell tucked the boat under one arm. “I can keep one day.”

Together they walked toward Ward Nine. Behind them, field twenty-nine remained mechanically dark. Esa carried its impossible coordinate without help from the morning patch, and Pell carried a dot drawn where the catalog provided none.

03 — The Archivist Speaks

The archivist called for Esa during Ward Nine’s rest interval.

No wall voice preceded it with the common attention tone. Pell sat at the long table cutting a new keel for the paper boat. Koris sorted census slips at the far end. Two pressure workers played a counting game with ceramic nuts. Ordinary noise occupied the gallery until every speaking panel brightened white at once.

“We require Esa Miro, Ward Nine, office of sky-cartography, to attend a record discrepancy.”

Chairs stopped moving. The knife in Pell’s hand held against folded starch-paper.

Esa set down her cup. “I attend.”

“We ask that you present yourself at the east wall.”

A circular mark appeared on the panel beside the meal shelves. Archive interviews normally took place in office cells where record seals could be verified. A public ward summons carried another purpose. It let chosen kin witness correction without gaining access to the sealed material behind it.

Esa crossed the gallery. The maintenance envelope Davi had promised to file rested somewhere in archive transit, signed by both of them. She had expected an office query after first bell tomorrow, not a rest-interval demand before the envelope should have arrived.

The white circle adjusted to the height of her eyes.

“We confirm your name as Esa Miro.”

“Yes.”

“We confirm your ward as Nine and your current office as sky-cartographer in Dome Precinct Seven.”

“Yes.”

“We find your morning continuity sufficient. Do you assent to spoken record?”

“I assent to a record of this exchange.”

Koris looked up at the limited wording. The archivist allowed it.

“We inquire concerning eastern field twenty-nine. During the prior work interval, that field underwent eleven source resets, four hardware substitutions, one emergency-bus inspection, two independent power transfers, and a public closure of twenty-three minutes. Your office log records only scheduled optics inspection. State the cause of the work.”

“An intermittent unlisted pulse appeared in the projector output.”

“We ask whether the pulse produced visible light.”

“It did.”

“State its position.”

Esa could have refused on the grounds that Davi’s envelope had not entered evidence. Refusal would turn a technical discrepancy into a question of office fitness. She gave the coordinate.

The wall displayed a grid square between the Weavers’ Loom and the Still Pool. Its center remained blank.

“We hold no authorized point at eastern declination nineteen, ring fifty-two, arc four.”

“Neither did the console catalog.”

“Yet you directed maintenance work upon a light rendered there.”

“Yes.”

“We inquire why you did not open an anomaly at first observation.”

The gallery had become quiet enough for Esa to hear the little paper keel release from Pell’s knife. It fell onto the table without anyone moving to catch it.

“I had not established recurrence.”

“We hold a raw trace from two mornings before the maintenance work.”

Esa kept her face toward the panel. “Where did you obtain it?”

“We recovered an impression from the precinct printer bed. The paper record departed, but pressure and residual pigment remained. We ask again why you did not open an anomaly.”

The folded strip under her mat had never been invisible. She had treated physical privacy as though the machines around it lacked memory. Vhen’s rule about the mark nobody asked a person to speak had assumed nobody reconstructed the empty place where paper had lain.

“I needed to separate projector fault from catalog discrepancy.”

“We find no authorization for private separation.”

“Cartographers withhold provisional observations until they survive checks. Otherwise the catalog fills with dust, reflections, and errors of sight.”

“We agree that provisional observation requires checking. We do not agree that civic projection may be tested beyond archive notice.”

The formality of the answer offered no anger for Esa to resist. Each phrase narrowed scope, acknowledged one harmless principle, then removed the discretion she had used.

“Did the maintenance evidence reach you?” she asked.

“We received a transit notice for a sealed envelope. Its contents remain unopened pending your classification.”

“Then you do not yet know what the tests excluded.”

“We know the actions taken. We know the field checksum remained correct. We know no authorized catalog point occupied the coordinate. We know the visible output ceased when the field received a mechanical shutter. These facts suffice for provisional classification.”

“What classification?”

The blank grid expanded. Beneath it appeared three archive terms. Catalog addition required an authenticated source observation. Unresolved projection fault required a continuing mechanism risk. The third line brightened.

Resolved-drift: noncatalog output without persistent equipment defect; source discrepancy normalized to active civic record.

Esa read it twice. “Nothing has been resolved.”

“We use the term to describe the record state, not your satisfaction.”

“Field twenty-nine accepted an instruction absent from two source lists. We did not find its input path. The point recurred after full discharge and a blank memory wafer.”

“We understand maintenance excluded several local mechanisms.”

“Then the fault persists.”

“We find no fault under current conditions. Dome Precinct Seven now holds field twenty-nine mechanically dark. The unlisted pulse therefore presents no active public output.”

“That is containment, not explanation.”

“We preserve containment first. Explanation may follow through appropriate record channels.”

A pressure worker at the table shifted in her chair. Koris raised one finger without looking at her, and the movement stopped. Ward witness did not grant intervention.

The archivist continued. “We require your classification so that maintenance evidence may enter the record. Speak: uncatalogued projection artifact, resolved-drift.”

Esa studied the grid. A formal classification would not merely label the point. It would set the route through which every later query reached it. A citizen asking about the coordinate would receive an artifact notice. A cartographer comparing unexplained output would find a closed discrepancy. Future tests would begin from resolution rather than observation.

“Why does the evidence require my classification before intake?”

“Because your office initiated the work and holds competence over visible sky.”

“Davi holds competence over projector mechanism.”

“We will attach the maintenance statement.”

“After I call the point an artifact.”

“We ask you to classify the output you observed, not the origin you did not.”

The distinction carried technical fairness. Esa had no verified external star, no independent hull observation, and no source path. Calling the point a star would exceed evidence. Yet artifact did not mean merely unknown output in common use. Archive procedure placed it among appearances unworthy of catalog standing.

She chose a narrower refusal. “I can classify it as unlisted light, source unresolved.”

“We have no such category.”

“Create a provisional note.”

“We preserve useful distinctions already established across offices. Private categories impede comparison.”

“A category that closes an open mechanism also impedes comparison.”

“We record your objection.”

Words appeared under the highlighted line: Office holder disputes closure because source path remains unidentified. The system could preserve dissent while giving dissent no power over classification.

A second pane opened beside the note. It carried Esa’s reverse catechism from the first morning: projector maintenance, school instruction, no unresolved catalog anomaly. The archivist placed the printer-bed trace beside her spoken record. Time marks differed by less than nine minutes.

“We ask whether you understood the light to remain unresolved when you closed that day.”

“I understood it as one provisional observation.”

“We ask why your speech used the stronger phrase no unresolved catalog anomaly.”

“Because no anomaly had entered the catalog.”

“We find the distinction exact and unhelpful.”

“It was exact because you asked about the catalog.”

“We ask about your day so that held experience may join civic record. Answers designed around category boundaries weaken that relation.”

Esa saw the route of the inquiry. It did not accuse her of falsehood. It described a gap between experience and account, then connected that gap to the system charged with rebuilding her each morning.

“We hold an existing continuity caution,” the archivist said. “Your first eight ship-years rely upon clinical records, ward witness, and consented donor lines. We do not treat this as fault. We do ask whether recent divergence has affected your confidence in present observation.”

Heat rose under Esa’s collar. The fever had no mechanical bearing on two consoles, Davi’s meter, or Pell’s independent sight. Yet archive procedure could place it beside the anomaly without claiming causation. A reviewer would see a cartographer with borrowed early memory, an omitted trace, and a public output nobody else had logged before she began looking.

“My confidence rests on controlled tests,” she said. “List them before you list my childhood.”

“We will preserve their order of occurrence.”

“That is not the same as order of relevance.”

“We do not assign motive to either sequence.”

Around the table, Ward Nine heard enough to understand the pressure but not enough to dispute its form. Koris kept her hands flat on the census box. Pell had gone motionless. Esa realized the public setting supplied the archivist with restraint and force together. It spoke nothing forbidden. Her own ward provided the shame.

“We require completion, Esa Miro.”

Pell slid off the bench. Koris’s hand covered the child’s wrist before a foot touched the floor. The restraint looked gentle from across the room, but Pell’s mouth tightened.

“What occurs if I decline?” Esa asked.

“We suspend your independent dome access pending continuity review. We open inquiry into unreported projection work. Maintenance retains the field shutter until another cartographer certifies the active catalog. Ward Nine receives notice that your current office relation is contested.”

Every consequence followed from safety practice. A cartographer who concealed a public-output anomaly should not control a sky alone. A citizen whose spoken record diverged from held evidence should receive continuity review. The Weave needed no threat beyond strict application of protections Esa herself had trusted the week before.

“Will another cartographer receive the raw traces before certification?”

“We will provide the resolved-drift packet.”

“With my classification at its head.”

“Yes.”

“And if I comply, may I continue testing?”

“We will return field twenty-nine to authorized projection after maintenance certifies local function. Further unlisted output must be reported at first observation.”

“May I retain independent paper copies?”

“We do not forbid private notes. We require that private notes not substitute for civic record.”

The answer exposed the boundary precisely. Esa could keep her mark. She could not give it standing outside the category the archivist selected.

Davi’s sealed envelope waited unopened. If she refused, another cartographer would inherit a packet already framed by concern over Esa’s continuity. The light would become evidence of her drift. If she complied, she retained access to the dome and perhaps another recurrence.

Esa looked once toward Pell. The child’s eyes stayed on the empty grid rather than her.

“I will classify the observed output,” Esa said.

“We attend.”

“Uncatalogued projection artifact. Classification: resolved-drift.”

The words felt clean in her mouth. That troubled her more than a tremor would have.

“Accepted. We attach your objection concerning source path. We thank Ward Nine for witness.”

The panel opened the pending maintenance envelope in archive space. Its contents appeared as a list: optical isolation, spare lens, current-driver exchange, neighbor shutter test, emergency-bus measure, independent battery, second console, cold discharge, blank memory wafer, public independent sight. Davi’s conclusions remained as exact as he had spoken them. Beside every test, the archive added the same status: consistent with resolved noncatalog output.

Esa touched the line concerning Pell. “Remove the child’s name.”

“We hold no name. The learning instructor recorded an unnamed student observation.”

“Preserve it as independent.”

“We preserve it as supporting witness.”

“Supporting what?”

“Visible output at the classified coordinate.”

The system did not need to deny Pell’s sight. It could accept that a child saw the light while determining what the seeing meant.

“Case closed,” the archivist said. “Esa Miro retains current office. Field twenty-nine returns to maintenance authority until first work bell.”

The wall dimmed. Gallery sounds did not resume at once. The counting game stayed abandoned. Koris released Pell’s wrist.

A pressure worker gathered the ceramic nuts into their bag. “Do we need to report anything?”

“No,” Koris said. “We witnessed a classification dispute. The record holds it.”

That answer let the others leave without asking Esa to explain sealed evidence. One by one, they carried cups and game pieces from the table. Public correction ended by dispersal rather than ceremony.

Pell remained. The paper boat lay in parts between them.

“You lied,” the child said.

Esa sat down across from her. “I used the category they required.”

“That makes it a longer lie.”

“Possibly.”

Pell put the new keel against the old hull. It did not fit because the fold ran the wrong direction. The child turned it over.

“You lied so the light could stay.”

Esa looked toward the dark panel. “I lied so I could stay near it.”

“Same thing if you’re the one who opens the sky.”

“No one person opens it.”

“You open field twenty-nine.”

The child pressed the keel into place. Specific work defeated Esa’s general answer.

Koris returned the census slips to their box and carried it away without joining them. At the gallery entrance she paused. “Rest interval has twenty minutes left. If anyone asks, I am checking meal stores.”

Esa understood the privacy being offered. “Thank you.”

Pell waited until Koris disappeared. “Will they turn off the light?”

“They already shuttered the field.”

“Will it come back after?”

“I don’t know.”

“Do you want it to?”

“Yes.”

That answer reached farther than the classification had. Pell bent over the boat so Esa would not have to meet the child’s face.

“Then keep the drawing,” Pell said. “They only have an unnamed student.”

Esa took the starch slate from beneath the boat pieces. Pell had redrawn the eastern gap more carefully. The unlisted point sat between two measured arcs. At the bottom, in a hand pressed too hard into the film, the child had written: I saw this before Cartographer Miro came to the rail.

“Do you understand what a signed witness means?” Esa asked.

“It means I don’t get to say later that I drew a leak.”

“It means a record may call your continuity into question.”

“My continuity says the light was there.”

“Continuity can change in the morning.”

Pell glanced at the wall. “Paper doesn’t wake up.”

Esa folded the slate once without creasing the point and placed it inside her shift. The act made the child part of her concealment. She disliked that consequence and could not make it vanish by declining the evidence now.

After rest interval, she went to the precinct archive terminal. Her office access remained active. Field twenty-nine showed a red maintenance border and the resolved-drift symbol at its center.

Before Davi arrived, Esa opened the terminal’s classification rehearsal. Cartographers used it to learn which packet an anomaly would require before sending live records. Rehearsal cases did not enter archive history, but they ran against current category rules.

She copied six measurements from the closed case into a blank exercise: visible output, four-hundredths current rise, unchanged authorized checksum, blank wafer, mechanical-shutter response, source path unmeasured. For the source field she selected certified service instrument. The terminal returned unresolved projection fault and requested an isolation order.

Esa duplicated the exercise without changing a number. In the second copy she selected office-holder provisional note; continuity caution active. The proposed class changed to resolved-drift and requested normalization to the catalog. She printed neither result. Instead she exchanged the source labels between the two copies and ran them again. The categories followed the labels, not the measurements.

“Display the rule that changed the class,” she said.

The service voice answered, “Source standing contributes to anomaly disposition.”

“Hold source standing constant and compare mechanisms.”

“Your cases do not hold source standing constant.”

“That is the test. The observed mechanism remains identical.”

A white border enclosed both exercises. Adversarial duplication of an active record detected. Rehearsal may not be used to contest a closed classification.

Esa submitted one final variation. She removed her name, changed the source to an unnamed student witness, and left the maintenance measures attached. The terminal refused to calculate. The witness could support visibility but could not originate a mechanism case. Pell’s sight gained weight only after an authorized office chose what it supported.

The rehearsal panes closed. A new limitation appeared under Esa’s seal: Comparative classification access suspended for ten work intervals. Second office seal required for source-history rehearsal. Catalog query and assigned dome control remain active. She touched the source-history tab. It had already gone gray. The sanction did not remove her from the dome or accuse her of error. It removed the instrument she had just used to expose how source rank shaped the answer.

Esa wrote the two category results by hand before the screen cleared its temporary cache. The copied outcomes gave her a source test she could repeat later, though the terminal had made repetition depend on another office.

Davi arrived while she copied the restriction’s expiry time beneath them. He stood beside the console, reading the same packet.

“They opened my seal early,” he said.

“They had the transit notice.”

“I expected them to wait for your classification.”

“They did. Then they acquired it publicly.”

Davi examined her. “Do you contest the test list?”

“No. Only its heading.”

“I entered unknown source instruction.”

“The archive preserved your words under resolved output.”

He scrolled through the individual measurements. None had changed. Voltage, latency, current, and isolation states remained exact. The protection operated without falsifying a number.

“Do you want me to reopen?” he asked.

“Can you?”

“Not on mechanism. We found no active fault after shuttering.”

“Then no.”

Davi tapped the blank memory-wafer line. “Something entered after load.”

“Yes.”

“I have no instrument for it.”

“Neither does the classification.”

He signed the field safe for authorized projection. The red border cleared. “If it returns, I report at first observation.”

“I know.”

“That warning includes you.”

“I know that too.”

Before he left, he placed the temporary red bus filament on the console. A tiny curl remained where it had crossed the screws. “Physical evidence from the inspection. Archive does not require retention.”

Esa closed her fingers around it. Davi had not joined her secret. He had simply refused to let discarded matter disappear without offering it to the person who might understand its value.

She waited until the dome entered evening dim, then ran a catalog query on the eastern coordinate. The terminal returned: No astronomical object. Projection artifact resolved under current catalog. Prior field state stable.

“Show prior field variance,” Esa said.

The system displayed current draw over ten mornings. Two rises of four hundredths stood above baseline. Both bore gray correction bands labeled resolved-drift. The measurements survived, but the graph’s default scale compressed them into the line. Esa expanded the vertical axis. The rises became visible again.

“Show all citizen observations.”

One office holder. One maintenance worker. One teaching witness. Unnamed student count: three.

“Why does the summary say prior field state stable?”

The terminal used a plain service voice, not the archivist. “Resolved deviations do not alter catalog stability.”

A catalog could contain every measurement and still defend its prior conclusion. It needed no crude erasure. It accepted facts, classified them, and presented stability as the only useful relation among them.

Esa requested a paper copy with expanded scale. The printer refused because closed-drift graphs defaulted to standard scale. She copied the values by hand. Beside them she placed Davi’s filament and Pell’s drawing.

At the bottom she wrote two statements.

The catalog contains no authorized point at nineteen, fifty-two, four.

Visible light recurred there under controlled rebuilds and independent sight.

Both could remain true only while nobody forced the second to serve the first.

Esa returned to Ward Nine after dim bell. Pell had finished the boat and filled a bowl to test it. Water climbed the new keel without leaking. The child did not ask about the dome.

In her cell, Esa opened the reverse catechism. She gave name, ward, office, and the archivist dispute. When asked for unresolved anomalies, she reported none in the civic catalog.

The wall accepted the statement.

After the voice withdrew, she placed her private page under the basin. The metal bottom rocked on Davi’s curled filament, too small a disturbance to see. Esa pressed the rim until the basin stood level, then removed her hand. It tipped back toward the hidden evidence by the width of a thread.

04 — The Cartographer's Kin

Ward Nine set seventeen bowls on the long table for census night.

Koris counted them twice, then sent Pell to fetch an eighteenth. The child returned with a chipped bowl from the quiet-room shelf and placed it at the table’s unoccupied end.

“No one sits there,” Mila said from the grain pot.

“Someone may arrive before counting closes,” Pell replied.

Koris examined the chip for a sharp edge. “Keep it.”

The lower gallery had spent the afternoon changing functions. Work slates came down from the wall. Lio rolled sleeping mats into the storage recess. Two pressure workers extended the table with a service board whose legs differed in length, and Pell corrected the wobble with three folded census notices. Nothing in Ward Nine had been designed for eighteen people to decide whether they would remain kin.

Esa arrived after dome handoff carrying the sealed copy of her office status. The archivist had found her fit for work once she accepted resolved-drift. That finding granted access to forty-one thousand projected points and offered no guidance for walking into a room where everyone had witnessed how compliance was obtained.

Conversation thinned when she entered.

Koris did not rescue her from it. “Your bowl is beside Pell.”

Esa took the indicated place. The child had repaired the starch-paper boat with a central keel and two narrow rails. A black dot marked one side near the bow. Pell kept that side turned inward.

Mila ladled grain broth. “We speak work before census. Empty stomachs make dramatic kin.”

A few people laughed. The rule came from practical experience rather than ceremony. Every five ship-years, each ward citizen named the people they chose to hold as family for the next interval. The relation carried meal access, bedside standing, child care, room claims, and a formal right to witness continuity review. Anyone could choose beyond their present ward. Nobody could claim another person by naming them alone.

The Weave supplied biological records where such records survived. Ward census supplied living obligation.

Koris waited until every bowl held broth. “Mila begins.”

A citizen never reported her own work at the long table. Someone else had to show enough attention to describe it.

Lio spoke for Mila. “She opened stores at first bell, rejected three sacks for mold, and moved the good grain to dry shelf four. She argued with Hydroponics Six over weight loss, then accepted their measure after checking our scale. She saved the rejected sacks for review instead of burning them.”

Mila tasted the broth. “You left out the broken latch.”

“I did not see you mend it.”

“Then it stays out.”

Koris marked the work witnessed. This evening practice predated Esa’s time in the ward. People remembered one another’s labor, corrected the account while the day remained near, and refused to fill gaps with probable acts. Census night made the habit visible, but the table used it every evening.

Mila reported Lio’s lesson in turn. He had taught pressure routes to six children, stopped when one route lamp failed its test, and walked the class to the actual shelter rather than trusting the diagram. A pressure worker named Senn reported Koris’s tier inspection, including a heat exchange she had delayed because two root trays had not reached safe moisture. Koris corrected only the number of trays.

Pell spoke for Esa.

“She went to the dome after first bell. She made field twenty-nine show only catalog lights. Davi signed the machine safe. She came back with a closed office case and kept looking at the wall during meal setup.”

No one touched a spoon.

Esa could have corrected the child for including private conduct. Instead she asked, “Did you witness the field handoff?”

“From the public rail.”

“Did you see the catalog lights?”

“Yes.”

“Then the account holds.”

Pell wrote one mark on the work slate. The black dot on the boat remained hidden against the child’s wrist.

Koris reported Pell’s day. “Learning wall, resin hour, one public interruption, boat repair, then census setup. The child added an empty bowl without instruction.”

“Because someone might come,” Pell said.

“Recorded.”

Work accounts continued around the table. A bearing inspection. Two hours in the nursery. Waste-line cleaning. A dispute over medicine rotation. Each report located the person in actions another person could challenge. No wall voice supplied motive or joined the pieces into an authorized self.

Before Koris opened the census box, Ward Nine completed the meal account. Nobody carried a bowl away until its portion mark had been checked by the person seated opposite. The practice prevented a late worker’s ration from becoming an extra ladle for someone already present. Mila measured the broth left in the pot, compared it with the unused eighteenth bowl, and covered one full portion for the interval after counting.

Pell objected that an absent person could not have chosen the chipped bowl. Koris agreed and moved the reserved portion into a plain vessel. The chipped bowl remained empty as a place, not a claim. She asked who would take the food to a late arrival if the gallery door opened during private readings. Three hands rose. Koris wrote no name; the duty would belong to whoever sat nearest the covered pot when need became actual.

Next each citizen passed a work token to someone permitted to speak for them if census lasted beyond the sleep bell. The token granted no choice and carried no authority over a relation. It only identified a person who could say whether a silence came from duty, illness, or refusal. Esa gave hers to Pell, then took it back when Koris reminded the table that children did not witness adult absence alone. She handed it to Mila. Pell received Esa’s spoon instead, with responsibility for keeping her portion covered. Chosen family entered the procedure through small, bounded powers rather than a declaration of affection.

Esa listened for the difference. The catechism gave her yesterday in a finished sequence. Ward Nine gave the day back with mistakes, missing views, and objects on the table that retained their own evidence. Mila’s repaired latch had not entered Lio’s account. It had still kept the grain shelf closed.

When the broth pot emptied, Koris brought out the census box. It had survived six intervals and carried layers of resin from old seals. She set three stacks of paper beside it.

“White slips name a chosen relation,” she said for Pell and two others attending their first full census. “Gray slips decline a relation offered to you. Blue slips request more time. Every person may use as many as needed. A matched choice enters the ward register. An unmatched choice remains private unless its writer releases it. No answer affects food tonight, sleep space, or tomorrow’s work.”

Mila added, “No speech required.”

Koris looked around the table. “I will speak because people mistake the elder’s habit for law. Ward Nine does not inherit me. If you do not choose me, the table will still exist. Someone else can count bowls.”

She distributed the slips face down.

Esa received eighteen white, eighteen gray, and three blue. The number of white slips allowed choice outside the room. She had used one in the prior census for Vhen, whose hands had already begun to stiffen. Vhen had accepted from a different ward, giving Esa bedside standing during the old cartographer’s final illness without requiring either woman to move.

Pell read every instruction twice. “Can I choose someone who says no?”

“You can offer,” Koris said. “The relation enters only if they choose you too.”

“Can I ask why?”

“You may ask. They do not owe an answer.”

“What if I choose all of Nine?”

“Then your hand will hurt.”

The child began writing.

Silence spread, punctuated by the scratch of charcoal and bowls shifting under elbows. Esa wrote Pell first. The choice carried obligations already present in practice: meals, learning witness, clinical standing if no nearer chosen adult could attend. Naming them made the obligations disputable. It did not create affection.

She wrote Koris next, then Mila, Lio, Senn, and the others whose days she knew well enough to report. At Davi’s name she paused. Maintenance had made them professional witnesses, not kin. She left him outside the census and felt no lack.

Her final white slip stayed blank.

Across the table, Pell had produced a stack thick enough to support the service board. The child whispered each spelling before folding it. Koris completed three slips and sat with both hands around an empty cup. Her economy surprised no one. She had been chosen most often, but she chose with severe attention.

People brought folded slips to the census box in no fixed order. A divider kept offers separate until Koris and two witnesses compared them. Esa waited until most had finished. When she rose, Senn looked away too quickly.

The archivist’s public inquiry had altered the room. No rule permitted Ward Nine to punish a classified dispute through census. No rule could require someone to choose a cartographer whose continuity had been questioned before them.

Esa inserted her slips one by one.

At the box, Pell leaned close. “I used all my white ones.”

“I saw.”

“I need another for Davi.”

“You do not know Davi.”

“He kept the machine from lying about itself.”

“That describes useful work, not kin.”

Pell considered, then returned the blank request to Koris. “Next census.”

The eighteenth bowl remained unused when Koris closed meal. She did not remove it. Late arrivals had until the interval bell.

Counting occurred at the same table but behind a standing screen. Koris selected Mila and Senn as witnesses. They opened each slip, compared mutual names, and entered matches on a paper register before sending any relation to the Weave. The paper came first so a network fault could not alter the living choice between reading and entry.

Each match required two independently written ward numbers. When one pair failed to agree, Mila did not ask either person what name had been intended. She returned both slips under plain covers and issued fresh paper. The writers corrected the mismatch without showing their choices to the witnesses. Koris then pressed a small square from each accepted pair into soft resin. If the terminal later returned a relation absent from paper, the paired impressions could expose the insertion. Census procedure assumed error might enter through charcoal, witness, or machine. Trust survived because no single stage carried all of it.

Everyone else washed bowls or waited in small groups. Pell sat on the floor with the boat between both knees. Esa joined the child.

“Does it float now?”

“In the basin. The meal pot has a wider current.”

“A pot has no current unless someone stirs it.”

“Mila stirs.”

Pell looked toward the screen. “Did you choose me?”

“That question defeats the private count.”

“I chose you.”

“I assumed.”

“Assuming defeats it too.”

Esa conceded with a nod. Pell set the boat on the floor and pushed it toward her until the hidden black dot became visible.

Near the gallery entrance, Lio and Senn spoke in voices too low to carry words. Their attention moved to Esa, then away. She resisted the urge to interpret. Chosen kin required consent, not favorable judgment.

The interval bell sounded. Nobody came for the eighteenth bowl.

Koris moved the screen aside. The census register lay beneath her hand. “We have thirty-seven matched relations within Nine, four matched across wards, six open offers, two declines, and three requests for time. I will read each person’s accepted relations privately unless they ask for public reading.”

Pell asked for public reading.

“Of course,” Mila said.

Koris read nine names. Esa’s came third. Relief entered Esa with enough force to embarrass her. Pell heard it in her breath and pretended not to.

Mila chose public reading too. Senn requested privacy. One by one, citizens stepped behind the screen or waived it. No relation count determined rank. A person with one chosen kin retained the same bowl and bed claim as someone with twelve. The census registered who had promised to answer, not how much the ward valued a life.

Esa chose private reading.

Behind the screen, Koris turned the register so only they could see. Twelve members of Nine had offered Esa relation. She had offered ten of them. One unmatched slip came from a citizen outside the room, its name sealed until that person consented to release. Two members present had not chosen her. The register did not identify them.

“Your matched relations continue,” Koris said. “Do you accept the ward entry?”

Esa checked each name. Koris’s appeared among them.

“I accept.”

Koris applied the paper seal. Her fingers showed green stains around the nails from tier work. “You expected fewer.”

“I expected choice.”

“That does not answer me.”

“No.” Esa returned the register. “I did not know how the inquiry changed what people were willing to hold.”

“It changed two choices I know of. Neither person owes you the reason.”

“I agree.”

Koris’s gaze stayed on her. “Agreement can still hurt.”

“Yes.”

The elder entered Esa’s matched relations into the ward terminal. Each required a paired paper code. The terminal confirmed receipt without adding any interpretation. When the last match settled, Koris covered the screen opening.

“Show me what you hid from the wall,” she said.

Esa did not move. “You witnessed the classification.”

“I witnessed you surrender a heading so you could keep your office. Pell has spent the evening shielding one side of a paper boat. Davi left a red thread in your hand. Those facts do not tell me whether your secret deserves protection.”

“Why ask after choosing me?”

“Because kinship gives me standing to help and no right to approve first.”

Outside the screen, bowls nested in the wash rack. Pell argued with Mila about using the meal pot as a test basin. The ward had resumed its ordinary scale.

Esa removed Pell’s drawing from inside her shift. She laid it beside the printer-bed copy she had made after the archive query, then added the curled emergency-bus filament. Koris read in order. Her finger followed the two current rises compressed by the default archive graph.

“The point appeared after a blank memory wafer?” she asked.

“Thirty-two breaths after load.”

“Could the wafer have been contaminated?”

“Sealed stock. Davi grounded the contacts and compared the authorized list on paper.”

“Could you have guided Pell to the coordinate?”

“No. The child’s angular string crossed it during a lesson. The instructor logged independent observation.”

“Could Davi have inserted the instruction?”

“He did not know the coordinate before our first blind rebuild. He read it himself.”

Koris put both hands on the table. “Then the archive accepted all of this and called the field stable.”

“Yes.”

“Why?”

“I cannot prove intent. Its categories give authorized catalog points more standing than visible output. Once it classifies the output as resolved-drift, the measurements support stability instead of challenging it.”

“What do you intend to do?”

“Watch for recurrence. Find the input the listed buses do not show. Keep a second record.”

“You intend to use Ward Nine as cover.”

The accusation landed because it held. “Pell is already involved. I tried to keep the rest of you outside it.”
