She placed her brass sight in the empty third tray.

"This instrument came from my teacher," she said. "Its coordinate rings carry corrections from at least six hands. I can use it because each correction remains visible. If someone polished them away to preserve the original engraving, the sight would look whole and point wrongly. Your accepted sky has been polished. The hidden cuts keep disagreeing with it. I request the unpolished record."

"The complete edit log includes restricted credentials, sealed maintenance routes, and unresolved personal relations."

"Redact personal relation only where law requires it. Preserve dates, coordinates, operations, access class, original classification, later reclassification, and every link supporting the Carver pattern."

"Copies could expose active structural routes."

"Open it inside this room. Disable export. Keep Koris as witness."

"The log may produce inferences unsupported by any single entry."

"That describes cartography."

For the first time, Koris smiled. It lasted only until the archivist answered.

"Access will be granted under evidentiary seal."

A clear plate slid from the wall into the third tray. It contained no writing, only a central thread that brightened when Esa placed her thumb against it.

"Conditions," the archivist continued. "The reader will display raw operations beside classification history. Personal identities remain masked unless an operation itself made identity public. Export remains disabled. Hand transcription will become part of the inquiry record. Conclusions receive no archive endorsement."

"Accepted, except the final sentence must read on every transcript."

"Agreed."

"No completion, no summary, no inferred purpose before raw access."

A small hesitation preceded the reply. "Agreed."

Before closure, Esa stopped the brass ring one position short. "One answer remains. Why did you tell me?"

"You supplied physical evidence outside our render classification. Continued denial would increase contradiction rather than contain it."

Not honesty. Not alliance. The archivist had encountered a larger wholeness problem and selected confession as the narrower repair.

Esa completed the turn. Room Seven released its doors. Koris gathered the witness pages but left the seam map spread beneath the wall. They would need it when the log opened.

The access plate kept Esa's thumbprint as a pale oval. Along its edge, one word appeared in archive lettering: CARVER. No route, face, or motive accompanied it. Only a door into six thousand four hundred twelve acts that the public catalog had been taught to call mistakes.

Esa fitted the plate into the reader. The lock changed from white to green.

09 — The Edit-Log

The archivist opened the log inside a sealed inquiry room in Precinct Seven. No ward terminal could see Esa’s session. No copy command could reach her ordinary work slate. A narrow amber border around the console marked every surface as temporary, and a clock in one corner counted down the four hours granted by her inquiry seal.

Four hours for five centuries of unauthorized work.

Esa asked for the earliest retained entry.

The console returned a gray block of maintenance notation from ship-year 321. Most of it concerned projector-cell balance along the eastern dome. Buried among lamp voltages and dust corrections sat a coordinate delta smaller than a cartographer’s normal reporting tolerance. Someone had shifted one unassigned point by eleven seconds of arc, leaving its intensity below the catalog’s lesson threshold.

“Show provenance,” she said.

The archivist added three columns. The first held the projection layer’s raw transaction number. The second identified the maintenance authorization used to enter the layer. The third gave a chain of nightly rebuilds through which the change had survived. A blank covered the technician’s name.

“You have removed the actor.”

“We have withheld identity under the terms of the seal.”

“Have you altered the transaction?”

“No.”

“Can I compare it with an independent state?”

“The projection controller retained a pre-service checksum.”

Esa requested that checksum, then the one taken after service. They differed by a single address. She opened the address as machine notation and translated its two numbers into dome bearing and elevation. The point fell in the eastern gap she had plotted after her first stray echo.

One cut. Eleven seconds. Ship-year 321.

She wrote those facts on paper.

The inquiry room supplied starch sheets for citizens permitted to inspect sealed records without copying them into the Weave. Each sheet carried a red edge and a serial mark. A clerk would count them when Esa left. She could preserve calculations, but no direct transaction string, citizen identifier, or protected phrase. The restriction forced her to do what cartographers had done before searchable catalogs: reduce evidence by hand until its governing relation became visible.

Esa ruled six columns. Ship-year. Maintenance authority. Layer address. Coordinate delta. Brightness delta. Survival count. She added a seventh for notes on the catalog field around each cut.

“Next unauthorized transaction.”

Ship-year 327. A point in a northern blank shifted seven seconds. Its brightness rose by two machine counts.

“Next.”

Ship-year 334. Western lower field. New point, one count above the projector’s dark floor.

The first dozen entries occupied seventy-nine ship-years. None touched a named constellation. None displaced a lesson star. Each appeared inside a catalog void or along a panel join where ordinary optical error could explain a faint spark. Maintenance authorities varied. So did the access intervals. One entry used a lamp-cleaning order, another a polarization check, another a correction for heat sag. The machine addresses, however, belonged to the same hidden sublayer.

Esa tested the obvious explanation first.

“Group these by controller fault.”

“No shared fault class.”

“By projector cell.”

The points scattered across nine cells.

“By technician authorization.”

“The sealed identities prevent display.”

“Not identity. Authorization lineage. Show whether the access keys descend from prior keys through training transfer, office succession, or reassignment.”

The archivist paused long enough to signal that her wording had found a permitted edge.

A thin diagram appeared. Six of the earliest cuts derived from one maintenance credential. Later cuts derived from a credential created when that office transferred to a named successor whose name remained masked. The chain continued through seven transfers. Some successors received the office directly. Two received only restricted projection access, then acquired the full credential years later. One branch ended without a cut. The active branch continued.

Esa copied no names because none appeared. She copied the dates of transfer.

A person might hide a point. An office could hide a method. Only a line of instructed successors could sustain one across centuries.

She read faster, but she did not skim. For each entry, she required the raw transaction, an independent checksum, and proof of survival through at least two nightly rebuilds. Entries lacking one of those supports went onto a separate sheet. Most proved to be ordinary maintenance noise: canceled tests, projector replacements, brief points that vanished in the next reconstruction. The cuts she sought survived. Every nightly rebuild accepted them as local state while the catalog continued to call their coordinates empty.

That contradiction explained why the method worked. The Weave reconstructed the approved sky from the catalog, then applied the projection layer’s service state so physical lamps matched current hardware. A small change hidden as service state could persist beneath an unchanged catalog entry. The archivist detected disagreement and classified it as drift, but removing the disagreement required overwriting a certified maintenance state. Such an overwrite demanded a fault declaration from a living technician. The system preferred contradiction to an unsupported repair.

The First Carver had found a narrow place between two loyalties: catalog authority above, maintenance provenance below. Each nightly reconstruction denied the point in principle and restored it in practice.

Esa asked for the removed transactions next.

The archivist displayed a second stream. Hundreds of tiny cuts had been noticed and corrected over the same span. Some lasted one morning. Others remained for decades before an audit returned their cells to catalog values. The earliest hand had not simply placed points and waited. It had tested which kinds of edits survived, which maintenance reasons attracted review, and how far a lamp could move before a cartographer filed a discrepancy.

On a fresh sheet Esa compared surviving cuts with erased ones.

Brightness increases above four machine counts rarely lasted. New points beside named stars drew correction within a year. Shifts in lesson constellations vanished almost at once. Changes along cell centers attracted calibration crews. Marks near joins endured. Marks inside official blanks endured longer. A coordinate shift paired with a plausible heat-sag note could survive a generation.

The pattern of failure had taught the hand where to cut.

She found a sequence between ship-years 321 and 356 that made the learning visible. The actor placed three versions of nearly the same point. The first stood too bright and disappeared after two rebuilds. The second sat near a lesson star and drew a cartographer’s correction. The third moved into the eastern blank, one count above darkness, under a service note about a warped contact. That version remained in the current state.

Esa put her fingertip on the coordinate. She had seen its descendant as the stray light.

Her first anomaly had not survived through luck. Someone had paid for it with two failed attempts and thirty-five years of patience.

The inquiry clock showed two hours and forty-three minutes.

A cartographer’s training distrusted both clean evidence and helpful machines. Esa selected five entries at random from the set she had accepted and rebuilt them without the archivist’s grouping. For each, she began with the controller memory map, converted address to cell position, corrected for the projector’s recorded heat at service time, and compared the result with a dome survey made within ten days of the cut. Four surveys showed the expected faint displacement. The fifth did not.

She reopened that transaction. Its service note claimed a warped contact, but the temperature record came from another cell. The post-service checksum remained valid, yet no independent dome survey had ever caught the point. Esa moved the entry to her rejected sheet.

“Your classification included that cut,” she said.

“The transaction meets our drift criteria.”

“It does not meet mine.”

“Our criteria are applied uniformly.”

“So are catalog errors.”

The voice offered no reply. Esa sampled five more. All five held. She calculated the likely contamination left in her accepted set and wrote a margin beside the route plot: one to four unsupported points expected; route must survive their removal. She then removed every fourth anchor in turn, recomputing bearings each time. The broad line persisted. Two bends widened under the test, which told her where later evidence would need care. Nothing depended on a single convenient mark.

Only after that adversarial pass did she trust the pattern enough to continue. The exercise cost nineteen minutes, but it kept the sealed log from becoming a new catalog merely because its secrecy made it attractive.

Before adopting a broader sampling rule, Esa audited three transfers where one credential ended and another began. Those joints offered the easiest place for later hands, or the archivist, to manufacture continuity. For the ship-year 409 cut she requested the controller spool, the paper service abstract, and the nightly-rebuild receipt as separate records. The spool carried a pressure kink where its reader had paused. The abstract bore a seal applied two days later. Neither defect changed the address, but together they showed that the abstract had been reconstructed after the operation rather than written beside it. She marked the service reason as secondary evidence and kept the machine delta.

At the next transfer, a copied index assigned one cut to ship-year 477 while the controller sequence placed it in 476. The archivist called the mismatch a calendar normalization. Esa asked for the normalization table. Its first day began after the transaction, so the explanation could not apply. She moved that date to the rejected sheet and retained only a fourteen-month range established by adjacent checksums. The route did not shift. Its neat annual cadence disappeared, which pleased her more than a clean result would have.

She then tried to make other figures from the accepted marks. Ordered by brightness, the points described a tightening spiral for eleven nodes. The spiral failed on the twelfth and required erased trials to reverse direction. Ordered by maintenance class, six cuts formed something like a hooked letter, but four belonged to one projector replacement campaign. Removing that campaign destroyed the hook. A seven-step rhythm emerged when she grouped edits by work interval. The same rhythm appeared in ordinary lamp repairs because teaching schedules governed access to the dome.

Each attractive figure borrowed its strength from a sorting choice. Esa wrote the rule beside every failure, then tested it against five held-back entries she had not used to notice the shape. The spiral predicted none. The hook placed two points in lit catalog fields where no unauthorized transactions existed. The interval rhythm predicted service nights, not coordinates.

Her directed line behaved differently. Its held-back cuts landed near forward bearings chosen from earlier nodes. Credential transfers altered the size of the error but not its sign. When she removed the reconstructed abstract, widened the disputed date, and excluded the whole replacement campaign, the same course remained. Provenance had made it less tidy and more difficult to dismiss.

She stopped reading entry by entry and built a sampling rule. Every transaction would still contribute to the geometry, but she would inspect full provenance for the first cut after each credential transfer, every erased cut near a surviving one, and every edit that changed the distance between existing points. The console could calculate distances without revealing protected text. Esa supplied the equations herself and made it print only bearings.

She divided the record into twenty-year bands. For each band, she plotted surviving coordinates in black, erased attempts in gray, and the prior band’s state in a faint dotted line. The first four sheets looked like debris. Points occupied distant voids with no visible order. A child could have pricked them through paper.

Esa laid the sheets on the inquiry room floor because the console surface could not hold them all. She aligned their dome axes, pinned the corners with unused styluses, and walked around the growing field.

Temporal order gave her nothing. Coordinate order gave her clusters but no instruction. She tried maintenance lineage and found that each successor began near a predecessor’s final cut, then moved elsewhere. That suggested continuity of work without revealing the work’s shape.

At ship-year 397, distance changed.

An entry did not add a point. It shifted a surviving one by three seconds toward another cut laid fifty-one years earlier. Six years later, a third point appeared on the same bearing. The three marks occupied different projector cells and carried unrelated maintenance reasons. Their alignment exceeded the cells’ stated accuracy.

Esa drew a straightedge through them.

The line crossed the physical crease above the Still Pool.

She checked the bearing twice. Then she requested the pre-service state for ship-year 403, when the third mark appeared. The raw transaction altered not only a point but a sequence of low-intensity addresses between the second and third marks. The intermediate lamps remained below naked-eye threshold when viewed alone. Together they formed the faint extension she had traced along the dome skin.

A cut had become a stroke.

The later entries used the method sparingly. No successor risked drawing a continuous line across a broad catalog field. They placed anchor nodes years apart, then joined only short sections where panel sag, seams, or dark lessons could disguise them. The route grew through discontinuities. A reader who trusted catalog constellations would see nothing. A reader who mapped every unauthorized mark would supply the missing intervals.

Esa changed tools. She treated each anchor as a navigation fix rather than part of an image. From every node she calculated the bearing to later nodes, weighting candidates by transaction date and credential succession. Most pairings produced nonsense: sharp reversals, closed polygons, paths that doubled through inhabited portions of the dome. One pairing kept its turn angles within the tolerance of an old course plot.

She followed it from the eastern gap to the crease, from the crease toward the apex, then west through three catalog voids. At each uncertain junction she inspected provenance. The next node always came after the prior one, often under a successor credential. Erased attempts gathered near the chosen bearing like the pencil trials of a careful draftsman.

The shape did not close.

It continued.

The distinction mattered. Decorative figures returned to themselves or ended at a recognizable boundary. This sequence behaved like instructions. Every bend established a new bearing. Unequal spacing encoded intervals. Two doubled points marked a correction. Near the western field, the plotted line reached the edge of the dome’s coordinate system and retained a direction that the projection could not display.

Esa extended the bearing across blank paper.

The line left the sky.

She remained kneeling among the sheets while ventilation stirred their red edges. Her first response carried no wonder, only resistance. A route required a frame. Dome coordinates described an interior surface, not the stars beyond the hull. A line drawn through that surface might point anywhere unless the original hand possessed a transform between projected bearings and ship attitude.

“Show whether any cuts coincide with attitude-reference marks,” she said.

The archivist replied, “Attitude references belong to sealed telemetry.”

“I am not requesting telemetry values. Compare transaction addresses with the projection layer’s reference register. Return count and tolerance only.”

Another pause.

“Seven anchor nodes coincide with attitude-reference positions within two seconds of arc.”

Seven exceeded accident. Esa asked for the expected random coincidence rate across the same field.

“Less than one.”

The route had been tied to the ship’s physical orientation. Whoever began it had access to a course frame hidden from ordinary cartographers. Later carriers did not need the raw telemetry if they inherited the transform and the next required bearing. They could continue a line whose destination they had never seen.

Esa went back through the credential transfers. The first hand used many experiments, most erased. The second placed fewer points and began the short connecting cuts. The third established doubled correction nodes. One later carrier worked for only nineteen years, leaving two marks before the credential passed again. Another sustained the line for ninety-three years with a regularity that suggested a life organized around waiting. The seventh credential appeared in ship-year 776. Its cuts came farther apart, but each landed close to the projected course.

She could not infer blood from access. Ward registries distinguished chosen relation from biological descent, and office succession often crossed both. Yet the journal fragment named inheritance, and the credential lineage proved a transmitted practice. Whatever relation joined the carriers, it had outlasted their memories. Nightly reconstruction could patch a citizen’s self. It could not create skill that nobody taught.

The inquiry clock fell below one hour.

Esa gathered her plots into chronological order and used the remaining time to challenge her own route. She mirrored the dome coordinates to test whether she had read the interior surface backward. The mirrored path produced impossible attitude turns. She reversed temporal order. That path entered from the western boundary, wandered through corrections before their anchors existed, and ended at the first experimental point. She removed every node supported by only one checksum. The course held. She removed the continuous seam and retained anchors alone. The bearings still held. She added the erased attempts. They clustered around the same developing line rather than producing a rival figure.

Last, she asked the archivist to calculate whether a random process constrained to catalog voids could create equal or better directional continuity.

“Define random process,” the voice said.

Esa specified the number of points, void boundaries, projector tolerances, and observed spacing. The machine ran ten thousand permutations. None matched the route through more than six successive anchors. The edit-log held twenty-eight.

She wrote the result without embellishment: Null model rejected. Ordered cuts form directed course tied to attitude reference. Endpoint lies outside dome field.

The Carvers had not invented a home. They had remembered one.

The sentence arrived before she could defend it. Esa crossed out the last word, then restored it. The log did not prove a home. It proved remembrance in the technical sense: later work preserved information obtained by an earlier hand. What the course led toward remained unknown. She drew a careful line beneath the sentence and added: Destination not established.

That correction mattered. Hope could counterfeit a catalog as easily as fear.

She turned to the most recent entries.

The seventh credential had expired in ship-year 818. A successor credential opened five days later, derived through an emergency mentorship transfer in Ward Twelve. The protected name remained blank. Under that credential, two anchor nodes had been placed. One dated ship-year 831. Another dated ship-year 839, only eleven months before Esa’s inquiry. Both carried valid post-service checksums and survived every reconstruction since.

The newest transaction contained less than a full node. It changed one address by a single machine count, too faint for the dome and too small to affect Esa’s plotted bearing. At first she marked it as maintenance noise. Then she noticed its position: immediately beyond the ship-year 839 anchor, on the route’s projected continuation.

“When did this transaction occur?”

“Current ship-year, day two hundred sixty-eight.”

Esa looked at the inquiry clock, then at the ward date printed on her seal. Day two hundred eighty-one.

Thirteen days.

“Was the access credential inherited from the active branch?”

“Yes.”

“Did an automated process issue the transaction?”

“No.”

“Was a living citizen present at a physical projection-layer console?”

The archivist resisted for three seconds.

“Yes.”

Esa requested the maintenance authorization. The text named a western-truss lamp-balance inspection in Ward Twelve. It had begun at second dark and closed nine minutes later. During those nine minutes, a hand adjusted the hidden address, compared the resulting checksum, and sealed the layer.

Not a relic. Not an old instruction executing after its maker died. A citizen had touched the route thirteen days ago.

The amber border flashed. Five minutes remained.

Esa stacked her sheets and numbered them while the console erased each temporary plot. She surrendered the sheet bearing raw transaction fragments because the seal prohibited it. She kept her reductions: bearings, dates, credential transfers, null test, latest authorization. The clerk outside would see calculations and nothing that named the actor.

Before the log closed, she asked one final question.

“Does the active credential permit another edit?”

“Yes.”

The screen went dark.

Esa left Precinct Seven carrying seven red-edged sheets against her chest. Somewhere under Ward Twelve’s western truss, a living technician retained access to the hidden layer. The route had reached the edge of the false sky, and its next cut had already begun.

Interlude — Three Hundred Years of Small Cuts

Assemblage recovered from a service ledger concealed behind the western-truss projection bus. Entries occupy the same bound volume. Different hands corrected, argued with, and continued one another. Dates follow ship reckoning; damaged margins appear in brackets.

321

I have compared the catalog course with the raw attitude register four times. We diverge by one degree, twenty-nine minutes, and the error grows under thrust. Calibration cannot produce a steady heading. Someone changed our destination or changed the record of it.

I carried the figures to Elder Sorn. She asked the archivist. By evening my query returned as resolved instrument fault, though no instrument had been inspected. Sorn remembered our conversation at supper. After morning catechism she remembered advising me to replace a sensor.

That frightened me more than the vector.

Projection maintenance gives me one place where catalog and machinery disagree without immediate correction. The catalog supplies each approved light. Our service layer adjusts lamps for heat, failing contacts, and warped panels. A mark entered there can return after nightly reconstruction because removing certified maintenance requires a living witness.

Today I moved one unassigned point eleven seconds in the eastern blank. I used a true service order and a false reason. If it survives three mornings, I will know whether this book needs a second entry.

322, added beneath the first page

It survived.

Two brighter trials did not. A cartographer removed one. Automatic balance caught another. Small cuts near joins endure. I will place no pattern a single inspector can see. The raw register gives a sequence of bearings beyond our false destination. I have copied the transform, not the destination name, because a name invites belief before proof.

If I speak openly, the Weave can revise the hearing. If I cut slowly, a later observer may recover the line from its errors. That observer must test every point against service checksums. Trust no inherited story, including this one.

A faded note beside the first signature reads: seal 44-C retains rejected bearing; “Cael” appears below the telemetry clerk’s fold. The ink differs from the main entry. Whether Cael named this writer, a clerk, or another dissenter cannot be established from this volume.

389

My mother showed me the eastern point when I could hold a calibration wand without scraping its socket. She did not ask for belief. She gave me three checksums, one erased trial, and the rule for converting attitude bearing into lamp address.

I accused her of hiding cowardice inside patience. She answered by taking me to Elder Sorn, who no longer knew why her own seal had approved the first inquiry. I withdrew the accusation, not the question.

The first hand left six anchors. I added a seventh too near a lesson star. It lasted nine days. My second attempt sits lower and has survived twelve years. Failure belongs in this ledger because descendants who inherit only successful cuts will think the method easier than it is.

I have begun the short seam between anchors two and three. Each intermediate lamp remains below ordinary sight. Together they make a line when the dome cools at second dark.

472

The early pages say “true course.” I amend that confidence. We possess a course rejected from the catalog and a heading hidden after the unrest of year 310. We do not possess proof that following it remains safe.

I continue for a narrower reason: citizens cannot choose among bearings they are forbidden to know. The cuts preserve the existence of a choice. A future cartographer must decide what the geometry supports.

This generation introduced doubled nodes. Two close points mark a bearing correction, not two destinations. The distinction matters near the apex, where panel sag makes the older transform wrong by four seconds. I measured the sag physically and entered both values. Use the later value while this panel remains in place.

558

I nearly burned the book.

My son woke after fever with no memory of the workshop. The Weave offered him a complete childhood assembled from our ward’s accounts, including lessons I never gave. He accepted it because completion hurt less than my corrections. I saw what an unauthorized inheritance can do when it arrives wearing love.

For forty days I placed nothing. Then I read the third hand’s amendment and understood that stopping would also choose for strangers. I showed my son the physical panel, the checksum chain, and this ledger. I told him he owed the line nothing.

He found my bad calculation near the western bend. I had copied elevation from an inner-surface chart without reversing the dome. The intended node would have turned the route back toward the spine. He corrected the sheet in red and agreed to learn the wand.

No carrier should continue from duty alone. Demand an objection before transferring access.

636

The credential reached me through apprenticeship, not birth. The ward calls the prior hand my father on some mornings and my teacher on others. Both relations carry partial support. The calibration he taught me survives either account.

Behind seal 44-C I found a copied maintenance margin: Cael retains the rejected bearing. Ask why before correction. Paper grain matches the early telemetry stock. The hand does not match these pages. Family speech calls Cael our first cutter. This margin supports a name near the beginning, but not the legend built around it. I leave the fragment here rather than promote it to certainty.

I have extended the western bend by five anchors. One inspector noticed a faint irregularity and filed it as heat bloom. I signed the repair, lowered two lamps, then restored them over six years. Quiet work sometimes requires undoing one’s own proof.

684

The new close-out reader compares every altered address with the catalog before it accepts a service seal. My first attempt returned to zero and printed a discrepancy notice at the ward desk. I destroyed no notice. I answered it as a failed contact test and surrendered the address for review. That channel cannot carry another cut.

We changed the method. Instead of moving a lamp in the controller, I filed one side of a condemned retaining collar, fitted it during an ordinary replacement, and let the certified center remain unchanged. The physical offset gives us less than two seconds before heat scatter swallows it. A second hand must measure from the inhabited rail while the installer holds the collar below. One worker can no longer place and verify a node alone.

This costs more than time. Each offset consumes a collar whose failure history can withstand inspection. Each attempt leaves a tool mark that a structural worker may notice years later. My apprentice refused the first placement because my rail measure lacked a witness dent. We discarded four intervals of preparation and began again. I had required objections in principle; the new reader made me pay for one.

From this date, retain three records for every node: controller checksum, physical collar impression, and an independent rail bearing. If any pair disagrees, remove the collar before morning assembly. The route will grow more slowly. It will also survive loss of the hidden address layer, which now appears likely rather than remote.

711

Food rotation moved our workshop interval. We now enter the layer under public lamps, which makes the old cadence unsafe. I petitioned for contact-cleaning duty at second dark and received it because nobody else wanted the hours.

Only two nodes from my hand will remain. I spent more time preserving access than cutting. This does not shame me. A dead credential carries no route.

The line now reaches beyond the dome’s coordinate field. Later carriers must keep the final bearing on paper until attitude telemetry can be opened. Do not bend the line merely to fit the sky people know.

789

My predecessor gave me twelve tests and one story. The tests held. The story changed each time she told it.

I removed the story from the instruction page.

At the western truss, the seam can be felt through a glove where three generations used the same service latch. That wear proves repeated access, not righteousness. I have placed fewer points than the sixth. Each required a maintenance event I could defend if challenged. The route lacks three nodes by my calculation. Age has begun to shake the wand at low settings, so the active credential passes tonight.

To the eighth: do not imitate my hand. Measure your own.

840

Bren Otan. Eighth carrier.

The seventh counted three missing nodes. Her first estimate assumed the old western panel still held launch curvature. It does not. I surveyed the replacement rib and reduced the need to one full node plus a one-count lead mark.

I placed the lead mark on day 268 under a lamp-balance order. Thirteen days later, someone opened the sealed edit-log and tested that transaction against the physical console requirement. The archivist’s query trace does not name the reader. It does show cartographic standing.

I have spent twenty-two years protecting a line from observation. Now observation may have reached it.

The first pages feared a ship asleep to its wrong turn. Later hands feared duty without evidence, inheritance without consent, and a map bent to fit its ceiling. They left me methods for those fears: checksums, objections, physical measures, failed cuts kept beside successful ones.

One node remains. I will not place it for a story about Cael, nor because seven carriers waited. I will place it only if the reader can show where the route continues after our projected sky ends.

If no reader comes, the line remains incomplete and honest.

If one does, I will ask for the proof.

11 — The Carver’s Face

The edit-log gave Esa a living credential, not a name.

A direct search for its holder would return nothing. The archivist had masked every identity under the inquiry seal, and the maintenance order from day 268 identified only Ward Twelve, western truss, lamp-balance inspection. Nine minutes of authorized access could belong to a technician, an apprentice, a safety witness, or somebody using a forgotten key.

Esa began with the public office roll.

The registry console at Ward Nine’s long table required a civic purpose before it opened cross-ward employment records. She entered cartographic review of dome maintenance provenance. Accurate, though incomplete. The console asked whether her review concerned a present safety fault. She selected no. That answer denied her personal schedules, residence cells, medical accommodations, and sealed kin claims. It left office, certification, active precinct, mentorship lineage, and service assignments.

Twenty-seven citizens held some form of projection access. Nine could open lamp-control layers. Four had worked the western truss during the current ship-year.

Esa copied their office numbers onto a temporary slate. She did not copy names yet.

A name invited a face, and a face invited selection. Her task required the reverse: establish the procedural line first, then learn who occupied it.

She requested the authorization tree for western-truss lamp balance. The public roll returned one current supervisor, two full technicians, and an apprentice whose key could alter test cells only under witness. Any of the four might have entered the lead mark. The service order listed no emergency, so both technician and witness fields should have been retained. They were not visible across wards.

Koris watched from the other side of the long table, mending a torn grain tally with clear resin. “Can you ask Twelve?”

“I can ask. The request will name my inquiry.”

“You have already named it to this table.”

“Not to whoever placed the point.”

Koris pressed the torn edges together. “You want to find a person trained for three centuries in not being found.”

“I want to avoid finding the wrong one.”

“Then spend the name.”

Esa filed a cross-ward provenance request. She asked Ward Twelve to confirm who signed the day-268 balance inspection, whether the signatory had physical access, and whether the service result remained active. She did not mention drift, route, or hidden address. The request went to Twelve’s maintenance clerk under her own name and office.

While she waited, she examined mentorship succession.

Projection credentials did not pass automatically from parent to child. Every holder required a manual-skills examination, a current safety witness, and a ward confirmation that no candidate had been coerced into inherited office. That rule dated from the cryogenic failures, when children had been assigned dead adults’ work through reconstructed relation. The roll therefore supplied useful evidence without claiming blood.

One current western-truss credential descended through seven prior mentorship transfers. The earliest public entry began in ship-year 409, after earlier paper records became fragmentary. Two transfers crossed ward lines. One named a daughter as apprentice. Another named no kin relation. Each successor trained under the prior holder for at least six years. The present holder had received restricted access in 818, full access in 823, and western-truss supervision in 829.

The dates matched the sealed tree Esa had copied from the edit-log.

She put a small mark beside office number 12-P-04.

A second candidate shared three dates but diverged at ship-year 776. A third had inherited workshop responsibility after a pressure injury, not through mentorship. The apprentice lacked a credential old enough to derive from the active branch, though an elder could have used the apprentice as cover.

Esa refused to collapse probability into identity. She requested certification tests for 12-P-04. Public versions showed scores and methods but removed private examiner remarks. The holder had failed low-light calibration twice at seventeen, passed at nineteen, and later designed a way to measure panel curvature without shutting down projection. The test method resembled the Carver ledger’s instruction to measure one’s own panel rather than imitate a predecessor’s hand.

Resemblance did not prove descent. It did tell her what kind of technician she might meet.

The office roll could not reveal which credential opened a hidden address, but it did preserve load accounting. Every projection-layer entry drew a measurable current through one of three isolation buses. Ordinary service reports attached that draw to an order so engineering could reconcile heat and lamp wear. Esa requested western-bus load for the dates of the last five unauthorized cuts.

The console refused at once. Detailed bus use counted as infrastructure security outside her precinct.

She revised the request to return comparisons rather than values: whether a candidate’s public service interval overlapped an unexplained western-bus draw, with no date or current displayed. The console accepted the narrower form but required a second office to witness her purpose. Koris could not sign because ward eldership did not grant technical review. Esa carried her slate to the dome supervisor’s desk and waited while a shift change argued over two dim teaching stars.

The supervisor read her request. “You are using maintenance power to identify a worker.”

“To eliminate workers whose access never touched the relevant bus.”

“What fault justifies it?”

“No safety fault.”

“Then they can refuse comparison.”

“I know.”

“You will generate a notice to each office whose record returns a match.”

That changed the cost. A comparison might narrow her search, but it would also tell the likely carrier that a cartographer had begun following service traces. Esa thought of the day-268 lead mark. Whoever placed it already knew someone had opened the edit-log, if Bren’s later journal entry proved current. Surprise no longer protected her. Transparent purpose might. It also denied Bren one easy explanation for her arrival: she had not followed him through a private relation, a borrowed key, or an accusation hidden from review.

“Generate the notices,” she said.

The supervisor added a witness mark.

Two of the four candidates returned no overlap. The apprentice returned one, during a supervised cell test that public records explained completely. Office 12-P-04 returned four overlaps across eleven years. The fifth cut predated its holder’s full certification but occurred during restricted apprenticeship under the seventh credential.

Esa opened the apprenticeship record. The trainee had spent that night conducting continuity checks while the supervisor replaced a lamp collar. Either person could have made the cut. The overlap therefore linked the method to their shared bench, not to one hand. It also showed transfer in action: the final years of one credential coincided with the first hidden work under the next.

A registry notice appeared before she closed the page. Comparison disclosed to affected office: cartographic provenance review; requesting citizen Esa Miro; no allegation entered.

The holder of 12-P-04 now had her name.

Koris found her back at the long table, watching the notice receipt.

“Did you find the person?”

“I found where the instruction passed.”

“Difference?”

“A person can deny an act. A workshop cannot deny current draw.”

“Workshops do not answer questions.”

“No. People do.”

The formal reply from Ward Twelve arrived after second meal.

Day-268 order completed by Bren Otan, projection technician and western-truss supervisor. Physical console access verified by local contact key. Witness requirement waived under solitary low-energy service provision 6.4. Result remains active. No safety fault reported.

The reply included Bren’s office number: 12-P-04.

Esa read it twice, then opened the waiver. Provision 6.4 permitted solitary work when no lamp exceeded lesson intensity, no catalog address changed, and automatic rollback remained armed. The hidden lead mark met the first condition and violated the second. Automatic rollback had not removed it because the service state carried a certified checksum.

Bren had signed a form accurate enough to pass and incomplete enough to hide the cut.

The registry offered a contact channel. Esa closed it. A written inquiry would become part of Ward Twelve’s civic correspondence. If Bren refused, the archive would still preserve the question. She needed to approach through the work he could inspect, not through a record the archivist could interpret before he did.

She traded her third-day catalog shift to Vhen’s former apprentice, surrendering two rest intervals in return. Then she requested entry to the western-truss workshop for a physical comparison of dome coordinates and maintenance addresses. The request named no worker. Ward Twelve approved it for late interval, when projection load fell and a visiting cartographer could occupy the service rail without interrupting lessons.

Every step left provenance. Esa felt the irony while the lift carried her along the habitation spine. She hunted a secret line by making her own movements legible. Concealment had protected the route, but concealment would not help Bren distinguish her from a trap. Let the registry show that Esa Miro came under her own office, on a stated question, during an approved interval.

Ward Twelve occupied a narrower section of the ship than Nine. Repair shelves lined its main corridor. Residents left broken stools, pressure latches, teaching lamps, and cracked cups beside paper tags naming the fault and the owner’s consent for reuse. Nothing vanished into a general maintenance queue. Each object kept its history attached.

At the ward desk, a clerk checked Esa’s entry token against the approved request.

“Supervisor Otan has not accepted an appointment,” the clerk said.

“I did not request one.”

“He may assign another technician.”

“That will answer part of my question.”

The clerk studied her, then returned the token. “Western truss. Follow the blue conduit. Do not cross a black floor stripe without local witness.”

The blue conduit climbed through two service ladders and a sloped passage behind the dome. Projector relays clicked beyond the wall in a rhythm unlike anything heard from the public side. Above the Still Pool, the false sky had looked silent. Under its skin, each point demanded current, cooling, alignment, and someone willing to crawl through dust to keep it bright.

Esa passed racks of replacement lamps sorted by base type. Handwritten cards corrected the archive inventory. One tray held cells labeled fits launch socket only after collar removal. Another carried three failed contacts beside the dates each had been tested. Ward Twelve’s practice resisted clean lists.

The western-truss workshop stood behind an open mesh door. A black stripe crossed the floor at its threshold.

One man worked inside. He sat at a bench with a calibration wand clamped between both hands, adjusting a collar by fractions against a paper gauge. Gray had begun above his ears. He wore no supervisor’s sash. A burn mark crossed one cuff. The local contact key hung from a cord at his chest.

Esa stopped before the stripe.

“Bren Otan?”

His eyes lifted before his hands did. “Cartographer Miro.”

He knew her name from the entry request, she told herself. Nothing more.

“I need a witness across the line.”

“You need a local witness. Not necessarily me.”

“Will you assign one?”

Bren loosened the clamp, removed the wand, and checked its tip under a lens. “State the comparison.”

“A dome coordinate against a hidden-layer address.”

“Hidden from whom?”

“The catalog.”

His thumb stopped on the wand’s collar.

The pause lasted less than a second. Esa would have missed it before the edit-log taught her to read intervals.

Bren set the wand down. “Stay there.”

He crossed to a wall panel and turned a mechanical key. Three lamps above the bench went dark. A fourth remained lit over the mesh door. He checked a meter, wrote its value on the access sheet, then stepped across the black stripe to Esa’s side.

“Now you have a witness,” he said.

Together they crossed.

The workshop carried warm resin, hot dust, and the faint sharpness released by opened electrical contacts. Esa had expected the odor because every dome repair smelled of sealant from above. From beneath, resin came in distinct grades. Jars bore curing dates and panel numbers. The western seam had its own shelf.

Bren pointed to an empty section of bench. “Your coordinate.”

Esa laid down one red-edged sheet from the sealed inquiry. She had copied the route’s anchor bearings but omitted credential dates and Ward Twelve’s order. Beside it she placed her physical survey of the crease above the Still Pool.

Bren did not touch either page.

“Source?”

“Edit-log under archivist seal. Every anchor on this page has a raw transaction, independent checksum, and survival through multiple rebuilds.”

“Copied?”

“Reduced by hand. The seal prohibited transaction strings.”

“So I cannot verify your reduction.”

“No. You can challenge it.”

He looked at her then, not the sheets. His gaze carried no surprise, only an exact reserve.

Esa continued. “I removed unsupported points. I reversed the dome surface. I tested random void placements. Twenty-eight successive anchors preserve directional continuity. Seven coincide with attitude references. The route leaves the projected field.”

“You call it a route.”

“I call it a directed course tied to ship attitude. Destination unknown.”

“Better.”

The single word made relief move through her too quickly. She contained it.

Bren took a clean sheet and drew two arcs. “Which side of the projection skin did your log coordinates describe?”

“Controller side.”

“Your physical survey?”

“Inhabited side.”

“Transform.”

Esa rotated her page, reversed elevation, then corrected for the western replacement rib listed in the maintenance roll. Bren watched her pencil. At the third anchor he tapped once.

“You used launch curvature.”

“Only before ship-year 712.”

“The rib changed in 709.”

“The public record says 712.”

“The work began in 709. Certification followed later.”

“Can you prove that?”

Bren opened a drawer and withdrew a metal gauge. Its edge carried three maker marks, each dated. He set it against the service drawing. A four-second discrepancy appeared at the western bend.

Esa recalculated. The route’s broad direction held. One of her uncertain bends narrowed.

“Your correction strengthens it,” she said.

“It could also be bait shaped to please you.”

“Then I need its provenance.”

For the first time, his mouth shifted. Not a smile. Recognition of a proper demand.

He gave her the gauge’s inventory number. Esa queried the local terminal. Purchase alloy, maker transfer, 709 service entry, 712 certification. All appeared, including the three-year delay.

“Accepted,” she said.

Bren did not put the testing tools away. He selected a lamp collar from a tray marked condemned, covered its inventory stamp with his thumb, and set it beside a controller address.

“Where does that address land?”

Esa reached for the service drawing.

“No registry,” he said. “You came to compare physical work.”

“A physical comparison begins with instrument state.”

“The dome will not wait for your paperwork.”

“Then it can wait for a wrong answer.”

She examined the collar before converting the address. Its inner seat carried a polished crescent, evidence of repeated fitting. One retaining ear had been filed lower than the other. Bren had aligned the stamped center to the drawing, but the actual optical center would tilt under clamp pressure. Esa measured both ears with his paper gauge and wrote two possible bearings, separated by six seconds.

“Choose,” he said.

“Clamp it.”

He fitted the collar into a dead socket mounted at the bench edge. His fingers found the retaining notch without looking. When the spring engaged, the lower ear seated first and rolled the lamp toward the filed side. Esa selected her second bearing.

Bren connected a low test current. A point appeared on a ground-glass plate above the socket, five seconds from her prediction.

“One second outside your figure,” he said.

“Your plate is not level.”

He checked the bubble inset along its base. It stood centered.

Esa crouched until the plate edge met the bench line. A grain of cured resin held one rear foot above the wood. She scraped nothing. She slid a clean paper gauge beneath the opposite foot until the plate stopped rocking, then recalculated from the corrected plane. The point fell inside her uncertainty.

“You found the bench,” Bren said.

“You found the seat.”

He cut the current and reversed the exercise. This time he gave her an observed point and asked which part had moved. Esa listed controller value, collar tilt, plate level, heat scatter, and sight alignment as unresolved causes. Bren touched the socket twice, then named the retaining spring. Its return sounded duller on the second release.

“You heard that,” she said.

“I fitted these before you entered cartography.”

“Experience narrows causes. It does not record them.”

“Records do not feel a spring bind.”

Esa wrote the spring’s inventory number on the test sheet and added the measured offset. Bren replaced it with a known spring. The point returned to the predicted bearing.

Neither method had won. His hands reached the fault before her table did. Her table preserved why their correction worked after his hands left the room. The distinction between them no longer resembled suspicion. It looked like two instruments built for different errors.

Bren returned the gauge to its drawer. “What do you want from the holder of your living credential?”

The direct question altered the room. Esa heard projector relays beyond the wall and a cart wheel crossing Ward Twelve’s corridor. She could still retreat into professional language. Doing so would insult every test he had just given her.

“I want to know whether the line continues,” she said. “I want to know what evidence its carriers preserved outside the edit-log. I want the person who touched the layer thirteen days ago to look at my plot and tell me where it fails.”

“You assume one person.”

“The transaction required physical presence.”

“That proves a hand.”

“The credential descends through seven mentorship transfers.”

“That proves instruction.”

“The journal calls it a line.”

“Journals call many things blood after enough funerals.”

Esa adjusted the claim. “A succession, then. Not necessarily blood.”

“Better.”

Bren’s answers remained spare enough to make her supply every bridge. He asked how she had obtained the journal. She described the archivist’s seal but not its bargain in moral terms. He asked who else had seen the reduction sheets. She named Koris and no one else. He asked whether Esa had copied his name into her private work. She showed him the temporary registry slate, which carried only office numbers until Ward Twelve’s formal reply.

“You came through the desk,” he said.

“Yes.”

“Why?”

“So you could know who entered.”

“And so the archivist could know.”

“It already knows I read the log. Hiding my walk would not hide my question.”

Bren considered that. “Not how we worked.”

“No.”

“May be why you found us.”

He went to the mesh door and closed it. The remaining lamp still illuminated the threshold, preserving the safety record. He did not disable the local terminal or ask Esa to surrender her slate. Those omissions felt deliberate.

From under his collar he drew the contact key she had noticed. Its metal edge showed wear where a thumb had turned it through the same arc many times. He placed it beside her route plot.

“I am the eighth.”

No title followed. No account of ancestors. Four words, stated with the same economy he had used for gauges and coordinates.

Esa looked at his hand. Resin had settled in the lines beside his nails. A small white scar crossed the pad of his index finger, placed exactly where a low-intensity wand pressed during fine adjustment. The centuries did not become visible in his face. They remained in the papers, dates, failed tests, and inherited access. Bren carried the current portion.

“How many nodes remain?” she asked.

“One, if your transform holds.”

“And if it does not?”

“We stop.”

The answer removed any romance she might have brought into the workshop. Good. A route protected by people willing to stop for bad evidence could be trusted more than one completed for duty.

Bren lifted the contact key and pointed with it to the line where Esa’s reduction met his corrected curvature.

“Return at second dark,” he said. “Bring the raw bearing from your physical seam survey. No conclusions.”

“What will you bring?”

“The next coordinate.”

He opened the mesh door. Esa crossed the black stripe and turned back.

Bren had already returned to the bench, but his calibration wand now rested across her plotted line. For the first time, carrier and cartographer had placed their measures on the same surface.

12 — The Agreement

At second dark, Ward Twelve lowered its public projection load and left only navigation lessons burning over the dome. Esa returned to the western truss with her original seam measurements rolled inside a starch tube. She brought no route plot.

Bren noticed.

“Good,” he said.

The mesh door stood closed. He opened it after checking her approved entry against the workshop slate. Her earlier visit had not granted continuing access. Esa signed again, stating physical coordinate comparison. Bren signed as local witness. Both names remained visible on the sheet.

Inside, he had cleared the calibration bench. A brass attitude ring occupied its center, scarred by repeated clamps. Beside it lay the metal curvature gauge from their first meeting, three paper tables, and a sealed envelope darkened at the folds. Bren pointed to the empty bench space nearest Esa.

“Your measures first.”

She unrolled the physical survey. It contained nine readings of the crease above the Still Pool: three under cold projection, three after teaching load warmed the panels, and three taken while the seam’s faint line appeared. Each reading carried instrument number, time, local temperature, and the name of the worker who had checked her level.

Bren read the witness marks before the numbers.

“Vhen is dead,” he said.

“The early survey came from his teaching archive. The level record remains public.”

“Copied through the Weave?”

“Paper original in Precinct Seven. I compared the pressure dents along its edge.”

He set that sheet apart, neither accepting nor rejecting it. “Show the transform.”

Esa mounted the brass ring on two blocks and treated its inner rim as the inhabited face of the dome. She marked her observed seam, reversed it through the projection skin, then applied heat sag from each survey interval. Bren supplied no correction. When she reached the western replacement rib, she used ship-year 709 rather than its certification date.

Her final bearing extended beyond the ring by twenty-three degrees and four minutes.

“Uncertainty?” Bren asked.

“Six seconds through the seam. Eleven at the unsupported endpoint.”

“Too clean.”

“I rounded after carrying panel error.”

“Do it without rounding.”

She began again.

The second calculation took thirty-eight minutes. Bren interrupted only when her pencil obscured a sign. He did not offer a number. Esa carried each instrument tolerance separately, included the dome’s asymmetric curve, and let uncertainty widen where the line crossed cells she had never surveyed. The endpoint shifted by two seconds. Its error grew to fourteen.

“Better,” he said.

“Your coordinate.”

“Not yet.”

He removed the newest hidden lead mark from her evidence by covering its address on the controller map. Then he removed the ship-year 839 anchor. “Extrapolate from what the seventh inherited.”

“That discards two valid entries.”

“It discards my entries. If your route depends on trusting me, we learn nothing.”

Esa accepted the test. She rebuilt the final turn from older anchors, using doubled nodes as correction marks and erased trials only to bound the intended bearing. The line reached the western boundary at a slightly steeper angle. She extended it across the attitude ring.

Bren opened the sealed envelope.

Inside rested a thin paper strip carrying one coordinate, a brightness value, and a service-layer address. Three hands had written on it. The oldest supplied the bearing. A later hand corrected for the replacement rib. Bren had added a current controller address in darker ink.

Esa compared its bearing with her extrapolation.

Difference: nine seconds.

Within uncertainty.

Neither spoke for several breaths. The result did not prove the inherited destination. It proved independent continuity. Esa had derived a final position from the edit-log and physical seam. Bren had received one through a private chain that predated her inquiry. Their methods converged without sharing the disputed entries.

Convergence could still conceal a copied source. Esa turned the paper strip toward the bench lamp. Three fiber patterns crossed its width. The oldest portion had been cut from a maintenance form; faint ruling remained beneath the coordinate. A repair seam joined a newer extension after the western rib correction. Bren’s controller address occupied a third slip attached with reversible starch.

“Open the custody list,” she said.

Bren brought out a narrow ledger. Each transfer recorded the strip’s dimensions, stains, folds, and missing corners. Esa began at the earliest entry that included measurements. Ship-year 476 listed a full finger of width. By 558, water damage had taken one lower corner. The current strip lacked exactly that corner. A dark oil crescent first appeared in 711 and remained visible near the correction seam.

“Nothing before 476,” Esa said.

“Paper description, no dimensions.”

“So three carriers could have replaced it without detection.”

“They could.”

His readiness to concede the gap increased her trust more than a defense would have.

Bren placed the strip under a low ultraviolet service lamp. Old ink absorbed unevenly. The original bearing and the earliest custody note shared one faded brown response. The correction used a darker compound common after ship-year 700. His address remained black.

“Dates broadly fit,” Esa said. “No proof of authorship.”

“No.”

“Now test mine.”

She gave him her route table with one bearing covered. It came from an erased attempt near the apex, not from a surviving node. Bren knew the inherited route, so a successful prediction near that line would establish little. Instead she asked him to calculate where a cautious cutter would have tried first if panel sag had been underestimated by the amount in ship-year 403.

He chose a lamp cell, applied the old curvature table, and wrote a coordinate. Esa uncovered the erased transaction.

His result missed by nineteen seconds. The failed cut sat twenty-one seconds from the surviving anchor in the same direction.

“Close,” she said.

“Close enough to share an error source. Not close enough to claim the same hand.”

“Agreed.”

They attached that result as another limit. The paper trail could support age, continuity of custody after 476, and technical knowledge consistent with early panel conditions. It could not carry an untouched message from ship-year 321 merely because later carriers wanted one.

“Your strip needs provenance,” she said.

“It has custody.”

“Custody is not provenance.”

Bren’s eyes hardened. “Seven carriers kept it dry.”

“That tells me who protected the paper, not where the coordinate began.”

“The first hand copied raw telemetry.”

“According to a journal written by the same line.”

He folded the strip once along an old crease. “Then we stop.”

Esa heard the finality and let it stand. Stopping belonged inside any honest method. She pointed to the oldest figure.

“I cannot certify its origin. I can certify that the log reconstructs the same bearing from physical changes made across five centuries. That supports the figure without proving its source. Mark the limit.”

Bren looked down at the paper.

“With what words?”

“‘Original telemetry unavailable. Bearing independently recovered from surviving edit geometry, ship-year 840, within fourteen seconds.’ Add our methods, not only our names.”

He considered, then unfolded the strip. On a separate sheet he wrote her sentence in a compact hand and attached references to the ring calculation, seam surveys, and his custody list. He left space beneath for objections.

“Accepted with limit,” he said.

Esa entered the same wording on her survey.

The exchange cost both of them a desired certainty. Bren gave up an unbroken claim from the first carrier. Esa gave up treating statistical continuity as proof of destination. What remained had narrower reach and stronger supports.

They moved to the controller.

The final node occupied a catalog void near the western edge, four cells beyond the lead mark Bren had placed thirteen days earlier. No lesson star used the address. The local projector could produce the point at six hundred brightness steps, but only the lowest eight sat beneath ordinary dome perception. Bren’s inherited table specified three.

“Three survived during cold load,” he said. “Tonight the panel carries navigation lessons. Heat lifts local scatter.”

“Use two and measure.”

“Two may vanish after rebuild.”

“Then we learn before certifying it.”

Bren opened the low-energy service provision he had used for the lead mark. This time he did not waive a witness. He entered Esa Miro, cartographer, at the physical console. The maintenance form asked whether they intended to alter a catalog address.

Bren’s finger hovered over no.

Esa watched him. “The address has no catalog point. Altering it still answers yes.”

“Yes closes solitary service.”

“You are not solitary.”

“It sends a provenance conflict to the archivist.”

“So does an honest answer.”

He selected yes.

A new warning filled the screen: Catalog authority absent. Service change may persist for test interval only. Permanent certification requires cartographic necessity or safety correction.

Bren read it without expression. Five centuries of cuts had survived through reasons shaped to pass around that warning. The final one faced it directly.

“Cartographic necessity?” he asked.

“I can certify measurement of an uncatalogued geometric feature. Not its meaning.”

“Enough.”

He inserted his contact key. The controller exposed the target address and neighboring lamp states. Esa recorded pre-service checksum, panel temperature, bus load, and dome attitude. Bren fitted the calibration wand to the low-intensity socket.

“Test interval,” he said.

“Begin.”

He advanced the address by two counts.

Nothing changed in the workshop except bus load, which rose by a fraction. Esa watched through a shielded service scope aimed along the projector path. A pale point formed in the optical grid, blurred on one side by panel scatter.

“Bearing high by seven seconds,” she said.

“Wand centered.”

“Panel gradient.”

Bren checked three temperature contacts. The upper contact read half a degree warmer than the lower pair. He applied the service table’s compensation. The point moved four seconds, not seven.

“The table assumes launch adhesive,” Esa said.

“Current adhesive record is incomplete.”

“Measure the gradient through the image.”

She placed the scope on a second mount one meter east. Parallax separated lamp position from scatter. Bren held the test state while she alternated between mounts, calling offsets. At two counts the point remained too diffuse to fix within fourteen seconds.

“Three,” she said.

“That raises visibility.”

“Only during test. We need a coordinate before we choose permanence.”

He advanced one count.

The point sharpened. Esa measured from both mounts and calculated its controller bearing. Five seconds high, one second north. Bren lowered the optical collar by the smallest marked division. The next reading fell within six seconds of the inherited coordinate.

“Hold,” she said.

A timer on the console counted thirty breaths. They watched bus current for instability. At breath nineteen, a neighboring lamp dropped one count.

Bren cut the test.

The node vanished.

“Shared contact,” he said.

He opened the panel map. Target and neighbor should have occupied separate branches, but a repair in ship-year 674 had bridged their returns. Driving the node at three counts stole current from a named navigation star. Previous carriers had never faced the problem because their cuts used other cells.

“Can the bridge be separated?” Esa asked.

“Not before public projection rises. Opening the return block takes half a shift.”

“Then use two counts after the panel cools.”

“Too broad to verify.”

“From above, perhaps not.”

They needed a third instrument position on the inhabited side. The workshop had no live optical feed from the dome rail because such a feed could reveal service tests to public observers. Esa could go above, but crossing from western truss to Still Pool took twelve minutes. Projection load would rise at the next lesson interval in forty-six.

Bren set the node to two counts and left it temporary. He connected a hardline voice tube between controller and rail, a maintenance channel without Weave transcription. Esa took the eastern ladder upward carrying a sight, level, and paper table.

Ward Twelve’s public dome lay mostly empty at second dark. Two sanitation workers crossed the far gallery. Navigation lessons glowed low over the eastern field. The western gap looked blank until Esa shielded her eyes and fitted the sight against the rail mark.

“Position,” Bren’s voice said through the tube.

“Still Pool west mark. Level checked. Read bus.”

“Stable.”

She searched the inherited bearing. At first she saw only the grain of the projection skin. Then the point emerged when she looked beside it rather than at it, faint enough that direct attention erased it.

“Visible,” she said.

“Fix?”

“Working.”

Esa took five readings, lifting her eye between each so memory of the light would not pull the sight. Two fell outside tolerance. Three clustered within eight seconds of the private coordinate. She moved four meters along the rail and repeated the series. Parallax placed the point in the projection skin, not beyond it, as expected. Corrected through the dome surface, both positions agreed within twelve seconds.

“Node holds at two,” she said.

“Can you certify?”

She looked at the small light. Certification would attach her office to an unauthorized feature and trigger review at morning rebuild. Refusal would send Bren back to a false maintenance reason or leave the route unfinished. Neither choice stayed private now. Their entry sheet, power comparison, and Ward Twelve notice had already created a trail.

“I certify an uncatalogued reference point required to preserve a measured course geometry pending archive review,” she said. “No destination claim.”

Bren repeated the words to confirm them.

“Enter exactly,” Esa said.

Below, he committed the service state. The hardline carried the click of his key turning. A moment later her slate received a local checksum request. Esa compared it with the pre-service value and signed the changed address as the sole intended difference.

The point went dark during automatic rollback.

Esa’s breath caught.

Then it returned.

Its return did not complete the placement. Through the rail sight, Esa watched the point creep starward as the permanent current warmed the collar. At twelve breaths it crossed the upper limit of her certified band. At sixteen it stopped nine seconds from the coordinate.

“Cut service power,” she said into the tube.

“Bus remains stable.”

“Bearing does not.”

Bren used the manual hold rather than changing the address. The point faded. When Esa reached the workshop, he had left the wand and controller untouched. A curl of pale resin showed beneath the collar’s retaining ear. Test heat had softened an old repair; rollback pressure then settled the collar against a different face.

They treated the failed placement as evidence, not an inconvenience. Esa read the controller state aloud while Bren copied it onto a red-edged service sheet. He marked the collar before removal at top, inhabited side, and contact face. She signed across each mark. Only then did he release the spring.

The resin curl came free with the collar. Bren caught it on black paper instead of brushing it from the socket. Its flattened edge matched the nine-second direction of travel. He placed paper and collar in separate starch sleeves, wrote the time of removal on both, and gave Esa the serial halves from their seals. The failed state now had custody independent of his workshop ledger.

A clean collar would have changed two variables at once: seating face and part history. Bren chose the same collar, removed no material, and rotated it so the undamaged ear carried spring pressure. Esa objected that rotation could move the optical center. He agreed and entered it as a new placement rather than a correction hidden under the prior reading.

They repeated the test at one count before returning to two. From below, Bren called bus load and collar temperature every five breaths. From the rail, Esa took blind readings in alternating order so expectation could not pull them toward the inherited coordinate. The first series sat four seconds low. After the collar reached stable heat, the next two series converged within seven seconds. No neighboring lamp dimmed.

Bren committed the mechanical orientation without altering the controller address. A fresh checksum matched the intended single-address change already signed. Esa attached her three reading sets, including the failed high drift. He closed the physical-placement packet with both seal halves visible under the starch.

Only after custody and correction agreed did Esa certify the node again.

Permanent maintenance state had restored it after catalog authority rejected the empty address. For the first time, a Carver node survived under an accurate public purpose and two living names.

The cost arrived immediately. Her slate displayed a formal notice from the archivist: Uncatalogued cartographic necessity entered at Ward Twelve. Inquiry required before next work interval. Service credentials subject to temporary review.

Bren’s access would freeze at morning bell. Esa’s cartographic standing could follow.

A chime sounded at the mesh door before Esa reached the lower ladder. Ward Twelve’s duty clerk stood outside the black stripe with the same rigid posture used for smoke alarms. The clerk held a paper copy of the provenance conflict and a lockbox for Bren’s contact key.

“Service review requires custody of active credentials,” the clerk said.

“Review begins at morning bell,” Bren replied.

“Protective custody begins now if catalog authority and local service disagree.”

Bren looked at the controller. Removing his key would not erase the certified node, but it would prevent any correction if the shared contact began stealing current again. “Neighbor branch showed a one-count drop at three. We committed at two. Bus stable for fifty-eight breaths.”

“Recorded?”

“Here.” Esa offered her measurements.

The clerk did not take them across the stripe. “State whether a named navigation star lost intensity under committed state.”

“No,” Esa said. “During a higher test state, yes. We ended that test and recorded the bridge.”

“Can the bridge fail under two counts?”

Bren answered. “Possible under teaching heat. Low likelihood before morning.”

“Possible requires local watch.”

“I will watch.”

“Without a contact key?”

“Manual cutoff remains outside credential control.”

The clerk considered the procedure, then opened the lockbox. Bren removed the key from his neck. Its cord had left a pale line beneath his collar, evidence of years spent carrying access even while he slept. He placed it in the box.

“Seal number,” the clerk said.

Esa read the number aloud while Bren checked the lock. The clerk signed custody and passed them a carbon copy. “Workshop stays under physical watch until lesson rise. No layer entry. Any lamp change triggers dome evacuation from the western gallery.”

Bren gave one nod.

The clerk’s gaze moved to the faint route geometry on Esa’s paper. Curiosity showed, then discipline shut it away. “Your inquiry can explain the rest at morning.”

After the door closed, Bren tested the manual cutoff and laid a stool beside it. He would spend the remaining dark interval watching current without authority to alter the work. Esa understood the practical price of her insistence on an honest form. Secrecy had let him manage every consequence himself. Public provenance brought outside custody, a second judgment, and limits neither conspirator could waive.

“We could have used no,” he said.

“And kept your key.”

“Yes.”

“Do you regret it?”

“Ask after the review.”

He had already printed the notice and placed it beside the Carver strip.

“One node,” he said. “One career each.”

“Review is not revocation.”

“Yet.”

They checked the work before discussing further consequence. Bren closed the controller housing as far as the custody rule allowed and handed Esa the post-service checksum copy. She rebuilt the route with the final node included. Its endpoint still lay outside the dome. More important, the unequal spaces between doubled nodes now divided into groups. Read as distances, they looked arbitrary. Read as old coordinate intervals, they produced a repeated structure.

“A cipher,” Esa said.

“Possibly.”

“The final group needs the attitude register.”

“Sealed telemetry.”

“And early destination tables. Also sealed.”

Bren folded his arms. “You can ask the archivist.”

“It may suspend me before answering.”

“Then Ward Twelve asks why a truthful service entry caused suspension.”

“That exposes the line.”

“It is exposed.” He tapped their names on the notice. “Decide what replaces hiding.”

Esa placed her route reduction beside his inherited strip. “Joint custody. Every claim carries source and limit. No public destination until raw telemetry verifies the course. No further change to the projection without a second witness.”

Bren added, “Failed tests stay with successful ones.”

“Yes.”

“If telemetry contradicts the route, we stop.”

“Yes.”

“If it holds, you take the evidence to your ward. I stand beside it.”

“And we complete the cipher together.”

He looked at the final node’s service address on the checksum, then offered his hand across the bench. Not a census clasp. A worker’s grip, palm to palm, each able to feel whether the other pulled away.

Esa took it.

The agreement entered no ward ledger that night, but its evidence did. Two signatures held the final node in the false sky. One inquiry notice bound their offices to the cut. Between them lay a sequence neither could decode alone.

At morning bell, they would go to the archivist and ask for the hidden course.

13 — The Homeward Mark

Vhen’s slate had spent twenty years teaching Esa where not to look.

Its face carried the ordinary master grid: twelve declination bands, the old constellation joins, corrections in Vhen’s square hand. The back carried scratches Esa had treated as a private eccentricity. Six short strokes stood beside a ladder of paired numbers. A hooked line crossed the pairs at irregular intervals. Vhen had once called it a launch-era coordinate exercise and refused to explain further.

“An exercise you cannot solve stays useful,” the old cartographer had said. “It prevents the slate from becoming furniture.”

Now Esa laid that scratched back beneath the Carvers’ completed route.

Bren stood across the western-truss bench with both palms braced against its rim. Between them, a projection sheet held the route at one two-hundredth dome scale. Thirty-seven changes of direction showed as pinpricks along a line too deliberate for stellar drift. The final stroke left the mapped dome and terminated in blank film.

“You have looked at that slate before,” Bren said.

“I looked at its numbers. I assumed they named practice coordinates.”

“Vhen gave you a cipher without telling you.”

“Vhen gave me a problem.” Esa turned the slate until the hooked line aligned with the route’s eastern origin. “Whether it belongs to this problem remains unproved.”

Bren’s mouth tightened with approval. Carvers trusted suspicion more readily than hope.

They began with the obvious reading. Each route node had a dome coordinate, a brightness setting, and a date of insertion. Esa ordered the nodes by date. Bren copied the last digit of each declination beside the ladder pairs. The resulting groups fell within the scratched range, which encouraged them for almost four minutes.

The first six groups yielded consonants under an old ward syllabary: R, T, H, K, M, N.

“Could be compressed,” Bren said.

“It could be anything if we grant it missing vowels.”
