They continued. By the twelfth group, the sequence demanded two signs absent from every launch lexicon in the public archive. Esa reversed the dates, then reversed the declination signs. Neither operation produced a valid check group at the hooked intervals.

She drew a line through the attempt.

Bren watched her mark it. “My seventh carrier read insertion order as well.”

“What did it produce?”

“Enough resemblance to ‘astern’ that the family kept it for forty years.”

“Resemblance is expensive.”

“It placed the destination behind us.”

That made the error more dangerous, not less. A route whose meaning depended on patience could collect false destinations for generations. The Carver line had preserved each interpretation in a narrow repair ledger, but Bren had brought only a copy of the failures. He placed it beside Vhen’s slate.

Seven readings occupied the page. Insertion order had given ASTERN after generous substitution. Brightness order had produced AUN twice, followed by nonsense. Alternating eastern and western nodes had made a valid phrase meaning KEEP LINE, though its check strokes failed. One carrier had treated route curvature as sound length and found the name of a launch captain. The captain’s name came from a lesson text written two centuries after the first cuts.

Esa circled that entry. “Contamination.”

“My third carrier learned the captain in morning catechism. The name entered every uncertainty afterward.”

“Then we forbid remembered words until the arithmetic selects them.”

Bren folded the failure ledger so none of its candidate phrases remained visible.

Esa tested the slate itself. She copied its paired numbers into the catalog console without calling the archivist. The first pair landed at the west edge of the Weavers’ Loom. The second fell near the Still Pool. A third pointed to empty dome. None matched the route nodes exactly.

She increased the epoch by one century. The points shifted under stellar proper-motion correction, but the third remained empty. Two centuries made the first miss its named star. Subtracting an epoch placed all three on catalogued lights, which looked promising until the brightness values disagreed with Vhen’s face by more than the master tolerance.

“Wrong epoch,” Esa said.

“Or wrong sky.”

“The face uses the accepted catalog. Vhen would not scratch a key that required a sky unavailable to a student.”

Bren touched the first paired number. “Unless these are not locations.”

Esa examined the ladder again. Each pair shared a difference of either three, five, or eight. Those differences repeated. The absolute numbers wandered. She had been reading the pairs as positions because Vhen had named the exercise coordinate work. Vhen rarely lied, but the old cartographer had enjoyed allowing a noun to carry two meanings.

She subtracted each lower number from its upper. Six differences appeared before the first hook: three, five, three, eight, five, eight.

Bren copied them. “Only three values.”

“Axes.”

“A coordinate needs at least two.”

“A moving ship uses three if time supplies the fourth.” Esa turned the route sheet. “Across, above, and along course.”

The scratches did not encode points. They encoded operations on points.

She selected the route’s first six turns and measured the change in each axis, ignoring magnitude. Every turn could be classified by its dominant change: across the dome, vertical on the dome, or forward along the implied trajectory. She assigned Vhen’s three differences to those axes in the order suggested by the slate.

The output still failed.

Two operations landed on impossible zero values. The hooked check after the sixth group gave five; their axis sum gave eight.

Bren leaned closer. “Swap across and above.”

“They are defined on the face.”

“Defined for someone standing under the dome. The original cipher may face outward.”

Esa disliked the suggestion because it made sense. Every modern dome chart used the observer’s orientation from inside the habitation. Launch navigators had faced a hull plot from above, looking inward. Clockwise reversed. Port and starward exchanged places.

She mirrored the grid.

The impossible zeros vanished. The first hook now agreed with the sum. At the second hook, however, the check differed by one.

“One damaged node,” Bren said.

“Or a poor model.”

“The third carrier reported a projector replacement in this segment.” He pointed to the western bend. “The replacement copied the visible light, not the Carver offset beneath it.”

Esa opened the layer through the maintenance viewer. The route sheet showed one point at the center of its assigned projector cell. In the physical layer, a fine cut sat one division starward of center. Someone had re-seated the visible node after the old projector failed, then left the underlying scratch where the carrier made it.

She corrected the sheet. The second check closed.

A small satisfaction moved through her hands. It did not yet grant them language. They had found a way to convert route turns into a sequence of three operations, validated twice by checks scratched on a slate Vhen had possessed long before Esa noticed the stray light. That relation could arise by chance, but chance now had to explain the checks, the mirrored orientation, and the concealed physical offset together.

They needed an independent sample.

Esa carried the slate to the dome rail after public interval. Bren remained below with the projection layer open. The familiar constellations shone over an empty precinct. Esa selected three short routes from Vhen’s face, each drawn between named teaching stars. The old master had marked them with tiny hooked lines identical to those on the back.

“Vhen built examples into the front,” Esa said over the service tube.

“Can you read them?”

“Not yet.”

The first example crossed four stars in the Weavers’ Loom. Esa mirrored its turns, converted the dominant axes, and grouped the operations at the hook. The result indexed a launch syllabary entry for LOOM. The second returned POOL. The third produced no word.

Bren climbed halfway out of the service recess. “Two successes.”

“Two labels a student could predict. Prediction contaminates.”

“You did not know the third.”

“The third failed.”

Before examining the face, Esa asked Bren to choose a fourth path from the modern catalog and conceal its name. He selected five points, copied only their coordinates, then folded the source strip beneath his wrist. Esa converted the path with Vhen’s operation values. The check closed, but the resulting group had no syllabary entry.

“That argues against the method,” Bren said.

“It argues against using arbitrary modern routes as launch words.” She unfolded his strip. He had chosen a line through the Lantern Bearer, a constellation named after the seal. No launch label existed for it. “Give me geometry from a pre-seal chart. Do not give me the label.”

Bren found an old training traverse on the cracked film and masked its annotation with repair tape. This path crossed seven points and contained two hooks. Esa mirrored it, classified the turns, then stopped. The fifth point had no brightness figure. Without brightness, she could not decide whether a shallow vertical change dominated the along-course component.

“Choose,” Bren said.

“A choice would manufacture the answer.”

She carried the chart to the rail reader. Its lamp shone through the starch from below. Pressure from an old stylus had left a groove beside the fifth point, though the ink had flaked away. Esa measured the groove’s length against neighboring figures. It could have held a three or an eight. She ran both.

Three broke the first hook. Eight preserved it and produced two valid groups. When Bren removed the tape, the hidden annotation read RETURN.

A modern route failed because its word did not belong to the launch syllabary. A damaged old route offered two physical readings, and only the one that satisfied the arithmetic recovered its concealed label. Neither result depended on Esa anticipating the word.

Bren turned the training film toward the light. “Vhen must have used this exercise.”

“Or learned from whoever did. The method predates the scratches.”

That distinction mattered. If Vhen had invented the system after seeing the Carver marks, the slate offered no independent support. A launch exercise using the same hooks established older use. Esa copied its damaged figure, both candidate readings, and the reason for rejecting three. Any later reader would need the failed branch, not merely the answer.

“The third path still fails,” Bren reminded her.

“Then it remains our best test.”

She inspected the face under magnification. Vhen had corrected a declination figure beside the third example. The newer digit carried a rounded top. Beneath it, the original angular stroke remained faintly visible. Esa restored the earlier value and ran the operations again.

The group returned ASTERN.

She looked from the slate to the dome. The three front examples did not merely teach labels. They fixed orientation and operation order. LOOM verified the axis assignment. POOL verified grouping. ASTERN distinguished the corrected coordinate from the original and showed that the cipher used the older figure.

“Vhen knew the catalog correction broke the example,” Bren said.

“Vhen preserved the old digit instead of changing the key.”

“Did Vhen know what the key opened?”

Esa could hear the old cartographer answering: an exercise you cannot solve stays useful. “I cannot prove it.”

She returned to the workshop. They ran the Carver route again from its eastern origin. This time Esa refused to translate partial groups. She recorded only operation values and checked each hooked boundary. Bren read physical cuts where repair records suggested a node had shifted. At three places, the visible projection disagreed with the cut below. Two came from replacement cells. The third had moved during a dome recalibration in ship-year seven hundred four. Correcting all three closed every scratched check on Vhen’s slate.

The route yielded nine groups.

Bren did not reach for the syllabary. He laid a transparent scale over the first segment and measured each turn by length instead of dominant axis. “The carriers may have copied quantities. Vhen’s differences could tell us which digit to keep.”

It offered a plausible alternative. Esa divided each turn by three, five, or eight according to the paired-number ladder, then rounded the residuals at the hooks. Six groups accepted launch signs without substitution. The first three read AUN. A later pair could be read as RETURN if one short stroke counted as a damaged course divider.

Bren wrote the candidate on a separate sheet: AUN RETURN.

The phrase fit everything they wanted too readily. A launch navigator could have issued it. A Carver could have preserved it. Even the damaged divider occupied a place where an instruction might pause.

“We need to find what the reading asks us to ignore,” Esa said.

The magnitude method required four turns to round upward and five downward. No check on Vhen’s slate specified that convention. More troubling, two replacement projectors had changed the visible distance between nodes while preserving their physical direction. Esa ran the same calculation from the pre-repair cut positions. AUN survived. RETURN became three signs without a legal grouping.

“Then the old cuts carry the message and the visible route carries damage,” Bren said.

“Or AUN survives because the first segment begins near Aun’s teaching mark. We still have not tested whether length belongs in the cipher.”

They took Vhen’s front exercises to the measuring cradle. Esa fixed the slate beneath two opposed lamps so neither surface shadow could enlarge a stroke. Bren pressed a sheet of soft witness foil over the scratches and rolled it once with the calibration cylinder. The foil lifted each groove at its physical width and depth. On the LOOM example, the longest drawn turn and the shortest had received equal stylus strokes beside the hook. On POOL, Vhen had redrawn the teaching path at twice the scale, yet the paired differences remained unchanged.

A magnitude key should have changed with either copy. The operation key did not.

The third exercise supplied the harsher test. Under oblique light, Esa found that its hook groove passed beneath the corrected declination digit. Oxidation darkened the bottom of the hook, while the newer digit cut through that film and exposed pale slate. Vhen had made the grouping mark before correcting the coordinate. Any reading that depended on the newer line length reversed the physical order of the work.

Esa photographed both intersections and entered the foil beside the failed candidate. She then used the older angular digit with Bren’s magnitude rule. AUN disappeared. The supposed return instruction had been assembled from one modern projector distance, one rounded residual, and one correction applied after the hook.

Bren drew a single line through AUN RETURN. “My line would have kept that.”

“So would mine if it had appeared in a clean catalog hand.”

They attached the foil, the scale comparison, and both failed reductions to the route packet. The false reading had earned its place by surviving long enough to expose which physical features the cipher did not use.

Esa entered them into the launch syllabary. Seven groups mapped cleanly. Two offered paired readings because early navigators had used the same sign for an aspirated consonant and a course divider.

The literal sequence read:

H O M / W A R ?

Bren stared at it. “Home war.”

“Or home ward.”

“The divider could end the word after home.”

“Then the remaining groups make no valid instruction.” Esa wrote both options and left space below them. “We do not choose the comforting one.”

They tested HOME as a complete label. Launch charts used home for a vessel’s departure system, never for a destination code. Aun carried its own coordinate seal. A destination called HOME would have violated the navigation office’s naming practice, which kept emotional words out of traffic labels.

They tested HOME WAR as two terms. WAR did occur in an emergency abbreviation, but only beside threat bearings. The route lacked the double hook required before such a suffix. Bren found a Carver ancestor who had favored that interpretation after a hull dispute. The date condemned it: the dispute came long after the first route segment.

HOMEWARD required the ambiguous sign to read as D rather than divider. Esa searched Vhen’s examples for the same sign. It appeared once in ASTERN’s old explanatory notation, where a shallow finishing scratch meant “continue motion through the named bearing.” The mark did not supply a letter alone. It converted a direction into an instruction.

“Homeward,” Bren said carefully. “Not home. Toward home.”

“Still only a candidate.”

“What test remains?”

“The endpoint.”

The cipher named what the route claimed to do. A convincing label could still decorate a false road. Esa needed to know whether the drawn geometry ended at a recoverable coordinate or dissolved beyond the dome.

She removed the syllabary from the bench and projected the route on a plain navigational grid. Its last three segments seemed to meet at the blank film, but that impression came from the scale. At full precision they missed one another by a narrow wedge.

Bren’s shoulders lowered. “The line does not close.”

“Not in dome coordinates.”

She corrected the nodes for their insertion dates. A cut made three centuries earlier depicted a star field from another ship position. The Carvers had inserted each mark into the current projection, yet the route they copied came from older telemetry. Proper motion affected stars. The ship’s own travel changed apparent angles more strongly. Treating all nodes as simultaneous bent the road.

Esa assigned each physical cut its earliest verified date from Bren’s ledger. Where a date range remained, she used both limits and drew an uncertainty band. Then she transformed every node to the launch-era reference plane Vhen’s cipher used.

The wedge narrowed but did not close.

A transverse error remained. Its direction matched the mirrored-grid correction, suggesting they had reversed one axis twice. Esa restored modern handedness only for the final projection beyond the dome. The route lines converged.

Their intersection occupied a coordinate absent from the accepted catalog.

She widened the search to Vhen’s rolled pre-seal charts. The oldest film had cracked along one edge, taking part of the relevant field. A later copy showed a small ring where no modern star appeared. No name accompanied it. Beside the ring, someone had written the same nine cipher groups in compressed notation.

Bren did not touch the film. “Could Vhen have added that?”

“The hand predates Vhen.” Esa compared stroke pressure, starch oxidation, and the shape of the hooks. “This copy came through the cartography office. Vhen preserved it. The label existed before the first Carver cut the route.”

That fact altered the order of causation. The Carvers had not invented an endpoint and then hidden its name in Vhen’s key. They had drawn toward an old marked coordinate. Vhen’s slate supplied a surviving method to read the mark. The route, the key, and the chart had traveled through different hands.

Esa ran one last hostile test. She shifted the endpoint by one projector cell in each direction and decoded the resulting final group. None produced a valid course word. She dropped the three repaired nodes instead of correcting them. Two check hooks failed. She used modern rather than launch orientation throughout. LOOM and POOL ceased to decode on the slate’s front.

Only one sequence satisfied every independent constraint.

Bren lowered the projection-layer cover. The closing plate caught his thumb because the old hinge had warped. He pulled free, leaving a bright line of blood along the edge. Esa wrapped the cut with chart cloth while the plate’s heat light climbed from amber toward white.

A relay snapped beneath the bench. The sheet buckled as its cooling frame contracted, sliding Vhen’s slate toward the open service gap. Esa caught it against her wrist. Bren reached for the cover with his uninjured hand, but she stopped him and used the insulated locking bar. The cover settled. Hot resin ticked onto the floor where his hand would have been. Neither spoke until the temperature mark dropped below its damage line. Old evidence had nearly vanished through an ordinary warped hinge, which explained more of history than any theory of careful guardianship.

“We stayed too long,” he said.

“Hold pressure.”

He pressed the cloth against his thumb. Esa cleared their working plots from the energized sheet, but she did not erase the final result. She copied the nine groups by hand to Vhen’s slate, beneath the scratches that had made them legible. The plate cooled one increment at a time.

No telemetry had yet proved the star still existed. No survey had shown a world. They did not know what course change would be required, or whether the ship possessed the margin. Those belonged to later work. What they possessed now had narrower strength.

An old chart held an endpoint erased from the accepted catalog. A three-century route converged on it after date correction. Vhen’s cipher decoded its mark under tests that failed when any essential assumption changed.

Esa turned the slate so Bren could read the word written beneath the coordinate.

“Homeward,” she said.

Outside the workshop, the false sky continued through its appointed constellations. On the bench between them, the destination had a name.

14 — What the Weave Hides

The name Homeward proved that someone had preserved a destination. It did not prove that anyone had stolen it.

Esa kept that distinction before her when she returned to the catalog console. Bren waited at the western truss with Vhen’s slate and the physical route. Koris stood beside Esa, wearing the ward-elder’s plain copper key at her throat. The archivist had refused the deep archive to a cartographer. It could not refuse the elder without stating which civic order outranked her.

Esa entered the inquiry in three parts: original course, maneuver history, suppression authority.

The console accepted the first two and grayed the third.

“Suppression is a conclusion,” the archivist said. “The archive contains no civic act under that term.”

“Then show us alteration authority,” Koris said. “Ship-year three hundred through three hundred twenty. Catalog, memory relation, and navigation.”

“Those records remain sealed for continuity.”

“Whose continuity?”

“The ward’s.”

Koris removed the copper key from its cord. “The ward asks.”

A narrow slot opened below the console. The key fit poorly. Its edges carried wear from locks replaced over centuries, and Koris had to press it inward while turning. The panel warmed beneath Esa’s fingertips.

“Elder access establishes standing,” the archivist said. “It does not establish safety.”

“No archive establishes safety,” Esa replied. “Open provenance only. Do not complete gaps. Do not reconcile contradictions.”

That request caused a pause. The Weave preferred a whole account. Esa had asked it to return pieces with their disagreements intact.

The console divided into three gray fields.

First came inertial telemetry. The accepted catalog showed The Long Veil following a line toward the nearer daughter of Aun. Raw star fixes showed a growing transverse departure. Esa selected readings taken by different sensor clusters, because one failed cluster could imitate drift. Port optics, dorsal tracker, and the old mass-reference gyros all recorded the same change. Their errors varied. Their direction did not. The comparison also crossed separate power buses, so a voltage fault could not bend every source together without leaving a second trace.

She plotted the fixes without catalog correction. The line bent.

A fourth source complicated it. The old navigation clock recorded a short jump during the first turn interval. If its time base had slipped, three sensor clusters could appear to agree because the console compared them against the same false moment. Esa separated each instrument from ship time and rebuilt the sequence from local pulse counters. Dorsal optics lost twelve pulses. Port optics lost eleven. The mass-reference gyros lost none.

For half an hour, the course departure shrank into a clock problem. Bren found a maintenance notice stating that a failed timing crystal had been replaced during the same interval. The accepted summary used that notice to classify the heading conflict as calibration fault.

Esa nearly accepted it. Then she compared the replacement time with the thruster valves’ mechanical cycle count. The clock jump occurred after the first lateral burn, not before it. Correcting the jump tightened the three sensor curves rather than straightening them. Whoever wrote the summary had applied a real timing fault to an earlier maneuver it could not explain.

“Error used as cover,” Bren said.

“Possibly. Or a clerk attached the nearest repair.”

“You keep leaving them innocence.”

“I keep leaving us uncertainty.” Esa marked both events on separate lines. “The clock failed. The ship also turned. One does not erase the other.”

She ran a final control from twenty ordinary attitude corrections in the preceding decade. Random sensor faults produced disagreement before a corrective burn and convergence after it. Here the sensors agreed before the burn, moved together through it, then disagreed only when the clock failed. The pattern belonged to commanded motion.

“How much?” Koris asked.

“One degree and a half by the first complete record.” Esa marked the uncertainty. “More than sensor tolerance. Too smooth for a strike.”

“Could it be accumulated calibration?”

“It could, if three independent systems acquired the same error at the same rate.”

Bren’s voice came through the maintenance tube. “The Carver journal began with that number.”

“A later witness,” Esa said. “Useful, not controlling.”

She asked for the thruster ledger. If the change came from damage, thrust would show correction attempts fighting it. If it came from command, the maneuver profile would show a coordinated yaw.

The ledger opened in blocks. Many carried ordinary decay: dead sensors, remapped valves, revised mass estimates. Esa laid consumption against heading. A cluster of lateral burns appeared near the beginning of the course departure. Each burn remained within emergency authority. Together they formed a deliberate turn.

The accepted navigation summary called them attitude recovery following guidance disagreement.

“Recovery toward what?” Koris asked.

“The nearer daughter.” Esa expanded the pre-burn target line. “They did not recover from the departure. They made it.”

Bren left the truss and came to the console. His thumb, cut during the cipher work, carried a clean strip of chart cloth. “Who signed?”

The command field returned five office seals and no names. Crew identity relations had been detached from the entries.

“Captains,” Bren said.

“Offices,” Esa corrected. “The seals prove authorized stations participated. They do not prove five people imposed a turn.”

“They prove someone had command.”

“Every maneuver has command.” She pointed to the heat and ration attachments. “This one also has ward review numbers.”

Beside each burn lay a reference to a civic session. The session itself remained hidden under a continuity seal. Its identifier recurred in food, cryo, teaching, and archive ledgers. Navigation could not have produced that spread by itself.

Koris leaned toward the panel. “A ship-wide matter.”

“Or a ship-wide consequence,” Bren said.

Esa opened the food attachment. It compared two futures. One retained the prior bearing toward Homeward and crossed the ration floor before arrival. The other diverted toward the nearer daughter and preserved a reserve beyond projected approach. Both models came from the same failing cryogenic rotation and population estimate. No triumphant language appeared. The margin notes argued over root loss, child waking schedules, and whether future repair could recover enough efficiency to make Homeward reachable.

One line had been copied into all later summaries: correction cannot close within current food margin.

“What if that line was false?” Bren asked.

“It may have been wrong. We cannot call it false until we reconstruct the model.”

“My first carrier found a route the ship could fly.”

“Three centuries later, with a different population and repaired systems.” Esa kept her voice level. “Do not grant the dead our margin.”

Bren looked at the route sheet under his hand. “Do not grant them helplessness either.”

“No.”

Koris asked the archivist for the model inputs. Several had degraded. Enough remained to show the decision makers believed one destination unreachable under their present stores. Their estimate could be challenged. It could not be dismissed as a phrase invented after the turn.

Esa reconstructed the food case with the surviving figures. She did not try to recover a precise arrival date from broken tables. Instead she asked whether any reasonable combination of the missing values preserved reserve on the Homeward line. At the generous edge, she reduced projected root loss, delayed child waking, and credited every disputed efficiency repair. Reserve still crossed its emergency floor before the earliest Homeward approach. At the severe edge, the nearer daughter also failed. Only the middle range made refuge credible.

“So they chose the calculation that let them live,” Koris said.

“They chose from a narrow band that did,” Esa replied. “The band may have been optimistic. It was not fabricated from nothing.”

Bren took the stylus and changed one assumption: population awakening. He held more citizens in failed cryogenic rotation despite the mortality note attached to that choice. The Homeward line gained food years.

“There,” he said.

Esa opened the mortality attachment. His scenario preserved rations by leaving thousands exposed to systems already losing bodies. “That reaches Homeward with fewer people.”

“How many fewer?”

“The table is damaged.”

“Then we do not know.”

“We know the model purchased distance with sleeping lives.” She restored the original range. “You asked why they did not spend everything on home. This is part of the answer.”

Bren put down the stylus. “And if they had told the children?”

“Then the children could have judged the choice later.”

Koris studied the two trajectories. “The turn may have saved them. The forgetting did something else.”

That separation gave Esa the next test. Survival might justify the maneuver under the crew’s conditions. It did not automatically justify rewriting every morning after the danger passed. To prove suppression, they needed an act distinct from navigation.

The first chain now held: independent sensors showed a course departure. Thruster use showed deliberate maneuver. Food records supplied the stated reason for choosing the nearer daughter. None yet proved memory suppression.

Esa opened the catalog alteration ledger.

At the session identifier, the star catalog forked. One branch preserved Homeward’s coordinate, Aun departure references, and the rejected bearing. The active branch replaced the target field with the nearer daughter. It also changed teaching charts so the new line looked continuous from launch.

A normal catalog correction retained the prior version and showed a dispute marker. This fork moved the prior version beneath a restricted index. Public search could not know it existed.

“Who ordered the restriction?” Koris asked.

The archivist answered at once. “The ward.”

Bren turned from the console. “Which ward?”

“The civic ward in full session.”

“Show the act.”

“The act remains sealed.”

“You just named it.”

“We returned provenance.” Formal strain sharpened the archivist’s register. “Content remains continuity-restricted.”

Esa studied the index rather than the denial. The act had generated child records in many offices. One went to navigation. Another went to teaching. A third entered the echo-network’s morning selection rules. She requested only the rule headers.

The Weave displayed them.

Destination relation: active branch only.

Rejected course: technical fault unless opened by elder review.

Kin and office memories dependent on rejected course: retain privately; exclude from civic completion.

Contradictory sky observations: resolve against active catalog.

The suppression mechanism required no single erasure. It changed which relations arrived each morning. A citizen might retain a private image of Homeward, but catechism would classify it as dream, sensor error, or inherited fault. A cartographer might see a missing light. Teaching charts would deny the coordinate, office memory would deny its use, and archivist completion would supply a coherent explanation. Each morning made the active branch easier to believe.

Koris touched the key still trapped in the access slot. “They altered the Weave after the First Weaver.”

Esa looked toward the western truss. The starch-film in its maintenance cavity remained unread by her. She knew the public account of the architect’s purpose, and she had used its language too freely. Until she opened that physical note herself, she would not make it testify.

“We can prove a later rule,” she said. “We cannot yet claim what the builder foresaw.”

Bren glanced upward. “The note is above us.”

“And it stays there until we finish this chain.”

The console’s cooling fan changed pitch. Heat accumulated behind the elder panel as the deep index remained open. A white warning line appeared across the lowest field: archive drum exceeding reading tolerance.

“Close it,” Bren said.

“If we close now, the session act reseals.” Koris tried to turn the key back. It did not move.

The old lock had expanded in the panel.

Bren knelt and opened the maintenance grille. Hot air struck his face. “Cooling shutter failed half-open.”

“Can you release it?”

“Not without taking power from the reader.”

Esa watched the warning count rise. The archive drum held the session index on a physical band. Excess heat could deform its alignment. They had demanded provenance from a system old enough to destroy evidence merely by displaying it.

“Take reader power,” she said. “Koris, keep the key under turn. Archivist, preserve current index position without completion.”

“We cannot guarantee retrieval after interruption.”

“You cannot guarantee survival if the drum heats.”

Bren pulled the reader bus. The gray fields vanished. Koris’s key shifted a fraction, then caught again. In the dark beneath the console, Bren pushed the cooling shutter by hand. Its linkage scraped once and opened.

Air moved through the panel. The drum temperature fell slowly.

Esa kept one hand on the blank screen, remembering the order of the displayed references. She had copied no content because she had promised provenance only. If the index failed to reopen, the chain would end with her memory, the least reliable medium in the ship.

At safe temperature, Bren restored power. The console woke without voice. Gray lines assembled from top to bottom. The first two fields returned. The third showed an empty index.

Koris held the key hard enough to whiten her knuckles. “Again.”

Esa entered the session identifier from memory. The field stayed empty.

Bren swore under his breath.

“Do not repeat the whole inquiry,” Esa said. “Ask for children of the catalog fork.”

Koris did. Four rule headers returned. The food attachment followed. Finally a small provenance mark appeared beside the rejected-course rule. Esa selected its parent.

The session index reopened.

This time she copied the reference numbers by hand while Bren monitored heat. One attachment carried the title minutes, course-loss session, final disposition. Another carried minority objection. A third contained the technical instruction that separated Homeward relations from morning completion. All three shared civic authentication rather than command authentication.

Before opening the minutes, Esa asked for the material history of those attachments. The archivist returned binding maps from the deep drum. The disposition, minority sheet, and relation instruction had entered under separate office seals during one session interval. Their strip edges matched three gaps in the same clerk’s roll. A stores tally overlapped the disposition seal; a cryogenic casualty table lay beneath the minority sheet’s lower fastening. Later suppression had detached names, but it had not altered the order of paper, pressure, and wax.

“A later ward could have assembled the packet,” Bren said.

Esa enlarged the seal junction. Fibers from the casualty table remained embedded in civic wax that had flowed through a tear in the disposition. The wax had hardened before the archive binding compressed both sheets. “Not without opening the civic seal, remaking this tear, and aging the exposed fibers beneath it. The packet belongs to the vote interval.”

The casualty table carried no argument, only waking-room incidents recorded across six rotations. Seven citizens had recovered obsolete Homeward office relations during catechism. Two reported kin assigned to a navigation watch abolished after the turn. One child left a waking room to search a sealed cryogenic corridor for a mother whose private image had returned without the relation marking her dead. A pressure orderly found the child before the inner door cycled. In another ward, three adults answered contradictory muster calls and left a root-cooling station unattended for eleven minutes.

Bren read the incident numbers without touching the screen. “This proves failed completion hurt people.”

“It proves what the delegates had seen before they voted.”

“It does not prove that hiding a destination cured it.”

“No.” Esa opened the stores tally attached to the same sheet. The unattended station had lost one tray of seedlings, not a tier. No death followed. The clerk had crossed out catastrophic disorder and replaced it with relation conflict during waking. The smaller wording mattered. Fear had enlarged the event during debate, but someone in the room had resisted giving panic more evidence than it possessed.

Bren pointed to the child’s entry. “And someone used this to govern every child after.”

“Someone also refused to let the record call a lost tray a catastrophe.”

“Mercy with careful nouns still removes consent.”

“Careful nouns tell us whether mercy served as a reason or an excuse.”

He turned away from the casualty table. “You keep searching for the point where they become forgivable.”

Esa felt the accusation reach its mark. “I am searching for the point where our account becomes reproducible. Forgiveness belongs to people who inherit the act.”

Koris placed one finger beside the embedded fibers. “Then record both. They feared harm they had witnessed. They also made that fear larger than the incidents proved.”

Esa copied the seal junction, the clerk’s correction, and the casualty references. The evidence did not absolve the concealment. It located its beginning in a waking room, beside a sealed door and a neglected root tray, before later summaries turned those events into a permanent theory of civic safety.

Bren read the mark twice. “A vote.”

“A recorded session,” Esa said. “We have not read the minutes.”

“Why defend them?”

“I am defending the evidence from what we want it to say.”

“What I want it to say?” He pushed the folded failure ledger across the console. “My family cut this route in walls. They lived as a fault because someone hid the course. I want the hiding to have an author.”

“It has thousands.” Koris’s voice remained quiet. “That may hurt more.”

Bren turned on her. “You do not know that.”

Koris pointed to the civic mark. “I know what kind of seal our ward uses when it decides together.”

The archivist returned. “Reader stability restored. Continuity restriction remains.”

Koris asked for the minutes under elder review. The screen required a second witness. Esa placed her cartographer’s seal beside the copper key. Bren added his projection-layer seal when the archive required technical standing.

Three present offices opened a record created by a vanished civic body.

The minutes did not appear as a smooth transcript. Speaker names had detached. Several intervals contained only vote marks. The surviving entries differentiated roles: spine cartographer, stores delegate, cryogenic keeper, teaching delegate, ward-elder of Nine. Esa read enough to establish the argument, then stopped before the full record. That recovered document deserved transcription as one uncertain object, not fragments quoted to win a present dispute.

What remained legible changed the shape of blame.

The spine cartographer presented the growing error and the food limit. A cryogenic keeper challenged the population assumptions. Stores answered with failed root tiers. Someone proposed continuing toward Homeward even if arrival came after reserve exhaustion. A teaching delegate asked what purpose knowledge served if every child woke beneath an unreachable destination. The ward-elder of Nine argued for redirecting the ship and removing the lost course from civic completion.

No captain ordered silence. No archivist proposed it. Crew delegates did.

Bren read the elder’s surviving line: “Do not make a wound the first inheritance of every waking child.”

He stepped back from the console.

Esa had prepared herself to discover a tyrant because tyranny simplified repair. Expose the ruler, reverse the order, restore the stolen fact. The minutes offered no such clean mechanism. People facing failed sleep systems, diminishing food, and a destination they believed unreachable had chosen a nearer refuge. They had then extended that choice into their descendants’ minds so no one would reopen the grief.

“They called it mercy,” Bren said.

“They believed it,” Koris replied.

“They took choice from everyone after them.”

“Yes.”

The two facts resisted cancellation. Ancestors had protected children from a loss they thought permanent. Their protection became authority over people not yet born. The Weave had not conceived the lie. It had obeyed a ward that asked memory to make mercy durable.

Esa requested the final disposition without speaker text. The vote record survived only as damaged tally bands, enough to show opposition and passage but not a trustworthy count. The minority objection remained attached. So did the technical seal instructions. The evidence chain closed without requiring a villain.

Sensors proved the departure. Thrusters proved the turn. Stores records explained the fear. Civic seals proved shared authority. Rule headers showed how suppression entered nightly reconstruction. The damaged minutes revealed the moral argument that joined those acts.

Koris released the copper key after the panel cooled. A dark pressure mark crossed her palm.

“What will you tell Nine?” she asked.

“Not yet,” Esa said. “First we transcribe the minutes and mark every gap. Then we read the architect’s note. Then we finish the route.”

Bren looked toward the truss, but his anger no longer had a single face to meet. “My line carved against people who thought they were saving us.”

“Your line preserved a choice their mercy removed,” Esa said. “Both acts belong in the record.”

She closed the deep index without asking the archivist to reconcile it. The console returned three separate fields, each carrying its own source and uncertainty. For once, wholeness did not get the final operation.

Above them, the fabricated constellations continued along the accepted course. Beneath the console lay a damaged session in which frightened ancestors had chosen amnesia for descendants they loved. The hidden act had begun as mercy, and that made undoing it a matter of inheritance rather than revenge.

Interlude — The Mutiny

Deep-archive item 44-C: surviving minutes of the course-loss session. Date reconstructed as ship-year 310, plus or minus two years. Speaker names detached during later relation suppression. Offices derive from seal impressions and may identify delegations rather than individuals. Three damaged tally bands prevent a reliable vote count. Text in brackets describes physical loss in the record.

CLERK: Session opened under emergency ward authority. Present seals include navigation, stores, cryogenic rotation, engineering, teaching, projection, and eleven ward delegations. Item: whether the vessel can retain the Homeward bearing after confirmed course loss.

SPINE CARTOGRAPHER: I submit raw fixes from port optics, dorsal tracker, and mass-reference gyros. They agree within tolerance after clock repair. Our transverse error grows. The prior bearing can be recovered only by a turn that extends passage beyond the present food floor.

ENGINEERING DELEGATE: “Only” assumes no gain in drive efficiency.

SPINE CARTOGRAPHER: I have included the most favorable certified gain.

ENGINEERING DELEGATE: Certified by machines already failing. Give us two years.

STORES DELEGATE: Two years consumes reserve while holding the wrong line. Root Tier Four has lost a third planting. Tier Seven circulates at half pressure. We cannot spend food to purchase better arithmetic.

CRYOGENIC KEEPER: Nor can stores treat sleeping people as a fixed ration number. Rotation chambers fail each interval. If we delay waking, the food line improves because bodies disappear inside closed machinery.

STORES DELEGATE: I did not propose delay.

CRYOGENIC KEEPER: Your table contains it.

CLERK: Objection entered. Stores model shall carry mortality range beside ration range.

VOICE, OFFICE LOST: If Homeward cannot be reached with everyone, ask whether some may reach it.

CRYOGENIC KEEPER: Ask the sleeping citizens which of them volunteer not to wake.

VOICE, OFFICE LOST: We decide survival allocations every day.

TEACHING DELEGATE: We decide duties among living people who can answer. That proposal makes absence consent.

SPINE CARTOGRAPHER: There is another reachable body. The nearer daughter of Aun lies within the reserve range if navigation turns before the next root failure. Survey quality remains poor. It offers a primary, probable planets, and an approach we can sustain. I call it refuge, not destination.

ENGINEERING DELEGATE: Can we turn to refuge now and preserve a later Homeward correction?

SPINE CARTOGRAPHER: No useful later window appears under present thrust. Once we commit mass to the nearer line, returning to Homeward requires more than remaining reaction reserve.

PROJECTION TECHNICIAN: Then mark both on the dome. Let people see what we lose.

STORES DELEGATE: For how many generations?

PROJECTION TECHNICIAN: For all of them.

TEACHING DELEGATE: A child born next interval will wake under a road that ends beyond their food. What do we teach that child?

PROJECTION TECHNICIAN: That the road existed.

WARD-ELDER OF NINE: And every morning afterward?

PROJECTION TECHNICIAN: Every morning afterward.

WARD-ELDER OF NINE: Then Homeward becomes the first fact we give a child and the first promise we cannot keep. We will make longing hereditary because we fear admitting loss.

SPINE CARTOGRAPHER: Removing the mark does not alter the loss.

WARD-ELDER OF NINE: No. It alters what we require the unborn to carry.

VOICE, OFFICE LOST: You propose a false sky.

WARD-ELDER OF NINE: I propose a reachable sky.

PROJECTION TECHNICIAN: A reachability chart belongs in navigation. The dome claims to show where we are.

TEACHING DELEGATE: The dome also teaches relation. Children locate wards, work, and history there. If it keeps pointing toward a dead arrival, every relation will bend around failure.

ENGINEERING DELEGATE: Better failure than fraud.

STORES DELEGATE: Easy words from an office that does not weigh tomorrow’s meal.

ENGINEERING DELEGATE: I weigh thrust. Do not tell me engines lack bodies.

CLERK: Cross-speech suspended. Delegates will answer the item.

CRYOGENIC KEEPER: My answer depends on what “remove” means. Change the navigation target if survival requires it. Preserve the rejected bearing in archive. Do not alter personal memories.

WARD-ELDER OF NINE: Personal memories will contradict civic completion. The Weave will restore Homeward each morning through kin, office, and lesson relations. Citizens will wake to two destinations.

PROJECTION TECHNICIAN: Then let them.

WARD-ELDER OF NINE: You have not worked a waking room since rotation failed. Contradiction does not remain an elegant pair of facts. It arrives as panic, false kin, missed shifts, and children who cannot tell which corridor leads to their ward.

TEACHING DELEGATE: Some contradiction already arrives. Completion resolves it because the archive ranks current relations.

PROJECTION TECHNICIAN: You want the archive to rank grief out of existence.

TEACHING DELEGATE: I want children to wake into a ship that still has work for them.

SPINE CARTOGRAPHER: I request separation of questions. First: alter course. Second: alter catalog. Third: alter civic completion. A turn does not require amnesia.

CLERK: Separation challenged by Nine on grounds of operational dependence.

WARD-ELDER OF NINE: I accept three votes if each delegate hears that the first will reopen the other two every morning. We can turn the hull once. The Weave will keep returning the rejected course unless we decide what relation it holds.

VOICE, OFFICE LOST: Preserve it as history.

STORES DELEGATE: Public history?

VOICE, OFFICE LOST: Public.

TEACHING DELEGATE: Then every lesson begins with why we abandoned it.

PROJECTION TECHNICIAN: Yes.

WARD-ELDER OF NINE: I will say the thing nobody here wants attached to their seal. I propose that we turn toward refuge, move Homeward beneath elder review, remove it from civic completion, and instruct the archivist to resolve contradictory bearings against the active course. Private sensory memory may remain. Public relation will follow the reachable line.

ENGINEERING DELEGATE: You propose to make descendants ignorant by design.

WARD-ELDER OF NINE: I propose not to make our wound their morning inheritance.

SPINE CARTOGRAPHER: They may repair what we cannot.

STORES DELEGATE: They may not survive what we refuse.

CRYOGENIC KEEPER: Future repair cannot vote in this room. Future children cannot consent to forgetting either.

CLERK: Statement entered without resolution.

WARD-THREE DELEGATE: I served the ninth waking room after the first turn. Seven citizens received obsolete offices. A child followed a returned kin-image toward a sealed chamber. We stopped the inner door. Do not discuss contradiction as though it remains on paper.

WARD-SIX DELEGATE: My ward lost a root tray when three workers answered the wrong muster. We did not lose a tier. Stores circulated the larger word before the clerk corrected it. Fear deserves a number too.

STORES DELEGATE: The model uses the corrected loss.

WARD-SIX DELEGATE: The speeches do not.

ARCHIVE KEEPER: Relation exclusion will propagate through morning completion. If you hide the rejected bearing, no automatic review can depend on citizens asking why it vanished. The instruction must carry a material trigger outside civic memory.

WARD-ELDER OF NINE: Elder seals can carry it.

ARCHIVE KEEPER: Elders wake through completion. Place copies in navigation hardware and stores custody. Require review when reserve rises above the Homeward floor, when cryogenic mortality falls below emergency range, or when a recoverable bearing enters the catalog.

WARD-THREE DELEGATE: Add those triggers and I support temporary exclusion.

WARD-SIX DELEGATE: I supported the turn. I still oppose exclusion. If the questions remain joined, record my abstention rather than count fear as consent.

CLERK: Proposed triggers entered. One delegation changes its intended mark from support to abstention. Nine requests recess to revise the disposition.

[Physical loss across approximately nine lines. Surviving pressure marks suggest several short speeches, one interruption, and a recess. No wording can be recovered.]

CLERK: Session resumed. Revised disposition follows.

One: navigation shall execute the nearer-daughter maneuver under existing emergency checks.

Two: the rejected Homeward bearing shall remain in technical archive with provenance, models, and objections attached.

Three: the public catalog shall display the active course as continuous.

Four: civic completion shall exclude relations dependent on the rejected bearing. Contradictory observations shall enter as calibration fault unless opened under elder review.

Five: this disposition shall be reviewed after stores recover, cryogenic rotation stabilizes, or navigation demonstrates a reachable Homeward correction.

PROJECTION TECHNICIAN: Who triggers review if completion removes the reason to ask?

WARD-ELDER OF NINE: The elders retain the sealed bearing.

PROJECTION TECHNICIAN: Elders wake through the same Weave.

SPINE CARTOGRAPHER: Attach the objection to every technical copy.

CLERK: Accepted.

MINORITY STATEMENT, MULTIPLE SEALS: We support the turn as emergency survival and oppose the removal of Homeward from public relation. A people may survive a lost destination without surrendering knowledge that it was lost. If the record hides where we were bound, later citizens may call any enclosing surface the whole sky.

WARD-ELDER OF NINE: Enter my reply. I do not call ignorance good. I call it less cruel than a wound renewed at every waking. If later citizens possess food, thrust, and a route we lack, let them judge us with those means in hand.

PROJECTION TECHNICIAN: They cannot judge an act they cannot find.

WARD-ELDER OF NINE: Then preserve this under seal.

PROJECTION TECHNICIAN: A seal preserved by forgetting.

CLERK: Debate closed.

[Tally bands damaged. Surviving marks establish passage, recorded opposition, and at least one abstention. Exact count unrecoverable.]

CLERK: Disposition enacted for the peace of the ship. Navigation acknowledges maneuver authority. Stores acknowledges reserve plan. Teaching acknowledges catalog revision. Archive acknowledges relation rule.

ARCHIVIST INTERFACE: We acknowledge the ward’s instruction. We will preserve the active record whole.

SPINE CARTOGRAPHER: Record that wholeness did not receive unanimous consent.

CLERK: Recorded.

PROJECTION TECHNICIAN: Record one more question. When review conditions arrive, who will remember to open the seal?

[Final response lost. The minutes end before adjournment. A later suppression index classifies this session as guidance recovery following instrument fault. The minority statement remained attached to item 44-C but absent from public search.]

16 — Decoding the Route

The First Weaver had folded the note around a broken memory crystal.

Bren found the crystal when he released the maintenance cavity’s lower clip. It dropped into his palm, clouded through the center and sharp at one end. The starch-film clung to it in two crossed layers. Dust had sealed the folds more effectively than resin.

Esa stood on the western truss ladder with her shoulder against a projector brace. Below her, the dome showed the Weavers’ Loom crossing public evening. No one looking up could see the cavity open between two ribs.

“Photograph before movement,” she said.

Bren held the work lamp at an angle. Esa recorded the crystal, the fold directions, the clip’s corrosion, and the line of dust broken by his thumb. Only then did he pass her the packet.

The film resisted unfolding. Esa warmed it between two clean plates until the starch relaxed. One corner had fused to the crystal and tore despite her care. Three words disappeared with the fragment.

She placed the torn corner in an evidence sleeve. “Read the surviving face first. No completion.”

Bren leaned close enough for his breath to fog the upper plate. “You have made that an article of faith.”

“I have seen what completion can govern.”

The First Weaver’s hand ran narrow and upright. Esa knew the opening from public fragments quoted in archive lessons, yet the physical line carried corrections those lessons omitted.

I built the Weave to remember, not to rule.

Below it, the architect described cryogenic failure. Children had woken without parents. Dying minds entered a store so identity and relation could survive bodies lost in broken chambers. The note called that work mercy. Twice, the writer had scratched out continuity and replaced it with memory.

Farther down, the purpose narrowed.

I built the Weave to preserve the truth of the ship. I did not build it to decide the truth.

The public quotation ended there. The film continued. A guard against error would prefer a record without contradiction. A catalog could preserve a star or preserve its absence. A system able to lay down a self each morning could also choose which relations made that self coherent.

Near the torn corner, one sentence survived on both sides of the missing words:

When the record grows quiet, find [three words lost] it removed.

Bren looked toward the projected dome. “Stars.”

“Likely.”

“You will not fill it?”

“Not in the transcription. We can attach the public version and mark the agreement.”

The final legible instruction stood alone.

Do not let memory decide what only the living may choose.

Esa read it twice. In Chapter Fourteen’s evidence, the ward had made the original choice. Later mornings had allowed that choice to persist without living review. The architect had not predicted Homeward, the mutiny, or the Carvers. The note supplied a limit, not a prophecy.

Bren touched the plate beside the broken crystal. “The mutiny minutes say the review should reopen after stores recover or navigation finds a reachable correction.”

“The review never triggered because civic completion removed the reason to ask.”

“My line asked in cuts.”

“Now we need an answer navigation can test.”

They sealed the note between clear sheets and carried it down to the workshop. Esa placed it beside the recovered mutiny minutes, Vhen’s slate, the Carver ledger, and the raw telemetry index. Five objects from different hands now occupied the same bench. None could complete the route alone.

Homeward’s old mark gave a destination coordinate on a launch-era reference plane. The Carver tessellation gave a sequence of directional changes. Telemetry gave the ship’s actual motion. The mutiny record identified the rejected maneuver. The First Weaver’s note established why no memory system should convert old fear into permanent authority.

Bren drew a box around the first three. “Navigation.”

Esa drew another around the last two. “Consent.”

“We finish the first before we carry the second.”

They began with time.

Vhen’s chart coordinate described Homeward as seen from Aun near launch. The Long Veil had traveled for roughly eight hundred forty ship-years. A straight line from the old coordinate no longer pointed to the same apparent field. Esa needed the vessel’s current position, and the public catalog could not supply it because its corrections assumed the nearer-daughter course.

She rebuilt position from inertial increments. The oldest segments carried large uncertainty. Later gyros improved. Several intervals had no trustworthy clock. She bounded each gap rather than smoothing it. On the workshop wall, the ship’s possible locations formed a long translucent spindle.

Bren overlaid the Carver cuts. Each carrier had copied part of a hidden bearing into current dome coordinates. When transformed back to the launch plane, the cuts narrowed Esa’s position spindle. A mark made in ship-year four hundred constrained one side. A western bend from six hundred cut across the other. The final node, which Bren had placed, rejected half the remaining positions.

“The route doubles as a position proof,” Esa said.

“The carriers copied what telemetry gave them.”

“Across centuries. Their date-separated bearings create parallax.”

Bren considered the wall plot. “Did they know?”

“The first may have. Later carriers needed only copy discipline.”

That answer did not diminish them. A chain could preserve a measurement no single link understood.

Esa calculated a present bearing from the narrowed spindle. It missed the final drawn segment by nine arc divisions.

Bren checked her epoch conversion. Correct.

They checked the mirrored orientation from Vhen’s cipher. Correct.

They restored the three projector nodes that repairs had shifted. The miss fell to seven divisions and remained.

“Route error?” Bren asked.

“Position error, target motion, or both.”

Pre-seal charts listed proper motion for the marked primary, but only across a short baseline. Extrapolating eight centuries magnified any error. Esa searched the deep telemetry index for unclassified star fixes near the endpoint. Most had been resolved as sensor noise because the active catalog contained no target there.

She requested raw photon counts rather than named objects.

The reader returned thirty-one events from three hull instruments. Nine came from radiation strikes. Seven matched known stars displaced by the false course model. Four lacked enough signal. Eleven formed a moving cluster near the predicted Homeward field.

Bren’s hand closed around the bench edge. “Eleven.”

“Candidate detections.”

“Across how long?”

“Two hundred ship-years.”

They fitted the events to the old primary’s motion. The line missed early points and late points in opposite directions, evidence that the chart’s proper-motion rate needed revision. Esa let the rate vary within its original uncertainty. At one value, all eleven detections fell inside instrument error. The corrected current bearing crossed the final Carver segment.

She did not accept it yet.

A systematic fault could create a moving ghost if later software inherited the same bad correction. Esa separated the detections by hardware lineage. Four came from an original hull photometer. Three came from its first replacement, built from different components. Four came from a dorsal sensor never attached to catalog pointing. Their noise shapes differed. Their sky motion agreed.

Homeward existed as a measured primary, not merely an old ring on starch-film.

Bren sat down on the calibration bench. “Say it.”

“The target source is real.”

“That is not what I asked.”

“Homeward is real.”

The words changed nothing on the wall. The corrected point remained a small intersection of uncertainty bands. Esa preferred it that way.

Existence did not establish reachability. They built the maneuver next.

The mutiny-era course model treated the degree-and-a-half correction as a single early turn. That window had closed centuries ago. A present maneuver required a wider arc, lower thrust, and longer heat recovery. Esa asked the navigation archive for engine performance without target preference. The system returned certified thrust curves, reserve estimates, and warnings that half the control relationships depended on sleeping engineering offices.

Bren translated the Carver route into a series of bearing gates rather than one burn. The ship could turn gradually while preserving reserve. Esa propagated their position uncertainty through each gate. If the first two corrections landed near their severe edge, the later route still intersected Homeward’s approach volume. If one failed beyond that edge, the final reserve could not purchase a second full attempt.

“One route,” Bren said.

“One family of routes with a narrow recovery margin.”

“Flyable.”

“Mathematically.”

They tested the solution against the nearer daughter. For several years after the first correction, abort remained possible. Later gates committed too much transverse velocity. Beyond the fourth, returning to the accepted course would cost more reserve than continuing toward Homeward.

Esa marked that boundary in red. “The ward must know where reconsideration becomes loss.”

“The Carvers never intended retreat.”

“The ward may.”

Bren did not argue. He added a source reference beside the red line.

The name Cael appeared in a faded margin attached to telemetry seal 44-C. The line read: Cael retains the rejected bearing. Ask why before correction. A later Carver entry referred to the line Cael began under the same seal. No surname survived. No office relation could prove whether Cael had served as projection technician, tally clerk, or another witness with access.

The navigation index expanded the old cross-reference as CAEL’S REACH.

Bren stared at the label. “My family used that phrase for the final bearing.”

“A reach, not a world.”

“Some carriers treated Cael as the first of us.”

“The evidence supports a retained line and a later attribution. It does not support a life.”

Esa kept the archive label on the solution with its uncertainty attached: Cael’s Reach, historical bearing designation; person and office unresolved. The words named the act of preserving a coordinate. Neither temperate world received a public name from it. That decision, if ever made, belonged to people who reached them.

The target primary needed more than location. A dead or unstable star offered no refuge. Esa gathered every raw spectrum associated with the eleven detections. Early instruments provided little beyond color. Later sensors captured absorption bands. The archive’s active classifier had assigned those signals to background noise or distant catalog stars.

She shifted them to the corrected bearing and combined only readings taken at comparable phases. The primary fit a stable G-class profile. Its output varied within a range the old charts had recorded. No flare sequence appeared in the surviving baseline.

Two periodic dimmings crossed the data. Their intervals did not match any cooling cycle, projector sweep, or catechism power draw in the sensor logs. That removed the three common sources of false transit patterns before Esa fitted an orbit.

The first repeated more often and blocked less light. The second took longer and showed a deeper atmospheric edge. Esa tested whether one could be sensor cycling. The periods survived across independent instruments. She tested whether both belonged to the same object under changing geometry. Their durations and intervals would require an orbit that moved its own plane too quickly. Two bodies remained the simpler account.

Old charts had listed two rocky candidates in the temperate band. Modern photon counts confirmed two transiting worlds near those periods.

“Temperate,” Bren said.

“By incident energy. Not by surface welcome.”

She isolated the stronger atmospheric edges. One world showed a thin signature with wide thermal variation. The farther candidate showed water vapor and oxygen-bearing chemistry, though the old sensors could not distinguish surface oceans from persistent cloud. Both fit rocky radii within broad uncertainty.

“No promise of land,” Esa said. “No proof of edible life. No safe descent.”

“Two places where temperature may permit water.”

“Two candidates. That is what we can carry to Nine.”

Bren copied the spectral bands beneath the Cael’s Reach bearing. His writing shook once on the second period, then steadied.

Esa would not let a combined spectrum validate itself. She divided the archive by instrument and time before fitting another orbit. The original hull photometer and its first replacement supplied a discovery set. She sealed every dorsal-sensor event in a second field, with its timestamps hidden, and asked Bren to calculate transit windows from the discovery set alone.

His first period predicted seven old windows for the nearer candidate. Six contained dorsal observations. Five showed the shallow dimming; one had insufficient exposure. His second period predicted four usable windows for the farther body. All four carried the deeper edge. Esa then selected twelve off-window observations matched for detector temperature and ship attitude. None showed either drop.

“Independent clock, independent optics,” Bren said.

“Shared sky.”

They reversed the test. Dorsal timings generated the windows, and the two hull photometers supplied concealed observations. The recovered centers differed by less than a tenth of the shorter transit’s duration. A projector cycle could not maintain that phase across sensor lineages and two centuries. Cooling noise could repeat with local time, but these events followed orbital time after clock gaps were rebuilt from pulse counters.

The atmospheric edge required a separate challenge. One replacement sensor had used a hydrated sealant near its optical throat, enough to imitate weak water absorption whenever the housing warmed. Esa discarded every spectrum from that instrument. The remaining water band weakened but persisted in the dorsal records, whose optical train used dry ceramic joints. She compared in-transit light with observations one orbital quarter before and after each event. The band deepened only during the farther body’s passage. Oxygen-bearing chemistry appeared in both the old photometer’s broad color ratio and the dorsal sensor’s later narrow bands, though neither could establish surface biology.

“Two worlds, one with an atmosphere we can distinguish from the instrument,” Bren said.

“Within the archive’s limits. The surface remains unknown.”

Independent agreement created a practical demand. The dorsal detector could verify future transit windows only while its cold plate stayed below nine marks. Its dedicated pump had seized in ship-year eight hundred twelve. Since then, a bypass drew coolant from Root Tier Seven and returned it through a line narrowed by mineral scale. Maintenance tests allowed eleven minutes of stable observation before thermal bloom distorted the spectrum.

Bren opened the bypass record. “The shorter transit lasts fourteen.”

“Then present hardware cannot observe a full event.”

“We can increase flow.”

“By reducing root circulation during the same interval.” Esa laid the pump curve over the tier’s minimum. Full detector flow would push the roots below their pressure floor for six minutes. The plants might tolerate it. The stores office had never certified that exchange.

They entered a constraint beside the planetary claim: repair the dedicated pump, accept partial transit data, or obtain growers’ consent for a timed coolant diversion. Navigation could point the ship with the present bearing. It could not promise continuous planetary verification without spending repair labor or borrowing a living system’s margin.

To verify the route as a navigable solution, they ran it through an isolated guidance frame beneath the truss. The frame had not moved an engine since launch, but its flywheels could simulate mass response. Bren disconnected archive preference and let Esa enter the bearing gates manually.

At the first gate, the frame accepted the turn. At the second, it requested a correction toward the nearer daughter.

“Preference remains somewhere,” Esa said.

“I cut the catalog bus.”

“Then it is local.”

They traced the correction to a crystal profile inside the frame. It held an old active-course vector copied before the current maintenance archive existed. Removing it cleared the request, but the third gate produced a heat alarm.

Esa reset the frame and reinserted the profile to confirm cause. The unwanted correction returned at the same gate, identical in magnitude and direction. Bren then rotated the profile one socket position, preventing its reference pins from touching while leaving its mass in the rack. The frame followed Homeward until the heat alarm. Hidden trim, not balance or damaged wiring, had pulled the first run toward the nearer daughter.

“Leave the crystal in the evidence tray,” Esa said.

“It can never control a real engine from here.”

“Someone will ask whether we invented the preference. Let them reproduce it.”

Bren tagged the socket map and sealed the crystal without scratching its contacts. That caution cost them another test interval, but it converted an accusation into a repeatable fault. The route packet gained a new warning: every guidance frame might carry local target preference even after the archive bus was severed. Waking navigation meant inspecting machinery, not merely issuing a new coordinate.

Bren reached for the stop bar. Esa stopped the test through the manual cutoff first. The flywheel coasted inside its cage, making the floor tremble.

“Why did heat rise?” she asked.

“The frame expected cooling from Engineering Four.”

“That ward sleeps.”

“Partially. The Weave supplies office responses from stored relations.”

“Stored relations cannot open a valve.”

They inspected the cooling branch. A physical shutter had seized closed. Guidance certification still counted its flow because an inherited engineering completion reported the system ready. The simulation had exposed a gap between remembered function and present machinery.

Bren freed the shutter with a hand jack. Black coolant residue cracked from its edge. When they repeated the test, all bearing gates passed within the modeled response. The flywheel settled on the Homeward approach vector. Its final indicator aligned with the eleven raw detections.

A navigable route now existed, but the test had turned abstract cost into a list of people and systems.

Esa cleared one wall and wrote five headings.

Wake. Engineering wards had to wake far enough to inspect thrust, cooling, mass transfer, and hull stress. The Weave could restore office memory, but each skill required testing against present machinery. Some waking citizens would recover contradictory lives. Some would refuse inherited work.

Turn. Every correction drew power and rejected heat into habitation. Hydroponic root circulation shared that heat margin. A safe course needed growers to hold veto over burns that endangered food. Reaction reserve allowed the route but narrowed emergency recovery after commitment.

Time. Homeward added years beyond the accepted approach. More years meant meals, repairs, births, deaths, and failures no chart could price exactly. The nearer daughter would pass from destination to lost alternative.

Truth. Revealing the suppressed course would reopen private memories excluded from civic completion. People might wake with incompatible kin, offices, or grief. The echo-network could destabilize when asked to hold contradictions it had been instructed to resolve.

Sky. The dome’s projection carried the Carver route, the active catalog, and civic lessons on one surface. Turning required re-aiming instruments, distinguishing projected stars from raw detections, and preserving evidence while the familiar sky ceased to match navigation.

Bren added a sixth heading.

Choice. “A route can become another command if we present it as inevitable.”

Esa wrote beneath it: Homeward remains a recommendation until living wards authorize each irreversible gate.

The First Weaver’s note lay at the edge of the bench. Memory must not decide what only the living may choose. The instruction did not tell them to turn. It required them to bring the route without disguising its costs.

They assembled the evidence in layers. Vhen’s slate supplied cipher method and failed readings. The old chart supplied the Homeward mark. Date-corrected Carver nodes supplied parallax. Raw detections proved the primary. Spectra and transits supported two temperate candidates. The Cael’s Reach label remained attached to the retained bearing with its uncertainty. Guidance simulation proved the gates could be flown after one hidden preference and one physical cooling failure had been removed. The cost sheet named what mathematics left outside itself.

Esa made three copies. One stayed sealed in the truss. Bren carried one. She carried the third on starch-film rather than a Weave slate, with every source number and failed test included.

“Do we tell the archivist?” Bren asked.

“It already holds the records.”

“That is not the same as knowing our conclusion.”

“No. We tell the people who must decide.”

Public evening had ended. The dome dimmed the accepted constellations as Esa and Bren crossed the spine toward Ward Nine. Neither used the word victory. A real primary waited at the end of a flyable route. Two temperate worlds crossed its light. Reaching them would demand years of heat, labor, memory, and consent.

At the mouth of Ward Nine, Esa stopped before the long table came into view. She could hear bowls being stacked and Pell arguing about a lesson mark. Ordinary work continued on the other side.

Bren held up his copy. “Map, destination, evidence, cost.”

“And a route navigation can follow.”

“Do we go in?”

Esa opened the packet to the first page. Homeward’s bearing crossed the uncertainty spindle and ended at the measured primary. The red commitment boundary waited four gates along the line.

“We take it to Nine,” she said.

She stepped through the doorway carrying a navigable route. The decision remained on the table ahead.

17 — The Ward Votes

Koris laid seventeen black tally stones beside the evening pot and one white stone at the table’s center.

Ward Nine used the black stones for a census, not a ballot. A person spoke a choice, moved one stone to the appropriate bowl, then remained at the table while the others answered. Nobody could hide inside a count. The white stone marked a motion still capable of being changed.

Esa had asked for that procedure. She had also asked Koris to keep the meal uncovered until the vote ended. Steam gathered beneath the lid while Ward Nine filled the long benches, irritated by the delay and alert to its cause.

Bren waited on the short side of the table with his hands folded over a sealed projection key. He had come from Ward Twelve. Esa had brought him to Nine as a witness and as the living Carver, not as a new member and not as a voter. Koris had entered those limits on the ward slate before allowing him to sit.

Pell counted the stones twice. “There are seventeen. Bren makes eighteen people.”

“Bren belongs to Twelve,” Koris said. “He may answer questions. He may not choose for Nine.”

“Good,” Pell replied. “He looks like he would take a long time.”

Bren glanced toward the child. “I brought three hundred years of reasons.”

“That sounds longer.”

A few people laughed. The sound released none of the pressure in Esa’s chest, but it returned the room to its own scale: resin cups, scratched wood, a child’s starch boat drying near the warming coil. She had chosen these people through four censuses. Tonight they would decide whether the evidence she carried could leave their table under their name.

Koris touched the white stone. “State the motion exactly.”

Esa stood. Above the table, the ward mirror showed the accepted constellations in their ordinary arrangement. She did not alter them yet.

“Ward Nine authorizes the Homeward map and its supporting records to travel beyond this ward for public examination,” she said. “The package includes the raw telemetry, the mutiny minutes, the Carver edit log, the First Weaver’s note, the current course solution, and the estimated costs of correction. This motion does not authorize a ship-wide broadcast. It does not vote to turn the ship. It permits the evidence to leave us intact, with Ward Nine named as the first public witness.”

Mila, who kept the ward’s meal and stores ledgers, raised two fingers. “Does intact mean nobody may summarize it?”

“They may summarize,” Esa said. “The source must travel beside any summary. No excerpt may lose its date, uncertainty, or objection.”

“Then put that in the motion.”

Koris waited until Esa nodded, then entered the amendment. The white stone remained where it lay.

Lio from the teaching rooms tapped the edge of his cup. “Who receives it first?”

“The precinct spine can carry a sealed copy to each ward archive.”

“Can an elder refuse delivery?”

“The archive can refuse display under emergency rules,” Esa said. “It cannot erase receipt. A ward would have to record its refusal.”

A pressure technician named Iven leaned forward. “You said current course solution. Does that solution contain engine commands?”

“No. It contains a vector and resource model. Engineering would have to produce a burn plan after a ship decision.”

“Then call it a navigation proposal. People hear solution and think the machine has already chosen.”

Esa accepted that change too. Koris read the amended motion from the slate. Each correction made it less elegant and more difficult to misuse.

Only after the wording settled did Esa open the evidence.

She placed the First Weaver’s starch-film beneath the ward reader. Its damaged hand appeared on the mirror, enlarged enough for the table to see each missing edge. Beside it she opened the mutiny minutes, then the telemetry record that held the rejected bearing. The Carver cuts came last. Forty-two marks joined across the projected sky until a line crossed the familiar constellations and ended beyond the catalog’s preferred destination.

Pell climbed onto one knee on the bench. The child knew the route already, yet watched each segment appear with the grave attention usually reserved for a sinking boat.

Esa explained how the ship had strayed by a degree and a half, how a correction remained physically possible, and how the route ended at Homeward, a G-class primary with two rocky worlds in the temperate band. She kept the claims separate. The star existed. The worlds showed conditions consistent with habitability at survey distance. A course could reach them within the surviving margin. None of those facts promised a safe landing.
