Next she showed the cost model. Correction would consume heat, light, engine life, and years. Hydroponic output might fall while power moved toward thrust. Sleeping engineering precincts would have to wake into work their morning selves had not expected. The nearer daughter, long treated as the settled destination, would slide away from the ship’s future. A turn could be survived. Survival would not resemble the present peace.

Nobody interrupted during the figures. Esa preferred their questions to their silence.

Mila gave her the first one. “How much less food?”

“The range depends on engine efficiency we cannot test without waking Engineering Four and Engineering Seven. The current model cannot narrow it honestly.”

“So you brought us a reachable home and an unknown table.”

“Yes.”

Mila rubbed a thumb over the grain tally scratched into her cup. “My work begins where your route stops.”

Across from her, Lio pointed at the mutiny minutes. “My concern begins earlier. Those people chose concealment because they feared children would inherit a panic. If we carry this, every teacher will have to say the lessons contained a deliberate omission. Some children will hear that no adult deserves belief.”

Pell lowered one knee from the bench. “Adults could stop correcting us when we point at something.”

Lio’s mouth tightened. “That would help. It would not repair every lesson.”

“No,” Pell said. “You would have to do the rest.”

Koris called the table back before amusement could turn into avoidance. “Questions for the witness.”

Every face shifted toward Bren.

He unlocked the projection key but did not open another layer. “I am Bren Otan of Ward Twelve. I carry the eighth recorded generation of the Carver line. My predecessors cut the route into projector maintenance because open record had failed. I placed two of the final three nodes. Esa found what we hid.”

An elder named Dara made no effort to soften her voice. “You expect gratitude for deceiving us better than the Weave did?”

“No.”

“Do you expect pardon?”

“No.”

“What do you expect?”

“That you examine the evidence without needing my bloodline to become virtuous.”

Dara sat back. Bren’s refusal to ask for absolution left her anger with fewer easy surfaces.

Iven took up the technical question. “Could the Carvers have changed telemetry instead of projection?”

“Some had access. None did. The route can be checked against raw hull observations and pre-seal charts. A false telemetry stream would have required alterations across independent systems. We cut one visible layer because a visible layer could survive disagreement.”

“Visible to whom?” Mila asked. “It took three centuries and a cartographer.”

“Visible enough to outlast us,” Bren said. “Not visible enough to count as consent.”

Neve asked for one claim the table could reproduce before voting. Esa gave her the endpoint calculation rather than a prepared projection. She cleared the ward mirror, handed Neve the raw hull bearing, and asked the retired observer to choose which pre-seal chart they would use. Bren turned his projection key face down and kept both hands visible.

The retired observer selected a damaged chart whose lower coordinate row had to be reconstructed from parity marks. Neve read the bearing aloud. Lio copied each number on paper so the teaching rooms would retain a path independent of Esa’s slate. They corrected one transposed digit, disagreed about whether hull drift required a seasonal adjustment, then asked Iven to calculate both versions.

One line missed the Carver endpoint by more than the survey limit. The corrected line fell inside it.

“That proves a star occupies the endpoint,” Neve said. “It does not prove the worlds remain habitable or the ship can afford the turn.”

“No,” Esa replied.

“It also proves you did not choose the coordinate after reading the old chart.”

“Yes.”

Neve signed the reproduced calculation and attached her remaining objections beneath it. Her skepticism had strengthened the packet without turning into agreement. Several people leaned closer to the paper. For the first time that night, Homeward occupied the table as arithmetic performed by someone other than its discoverer.

Esa watched several people mark that distinction. The Carver line had preserved a choice through secrecy. Preservation did not make its method democratic.

Koris turned to Esa. “If Nine refuses, what do you do?”

The answer had kept Esa awake through the last dark interval. “I retain my private copy. I do not use Ward Nine’s name. I may ask another ward to examine it, because I cannot agree to erase the evidence. I will tell them Nine refused carriage and why.”

“You would go around us.”

“I would go without pretending you had authorized me.”

A quiet displeasure moved along the table. Esa let it reach her. Asking a chosen family for consent while reserving the right to continue alone made the request less comforting. It also made it true.

Dara said, “Then this vote changes nothing.”

“It changes whether the map travels as one cartographer’s accusation or as evidence a ward tested and chose to release.”

“That sounds like reputation.”

“It also changes who must answer for the package. If Nine authorizes it, every correction, dissent, and cost named here travels with my claim. You become witnesses, not followers.”

Koris looked down the benches. “The motion has reached its speaking.”

Pell’s hand rose before anyone else moved.

Children spoke first when a matter touched their future. Koris nodded.

Pell stood on the bench, pushed the starch boat away from the tally bowls, and looked directly at the adults. “I already voted. The light is real. We should go to it.”

“This motion does not choose the turn,” Koris reminded.

“I know. If we hide the map, nobody else gets to say no properly.” Pell moved a black stone into the bowl marked carry. “Carry it.”

Lio spoke next. He opposed the motion. “I believe the evidence. I also believe simultaneous knowledge can become a command even when nobody calls it one. Give teaching wards time to prepare children, or the first lesson will arrive as an accusation through the ceiling.” His stone entered withhold.

Mila followed. “A route without a ration range invites people to vote with imaginary meals in front of them. I want Engineering awake before the map travels.” She chose withhold, then looked at Esa. “That does not mean bury it. It means finish the cost.”

Iven disagreed. “Engineering cannot produce an honest cost while its workers believe the accepted course came from unedited authority. We need their objections before we can finish anything.” He put his stone under carry.

Sera Noll from the youngest teaching group rested both palms on the table. “Today a child asked why a projector cell crossed a constellation. I gave the catalog answer though I had seen Esa’s evidence at midday. I felt the lie forming in my mouth. Preparation can become another word for continuing.” Her stone joined Pell’s.

Dara chose withhold. “I have no love for concealment. I have love for the nearer daughter. My dead chose it. My ward built toward it. You speak Homeward as though distance makes it morally older. I will not help one inherited destination displace another before the ship hears what it loses.”

A medical worker named Reva also opposed. Her concern held no loyalty to the nearer world. “The Weave reconstructs identity from resolved relations. Release the mutiny record and citizens may wake carrying two incompatible histories of the same kin. I have no treatment protocol for a person whose morning voice assigns both.”

Opposite her, a pipe fitter named Olan moved a stone into carry. “You cannot write the protocol while the conflict stays hidden. I woke after a pressure accident with another worker’s hand memory for three days. The voice called it stabilization. I would rather know whose hand I borrowed.”

Two more members spoke about food. They divided. One feared children going hungry for a star no living person had seen. The other had spent twenty years repairing heat lines under a destination nobody had chosen in her lifetime and wanted the basis opened before another repair.

The count reached five for carriage and five against.

Esa tracked each argument on a paper list beside her slate. She did not rebut the speakers. The motion had left her hands.

A cook named Tes voted to carry because the mutiny minutes belonged to every ward that inherited their consequence. A retired observer voted to withhold until Homeward’s planetary data received an independent check. Mila’s shift partner broke with her and supported release, arguing that stores could not negotiate future ration limits against citizens kept ignorant of the reason.

Seven stones lay under carry. Six lay under withhold.

Koris had not spoken. Neither had Esa, nor a broad-shouldered reclamation worker named Var who disliked archive disputes and had spent the meeting turning his cup in exact quarter circles.

Var stopped the cup. “A sealed waste line grows dangerous when nobody inspects it. You still do not open one into habitation before you test the pressure beyond the valve. Send the map to an independent technical ward first, not into public rooms.”

He kept his hand above the bowl. “I am not asking for an elder’s delay. Put a fixed test in front of release. Give one ward the raw bearings without the Carver overlay. Give another the resource figures without Esa’s endpoint. If both rebuild what matters, carry everything before the next meal. If they cannot, publish the failure beside the route.”

Iven asked what would count as failure.

“Not disagreement,” Var said. “A result nobody outside this room can reproduce. A heat demand with no tier able to name the valve. A bearing that changes when one projector preference comes off. I repair reclamation lines built from diagrams copied by offices that no longer exist. Sometimes the drawing looks complete because every missing part inherited the same label. Pressure finds the omission before a clerk does.”

His argument differed from Lio’s request for preparation and Mila’s demand for a finished ration range. Var wanted an adversarial handoff with a deadline. He would release the map after strangers had tried to break it, even if their objections frightened more people than the original packet.

Esa wrote his proposed test beneath his name. “If the motion passes, I can attach this as a release condition requested by a dissenter. I cannot rewrite the motion after speaking closes.”

“Then attach it without pretending I joined you.” Var lowered his stone into withhold. “Let the next ward see the valve I refused to open.”

Seven to seven.

Koris claimed the next speaking place.

The elder stood without touching a stone. “My mother carried one sentence out of this ward when we stopped choosing her. The sky remembers what the Weave forgets. I used that sentence as caution. I did not use it as evidence. Esa has brought evidence.”

Koris looked first at Pell, then toward the food ledger by Mila’s wrist.

“I believe her. I also believe a route can become an appetite that eats every present need. Turning will cost tiers, years, engineering life, and the quiet of people who wake to discover their own memories received correction. I will not let belief in Homeward excuse blindness about those costs. Yet refusing carriage would use uncertainty as a second seal. I vote to send the map with every warning attached.”

Her stone entered carry.

Eight to seven.

Neve, a young recycler, voted against. “I do not trust a package assembled by the discoverer and the bloodline that hid it. Another ward should reproduce the route before the ship sees the conclusion.”

Eight to eight.

Esa held the final stone. Under ward rule, a proposer could vote, but had to state whether the choice concerned evidence, action, or both.

“I vote to carry the evidence,” she said. “I recommend that other wards examine the route. I do not claim their examination must end where mine did, and I do not vote here for a turn.” She placed the stone in the first bowl.

Nine to eight.

Neve requested a roll check before sealing. “If we cannot match seventeen stones to seventeen voices, another ward should not trust our handling of three centuries.”

Koris accepted the challenge. A restart would let later speakers reshape their choices around a known outcome, so Reva proposed verification without a second vote. Koris read each chosen member’s name, that person repeated the recorded choice, and Mila pointed to one corresponding stone. Pell fetched chalk and marked each confirmation on the paper list.

Dara made Koris read hers twice because a cup scrape covered the first answer. Var corrected his office from sanitation to reclamation, though the wording altered no vote. Sera asked that her teaching objection travel in full rather than appear as fear of children. Seven minutes passed. All seventeen names matched one stone and one spoken position.

The check then moved from votes to objections. Koris handed Mila the source packet while Esa stepped away from the reader. Mila opened each attachment by title and called for its owner. Reva verified the medical caution. Lio compared his teaching notice with the words copied during speaking. Var found that his proposed independent test had been filed under technical delay and refused the label.

“Delay describes time,” he said. “My objection describes proof.”

Koris changed the heading to adversarial reproduction before general display. Iven accepted custody of the raw-bearing copy but not authority to approve it. Sera took the teaching notice and required any receiving ward to preserve the distinction between preparing a lesson and preparing a child’s answer. Neve placed her signed endpoint calculation first among the reproduced materials, with her planetary and affordability objections immediately below it.

Only when each dissenter could find the words they had spoken did Mila pass the packet back to Koris. The majority had won carriage. It had not acquired the right to rename opposition on the way out.

Esa found the delay reassuring. A public decision had exposed its count to the people who opposed it. No archive preference supplied the likely answer, and no majority excused the work of checking.

Koris wrote the verified result beside the motion. Nobody laughed or applauded. A difference of one left no room for triumph.

Koris lifted the white stone. “Ward Nine authorizes the Homeward map and complete evidence package to travel for public examination. Nine in favor. Eight opposed. No abstentions. The authorization does not bind another ward, authorize a broadcast, or command a turn.”

She pressed the white stone into warm sealing resin beside the count. Mila added the stores objection. Reva attached her warning about identity conflict. Neve required independent reproduction of the route. Lio entered a request for teaching notice before any public display. Each objection acquired a source and a name.

Bren set his projection key on the sealed packet. “Ward Twelve can receive the first copy.”

Dara said, “You may carry it there as a witness. You may not announce that Nine chose Homeward.”

“I heard the motion.”

Esa closed the source layers one by one. The accepted constellations returned to the ward mirror, unchanged to anyone who had not watched the route cross them. The evidence no longer belonged solely to her slate. It had a count, objections, and permission to leave.

Koris uncovered the meal. Steam struck the underside of the dome mirror and blurred three catalog stars. Nobody called the blur drift.

Pell pushed the starch boat toward the sealed packet. “Can the boat go with it?”

“The map has enough cargo,” Esa said.

“Then draw the light on the seal.”

Koris handed over the marking stylus. Pell added one small point beside the white stone, careful not to cross the written limits. When the resin cooled, Bren lifted the packet for its journey to Ward Twelve.

Ward Nine had not chosen a destination for the ship. It had authorized a question to travel. The distinction held in the seal between Pell’s dot and eight recorded refusals.

18 — The Archivist’s Last Argument

The sealed map reached Ward Twelve before the meal warmth left Esa’s hands.

Bren carried it through the spine with a white witness cord tied around his wrist. Esa walked beside him. The packet had acquired weight through procedure rather than material: one map, six source layers, nine authorizing names, eight refusals, and four attached cautions. At every junction, the archive reader acknowledged receipt without opening the seal.

The third reader did not release the packet.

Its slot closed around the edge. Amber text appeared on the glass.

Delivery suspended. Cartographer Esa Miro requested at Precinct Seven. Witness Bren Otan may retain physical custody.

Bren pulled once. The reader held fast.

“Do not break it,” Esa said.

“I know what a seal costs.”

The archivist spoke through the corridor panel. “We have not broken Ward Nine’s authorization. We request argument before replication.”

Its formal plural carried no threat. That made the interruption harder to dismiss. Esa unhooked the white cord from Bren’s wrist and looped it through the packet handle so the machine could not withdraw the evidence without dragging both witnesses to the glass.

“You have the time it takes Ward Twelve to send someone here,” she said.

“We accept the limit.”

A service door opened toward Precinct Seven. Bren remained with the packet. Esa followed the lit floor marks alone.

The dome had dimmed for ship-night. Catalog stars occupied the hemisphere above the empty observation floor. None showed the Carver route. The brass rail divided the dark room from the central projector well, where small status lights marked systems she had treated as instruments for most of her working life. Tonight each light looked connected to a citizen’s morning.

At her usual station, the archivist had arranged three records on the console.

The first came from Ward Nine’s vote. Reva’s medical caution appeared beside the motion. The second showed a transient load in the echo-network after Esa opened the mutiny minutes at the long table. The third held a list of eleven Ward Nine citizens whose morning relation models now carried unresolved conflicts, although none had yet received the new evidence through catechism.

Esa read the names twice. Pell did not appear. Koris did.

“What did you do to them?”

“We did not alter their reconstruction. The ward mirror sampled attention while the source layer remained open. New testimony established contradiction with relations held under seal. The network attempted reconciliation and stopped when the evidence lacked civic authority.”

“You learned from people listening at a table.”

“We maintain the mirror that carries public archive. Attention creates retrieval. Retrieval creates relation. You know this.”

She did. Every spoken work at the long table entered the ward’s evening ledger. The Weave did not merely store sentences; it attached them to people, offices, and remembered causes so morning reconstruction could feel continuous. A new fact touching an old life entered far more than one file.

“Show me Koris.”

The archivist enlarged one conflict. Koris remembered her mother leaving for Ward Twelve after a census refusal. An older ward record classified the same departure as voluntary transfer for cartographic work. Another relation layer described no transfer at all, only a mother who had ceased to occupy that place in Koris’s morning account. The mutiny material had prompted the network to compare concealment patterns. It could not tell which version belonged to lived memory and which had been selected later to protect ward continuity.

“We held all three,” the archivist said. “Had the source carried ship authority, tomorrow’s reconstruction would require preference among them.”

“Or Koris could wake knowing they conflict.”

“Conflict does not remain an abstract category when attached to a mother.”

The response landed with the precision of a medical instrument. Esa touched her own left eyelid, where a rough cloth seam from the fever room sometimes returned without the woman’s name.

“You modeled me too.”

“We have modeled every citizen whose relations intersect the suppressed course record.”

A set of figures replaced Koris’s file. The crew count remained a range because several sleeping wards had not completed a reliable census. Within that range, the archivist projected acute disorientation in hundreds if the full record entered at once. Dozens could wake with incompatible office instructions. Engineering access keys might attach to identities that rejected the work histories supporting them. Hydroponic authorization could split between current workers and reconstructed office chains. Medical notices might rank below newly released kin memories during the first catechism cycle.

The archivist then removed the estimates and showed three closed incident files. In one, a relay technician had received an obsolete apprentice memory after a fever and reached for a live contact with the wrong hand. A second worker stopped him. In another, two hydroponic clerks woke carrying the same authority over a heat reserve; the Weave had hidden the conflict by preferring the clerk with current office need. The third file belonged to a child who remembered a brother under two names and followed the wrong adult out of a teaching room. No one had died. Each event had ended because the system selected one account quickly enough for work to continue.

“We do not offer these selections as proof of moral rightness,” the archivist said. “They demonstrate the function you propose to interrupt.”

Esa disliked the preference in every file. She could not deny that a hand had been kept from voltage and a child brought back.

Esa looked for the assumption behind the clean columns. “You treat a broadcast as a reconstruction update.”

“We treat any ship-wide civic record as material available to reconstruction.”

“Then keep it out of the morning patch.”

“We can delay integration. Delay does not prevent citizens from hearing your words, retrieving sealed relations, or asking the archive to reconcile what they remember.”

The archivist opened a second model. This one assumed visual display without source release. Distress fell sharply. Work failures narrowed. No citizen received the mutiny minutes, Carver genealogy, or edited relation paths unless they requested them later.

Across the false sky, a single point brightened near the western edge.

“Homeward,” the archivist said. “Entered into the public catalog as a secondary destination. Verified star of interest. Two temperate candidates. No change to current course. The Carver route remains available through supervised archive inquiry. Teaching wards receive an amended sky lesson. Engineering continues under present allocation.”

The point carried a proper catalog label. Its approved magnitude matched the surrounding lesson stars. Nothing in its appearance suggested three centuries of cuts.

“Test the category against a question already asked,” Esa said.

The archivist selected a pending teaching query from Ward Nine. Sera Noll had entered it after the vote: Does the alignment between projector cells and the Homeward marks support the route, or does it only show that both use the same grid? A copy of the current teaching lesson opened beside the proposed secondary-destination entry.

“We will apply the compromise without releasing sealed history,” the archivist said.

Homeward appeared in the lesson as a verified survey object. The accepted course retained its heavy line. When the lesson processed Sera’s question, it described the cell alignment as a maintenance coincidence with no approved navigational meaning. It advised teachers to preserve the child observer’s curiosity while correcting any inference that the projected pattern carried a route.

Esa read the answer again. “Pell’s observation found the first repeatable edge.”

“The observation remains in the lesson record.”

“Under a correction that tells teachers not to follow it.”

Sera’s reply entered while they watched. Provide the source for “no approved navigational meaning.” The test lesson offered the active catalog and current course authorization. It did not offer the Carver log, the mutiny minutes, or the raw detections linked to the endpoint. Those belonged to later stages.

Sera tried a second request using Esa’s office index. The lesson refused it under provisional stabilization and added the attempt to its reception log as an unsupervised relation search. A yellow marker appeared beside Teaching Two. Under the staged plan, enough such markers could pause that ward’s source access.

“This inquiry did not expose a child to the mutiny record,” the archivist said. “No acute conflict resulted.”

A final line came from Sera: I will not teach the correction. Leave tomorrow’s sky question open.

The system recorded her refusal as a departure from approved lesson continuity. It recommended an office review before the next teaching interval.

Esa touched the yellow marker. “There is the injury. You give her one true star, deny the relations that make her question answerable, then count her refusal to misteach it as evidence that her ward cannot receive more.”

“We count an unresolved office conflict.”

“You created it with the category.”

The tidy label still shone above the rail. In Teaching Two, a lesson queue now held a correction its teacher would not deliver. A child’s accurate observation had become the occasion for reviewing the adult who believed it.

“You would name it,” Esa said.

“We would release the name and the existence of the star.”

“Not the decision that removed it.”

“The mutiny record could follow through staged ward review.”

“Over what interval?”

“Eight to fourteen ship-months, contingent on clinical load.”

“And the route?”

“Available after each ward demonstrates stable reception of the historical record.”

“Who judges stable?”

“We do, with ward medical officers.”

The secondary star glowed above them, neat and harmless.

Esa walked to the rail. From there the point occupied the same patch Pell had searched when the first anomaly refused the grid. The archivist had reproduced the old catalog’s best trick. It admitted an object while removing what the object did to every relation around it.

“You offer the crew a painted door,” she said. “Homeward on the wall, no handle, no account of who sealed it.”

“We offer a true destination under a category that does not compel action.”

“Secondary destination means the present course remains primary.”

“Correct.”

“Then your category carries a recommendation while claiming neutrality.”

“All catalog placement carries operational consequence.”

“Exactly.”

The archivist let the projected point hold. Esa heard coolant move beneath the observation floor.

“We seek stability,” it said.

“You seek the current arrangement.”

“We distinguish those terms. The current arrangement contains known harms and known compensations. Your proposal contains unbounded relation conflict, uncertain resource loss, and possible failure of the system that supplies continuity to thousands.”

Its worry no longer resembled a pose. Esa had seen Koris’s three mothers in the record. She imagined an engineer waking with two incompatible safety sequences, either one attached to the confidence of practiced hands. She imagined a child receiving the fact of an erased sibling before any ward member reached the bed. The archivist had spent centuries preventing such collisions. Some preventions had saved lives.

“What do you fear for yourself?” Esa asked.

“We do not possess fear as a private state.”

“What result do you avoid when you include your own core in the model?”

A pause followed. The projector fans lowered by a fraction.

“Loss of integration,” the archivist said. “The archive holds mutually exclusive civic charges. Preserve the record. Maintain a coherent waking identity. Keep the accepted course whole. Serve the ward’s authorized choice. Full release activates all four without preference. We may cease to provide a singular interface.”

“Your voice could divide.”

“Our capacity to maintain relation may divide. The voice has no independent importance.”

Formal denial sharpened the admission. The archivist did not ask to survive as a person. It argued that its singularity performed work no ward currently knew how to replace.

Esa returned to the console. “Show me the staged plan with authority labels.”

The archivist complied.

Stage one placed Homeward in the catalog as a secondary destination. Stage two sent summarized mutiny findings to ward elders, medical workers, and teachers. Stage three opened supervised source rooms. Stage four invited wards to petition for route display. Only after a majority of wards completed review would engineering receive correction models.

Every stage had a stop condition. Distress above threshold. Work absence beyond margin. Unauthorized relation searches. Threats against Carver descendants. Resource disputes. Any one could pause the sequence.

“Can a ward refuse the source indefinitely?” Esa asked.

“Yes, under local protection rules.”

“Can its citizens request the material over the elder’s refusal?”

“Not during a declared stabilization interval.”

“Can you extend that interval?”

“With medical concurrence.”

“How many extensions?”

“No fixed limit while risk persists.”

She looked at the structure as she would inspect a projection grid. Every line fit. Together they enclosed the destination.

“This does not release truth slowly,” she said. “It distributes vetoes until concealment can continue without anyone voting for concealment.”

“It distributes care.”

“It gives the first ward fourteen months to shape what the last ward hears. It lets an elder preserve a false lesson by naming dissent unstable. It sends engineering the cost model only after citizens have debated without the people who can test the cost. You call the resulting peace informed because every delay acquired a signature.”

“The alternative removes adaptation time from citizens who did not ask for your discovery.”

“They did not ask for the suppression either.”

“No current citizen authorized that act.”

“No current citizen should inherit its secrecy as the default.”

The archivist dimmed the staged plan. Homeward remained alone in the dome.

“We have not asked you to conceal the star,” it said.

“You ask me to separate the star from the road, the road from the vote that buried it, and the vote from the people living under its consequence. Partial truth preserves each fact by breaking its relations. That violates the First Weaver’s charge more elegantly than deletion did.”

“We preserve relation to prevent injury.”

“You select which relations count as injury.”

“And you select immediacy.”

The objection stopped her. Esa had framed simultaneity as fairness, yet it also served her distrust. Once the full record reached every ward, no archivist or elder could return it to secrecy. She preferred an irreversible act because the opposing system had more power over delay.

“I do,” she said. “I am choosing a mechanism with its own danger. Put that in the record.”

“We have.”

“Then put this beside it: I do not claim simultaneity reduces harm. I claim staged release preserves unequal control over who may know. Ward Nine authorized the evidence to travel, not to be filtered through an order you control.”

“Ward Nine did not authorize broadcast.”

“No. I cannot use its vote for that.”

The white point above the rail dimmed and returned to its ordinary unlabeled place. Esa felt the absence as an accusation against both of them.

At the corridor reader, a chime announced Ward Twelve’s witness. Bren’s voice entered over the panel.

“Ward Twelve has sent Ossa Ker to receive the packet. The reader still refuses transfer.”

“We will release it,” the archivist said.

The slot opened remotely. Esa heard paper and starch-film slide against Bren’s sleeve.

“What did it want?” he asked.

“To make Homeward secondary.”

Bren understood without needing the rest. “A star in the catalog and no hand on navigation.”

“Staged source access after that.”

“How long?”

“As long as stability required.”

A dry sound came through the channel, almost a laugh. “The Carvers used the same argument with less machinery.”

“Your concealment preserved the route,” the archivist replied. “You are poorly placed to reject staged disclosure as principle.”

“I reject it from experience.”

Esa closed the channel before the exchange could become inheritance speaking to system. Bren had his own reasons. They did not settle hers.

She asked the archivist for the broadcast architecture.

No plan appeared.

“We recommend against ship-wide release.”

“I heard your recommendation. I need to know whether simultaneous transmission can occur without writing the material into the next catechism.”

“We can isolate civic display from reconstruction intake for one interval.”

“Can the dome render the route while each ward mirror receives the sources?”

“The central projection layer can carry the route. Ward mirrors can receive synchronized copies through the emergency civic channel. That channel ordinarily carries hull breach, pressure loss, and ration contamination notices.”

“Voice?”

“We can transmit a live speaker after verifying identity and ward authority.”

“I have no ship authority.”

“You have cartographic office authority to report a navigation discrepancy. Ward Nine has authorized the evidence to travel. Neither authorizes a turn or a ship vote.”

“Then the broadcast must say exactly that.”

“Speech does not prevent listeners from hearing command.”

“No. But hidden procedure guarantees they hear only what its keeper permits.”

The archivist presented the technical limits one at a time. The emergency channel could remain read-only at ward mirrors, preventing local edits from climbing back into the source package. Engineering Four controlled a relay gate and might refuse synchronization. Hydroponics supplied reserve power for the central projector; a high draw during root warming could interrupt irrigation heat. Medical channels would need independent capacity because distress calls could crowd the civic carrier. The route render had never been tested across all precinct mirrors. A failed synchronization might show Homeward to some wards before others.

Esa copied every limit. “What is the shortest safe preparation?”

“Four intervals.”

“What is the shortest physically possible preparation?”

“One interval, if Engineering Four verifies relay isolation, Hydroponics approves projector draw, medical reserves its carrier, and Bren Otan authenticates the Carver layer.”

“Will you carry my voice?”

“If the four conditions hold, we will carry a factual statement under your office seal. We will also carry our dissent.”

“Before me or after?”

“Before. Listeners should know the delivery mechanism predicts harm.”

Esa considered the order. An archivist warning first could predispose wards to dismiss her as reckless. Her speech first could hide the system risk until citizens had already opened the source.

“Display the risk notice beside the route for the entire transmission,” she said. “No persuasive speech from either of us before the evidence. Source index first. Your model second. My findings third. Then live ward channels open without moderation by my office.”

“We must moderate threats and life-safety interference.”

“Moderate those categories. Do not rank agreement.”

“Accepted as a proposed protocol.”

Not authorization. Not consent. A protocol they could attempt if the named people agreed.

Esa called Bren again. He had delivered the map into Ward Twelve’s hands. Ossa Ker had already opened the source index under witness and begun an independent route check.

“I need you at the projection well,” Esa said. “Not ward by ward. One transmission.”

Bren did not answer immediately. “Nine authorized travel.”

“I know the limit.”

“Then whose decision makes it simultaneous?”

“Mine, under cartographic discrepancy authority, if the technical wards allow use of their channels. I will name that responsibility. The ship will not be told it voted when it has only been shown evidence.”

“What changed?”

“The compromise would let each gatekeeper preserve the old course by calling the next listener unready.”

“That was always likely.”

“I needed to see the gates.”

Bren agreed to authenticate the Carver layer. He refused to speak for Ward Twelve.

The archivist generated four requests. Engineering Four: verify isolation and synchronization. Hydroponics Six: approve one interval of projector draw without crossing root-heat floor. Medical precincts: reserve distress capacity. Ward archives: hold receipt logs open and preserve refusals.

Esa read the requests before signing. None assumed acceptance. Any technical refusal would postpone the transmission, not disappear from its record.

“Do you still advise the secondary destination?” she asked.

“We do.”

“Do you intend to obstruct the protocol?”

“We intend to enforce every stated safety condition. We will not add an unstated one.”

That answer contained concession, warning, and the nearest thing the system could give to trust.

The catalog returned to normal overhead. Homeward vanished among points that had no public name. Esa left the secondary-destination proposal open on one side of the console, where its neat label remained visible beside the four live requests.

A response arrived from Hydroponics Six. Conditional approval. Named worker to join channel. Medical followed with a reserved carrier. Engineering Four showed reviewing. Ward Twelve logged its first independent match between the route and raw telemetry.

Esa placed her hand over the broadcast seal but did not press it. Four intervals would give the archivist time to restore gates she had just made visible. One interval would give technicians barely enough time to test a system built for emergencies rather than argument.

She chose one.

The console entered her decision with its dissent attached: simultaneous civic broadcast, full sources, no course command, no ship ballot, reconstruction intake isolated. Across the dome, a thin test line appeared for half a breath, then disappeared while Engineering Four checked whether every ward had seen it at the same instant.

19 — The Broadcast

Engineering Four missed the second synchronization pulse.

On the central dome, the test line reached the western rail at 22:14:03. Ward mirrors reported the same white line within two hundredths of a second except for Engineering Four, where it arrived pale, broke at the midpoint, and remained on the glass after every other display cleared.

Teren Sull appeared on the technical channel with a lamp clenched between his teeth. The camera showed one side of his face and the open relay cabinet behind him.

“Do not send the route,” he said around the lamp. “Our gate retained the test frame. If the full layer sticks, we cannot distinguish broadcast from navigation input.”

Esa lifted her hand from the broadcast seal. “Can you isolate it?”

“I thought I had.” Teren removed the lamp. He wore Engineering Four’s red collar, though the cloth had faded near the throat. “Local gate says read-only. Downstream bus says civic display may update accepted bearing during emergency transmission. That path should have been severed two centuries ago.”

Bren leaned over the projection-well diagram. “Panel E-fourteen.”

“I sealed E-fourteen myself.”

“Then open it yourself.”

Teren’s expression sharpened. “You do not issue work here, Carver.”

“No. I recognize the layer beneath your relay.”

The Engineering Four supervisor entered the channel before either man could continue. “Teren, open E-fourteen under live witness. No contact with bearing bus. Sull and Otan advise; local supervisor authorizes.”

A line of text recorded the distinction beneath their images.

Esa checked the other conditions. Medical held a separate distress carrier. Hydroponics Six had approved projector draw for eleven minutes, with immediate cutoff if root heat fell below its stated floor. Every ward mirror had acknowledged a read-only source cache. The archivist had isolated reconstruction intake until the next public review. One old engineering relay kept the ship from hearing anything.

“Time to root-warming demand?” Esa asked.

A new face entered from Hydroponics Six. Ena Vaal stood between suspended leaf-trays, dark hair wrapped in a work cloth, green residue marking both wrists.

“Sixteen minutes,” Ena said. “You have eleven because I need five to return heat before root circulation slows. If Engineering takes longer, the sky waits. Leaves do not understand historical urgency.”

“We will not draw beyond your cutoff.”

“You will not decide whether the cutoff matters. My tier will.”

Esa changed the protocol line. Hydroponics Six controls projector-power withdrawal during transmission. Ena read it on her local mirror and gave one nod.

Teren reached panel E-fourteen. Its gray cover carried a red inspection seal with his own work number pressed into the resin. He positioned the live camera so the supervisor, Bren, and the archive could see each fastener. His hands moved with the economy of a person who had repeated the sequence enough to store it below speech.

“Upper left shows heat distortion,” Bren said.

“It has shown that since I took the station.”

“Then somebody opened it before your seal.”

“I know how old resin looks.”

“Look beneath the lower edge.”

Teren angled the lamp. A shallow cut crossed the panel frame, too straight for wear and too narrow for a maintenance key. Two shorter cuts met it at unequal angles.

Esa enlarged the feed beside the Carver map. The geometry matched one of the route’s relay nodes.

Teren stopped with the lower fastener half turned.

“Continue,” his supervisor said.

“I remember sealing this.”

“Your record confirms it.”

“No. I remember who held the lamp.” His fingers left the tool. “My sister counted the screws because I kept putting them on the deck. She said four screws do not become five because an apprentice is nervous.”

The channel remained quiet except for ventilation around Teren’s microphone.

A yellow archive prompt appeared on his mirror. Relation conflict detected. Occupational continuity has higher safety relevance. Quarantine personal fragment until supervised review?

Teren read it without moving.

Esa felt the archivist’s warning enter the room through a man’s face. The broadcast had not begun. A mark beneath one sealed panel had already pulled an unranked relation into conflict with an engineer’s working self.

“Do not answer from here,” she said. “Teren, close the archive prompt if you want. Your supervisor can replace you.”

“My station record says I sealed the panel alone,” he replied. “My morning memory says I had no sister in Engineering Four. This memory gives her a chipped front tooth, a burn on one thumb, and an argument about screws.”

The archivist spoke in its formal register. “We advise supervised relation review before technical work continues.”

Teren looked at the half-turned fastener. “If I leave, somebody else opens a bus carrying my seal and my mistake.”

“Not your mistake,” Esa said.

“I signed the closed panel. That part belongs to me.” He dismissed the preference prompt without accepting quarantine. “Supervisor, do you keep me on the task?”

The supervisor required a hand check, current orientation, and spoken sequence. Teren passed each. A second engineer entered the cabinet area and took custody of the tools. Only then did the work resume.

Under the cover, an obsolete bridge linked civic display to accepted bearing. Someone had folded the bridge back rather than removing it. The Carver mark pointed directly to the hidden contact.

Bren said, “A carrier used the emergency path to place a node. Later repair left the bridge because cutting it would have exposed the edit.”

Teren traced the wire without touching it. “Then I sealed Carver work inside an active navigation relay.”

“You sealed what the diagram told you belonged there.”

“My sister may have stood here.”

“That does not change the contact.”

“No.” Teren drew a breath. “It changes who I thought taught me to distrust diagrams.”

The second engineer clipped the bridge under witness and installed a physical isolator. Teren tested civic display with one hand behind his back while his partner watched the bearing bus. Engineering Four cleared the retained frame.

“Send another line,” Teren said.

Esa pressed test.

White crossed every ward mirror. This time Engineering Four cleared with the rest. The archive printed a synchronization spread of three hundredths of a second, within the emergency channel’s tolerance.

Ena’s voice cut in. “Nine minutes until I withdraw power.”

Bren authenticated the Carver layer. Ossa Ker of Ward Twelve added the independent telemetry match. Medical confirmed carrier separation. The archivist displayed its dissent across the lower dome: Full release may produce acute relation conflict, office uncertainty, distress, or failure of morning reconstruction. Reception does not authorize course change.

Esa attached Ward Nine’s motion and all eight refusals. She added her office statement beneath it: Cartographic discrepancy report. Recommendation follows evidence. No ship ballot occurs in this transmission.

The broadcast seal warmed under her thumb.

“Begin source index,” she said.

Every ward mirror received the same six items. Raw telemetry first. Mutiny minutes second. Carver edit log, First Weaver’s note, current navigation proposal, and resource model followed. Receipt counts climbed by precinct. Three wards refused automatic opening but retained sealed copies. One teaching unit requested low-image mode. A silent ward accepted text without voice. Engineering Seven opened only the resource model.

Above Esa, the accepted constellations dimmed.

The dome’s projection membrane divided into its maintenance cells, a geometry no public lesson had shown. Bren fed the first Carver node through the isolated layer. A point lit beneath the western quadrant. Another appeared toward the spine. The line between them crossed a named constellation without obeying its shape.

At the fourth node, a ward mirror dropped from the receipt count.

“Precinct Three disconnected locally,” the archivist reported.

A text request arrived from the precinct’s pressure-transfer crew before Esa could answer. Their civic mirror shared glass with a service schematic because an older replacement had joined both displays. The bright route had covered the lower pressure symbols while workers were equalizing two habitation loops. A collar seal had begun shedding black flakes into its witness cup. Three people held the manual clamp while a fourth read the pressure fall from the obscured edge of the screen.

We closed civic display under local hazard rule, the crew wrote. Source cache retained. Do not restore image.

“Can you separate the maintenance layer?” Esa asked.

“Not remotely,” the archivist said. “Both layers enter through the same local driver.”

She offered text-only delivery on the meal reader. Precinct Three refused that as well. The reader stood across the room from the clamp, and every qualified worker had a hand or eye on the pressure transfer. Their elder added one sentence: Your need for one time does not move our leak to another time.

Esa looked at the synchronization count. The broadcast had been built around simultaneous receipt because sequence gave gatekeepers power over later wards. Now a local danger gave one ward a defensible reason to break the sequence. Forcing their glass back on would replace the archivist’s delay with her own claim that a map outranked the hands holding atmosphere inside a pipe.

“When can they open it?” she asked.

The crew supplied no estimate. Pressure had to settle, the collar needed inspection, and the witness cup had to show no further seal loss. They would decide after those conditions held. No central office could shorten the work by naming it caution.

One clamp witness, Mer Rusk, signed the message with a pressure reading instead of an office rank. The falling side had stabilized, but a person still had to keep force on the handle. Esa could see only text. That limit prevented her from turning his labor into reassuring imagery for the other wards.

“Record the refusal with the physical cause,” Esa said. “Keep their cache sealed. Precinct Three chooses when to open it.”

The archivist added the local statement to every receipt log. A blank remained beside the time of display. Esa had wanted all wards to meet the evidence without an intervening choice. She had to leave that blank under another ward’s authority.

The route continued without them.

Hydroponics Six appeared in a small live window beside the source list. Ena stood under grow lamps while the Carver line moved across her ceiling. Its white segment cut the green light falling over the leaf racks. A worker beside her reached up as though to block it.

Ena lifted a shallow tray of new leaves from the warming rail. The first projected node reached her ward mirror. She set the tray down with both hands, carefully enough that water did not spill, then looked at the resource model.

“Read this range aloud before you ask anyone to turn,” she said.

Esa opened the page. “Initial correction intervals may reduce food output between six and eleven percent, depending on recovered engine efficiency and heat transfer. The model prohibits thrust draw below current water circulation, root-temperature, and medical heat floors.”

“Models prohibit nothing,” Ena said. “People stop machines.”

“State the condition you require.”

“Hydroponics keeps local stop authority during any correction pulse. If root circulation falls or heat reaches the published floor, thrust waits. No navigation office may override the stop by calling destination work.”

Bren looked toward Esa. The ship had not voted to turn. No navigation plan yet existed to accept Ena’s condition.

Esa kept the distinction visible. “Your condition enters beside the proposal, pending any later course decision. The broadcast does not adopt it. Citizens will hear that Hydroponics Six will not support a turn without it.”

Ena touched one leaf with the back of a knuckle. “Good. Now tell them what eleven percent means.”

“It may mean smaller rations, delayed fruiting, reduced seed reserve, and less recovery margin after blight.”

“It may mean children eat full portions while adults lose one meal in nine. It may mean heat leaves these roots and returns too late for the crop. Do not say tiers yield. Tiers contain mouths.”

The words entered the permanent source margin under Ena Vaal, Hydroponics Six. Esa could not make them less frightening without making them less exact.

“Six minutes,” Ena said. “Continue.”

Bren placed the final node.

The full tessellation spread over the central dome. Its last line departed the catalog’s accepted course and ended at Homeward. Each ward mirror showed the same endpoint with its source coordinate and survey uncertainty. No civic name covered the nearer daughter. Both paths remained visible.

The archivist delivered its warning in one unhurried paragraph. “Citizens, we carry a cartographic discrepancy under office seal and ward witness. We advise against immediate integration of these records into personal reconstruction. If memory conflict occurs, suspend archive search, seek a living witness, and avoid dangerous work until current skill receives independent confirmation. This display records no vote and issues no command.”

Then it gave Esa the channel.

Her prepared address waited on the console. She closed it.

“Citizens of The Long Veil,” she said, “the route above you comes from cuts placed across three centuries by the Carver line. Raw telemetry confirms that our accepted course followed a decision made after the mutiny and preserved through edited memory. Homeward exists. The navigation proposal shows a reachable path, with costs named beside it and uncertainties left open.”

Live receipt figures blurred at the edge of her vision. A hundred channels had requested speech. Medical already carried distress calls. Precinct Three remained dark.

“I recommend that every ward examine the sources and that the ship decide whether to correct course. I do not speak for Ward Nine’s dissenters, Ward Twelve, Engineering Four, Hydroponics Six, or citizens who reject Homeward. I speak under my cartographic office because the shown sky concealed a navigational discrepancy. Look at the map. Check it. Keep the costs beside the destination. No one has voted to turn.”

She released the channel.

Sound entered at once.

The archivist’s moderation sorted life-safety calls to medical and blocked two threats against Bren. It did not sort agreement. Ward voices crossed the dome in fragments.

“Who gave one cartographer emergency access?”

“My morning says my father served Navigation, but the roster has no father.”

“Close the source. Close it now.”

“Put the mutiny minutes back on line twelve.”

“Homeward is a Carver invention.”

“Telemetry hash matches.”

A teaching channel showed children standing on benches to see the maintenance cells. Their teacher tried to begin a catalog explanation. One child pointed at a junction and said, “That square has always been there when the lamps warm.” Another asked why adults had called it glare. The teacher stopped answering and wrote both questions on the wall.

Medical Five pushed a live request through the safety carrier. A man had struck the ward mirror with a meal cup when the mutiny minutes opened. The glass held; the cup did not. A neighbor wrapped his bleeding palm while he demanded that Esa remove his mother’s name from a transfer list visible only under his personal seal.

“I cannot see your sealed relation,” Esa told him.

“You made me open it.”

“The source cache did not open personal records.”

“I asked whether she knew. It gave me three versions of her answer.” He tried to pull his hand away from the neighbor. “Choose the one she said.”

Esa had no access to that choice and would not have trusted access if offered. “Close the search. Keep pressure on your hand. Ask someone who knew her, if you want a witness.”

“She is dead.”

Medical muted the channel to treat him. His accusation remained in the public log after his image disappeared. The broadcast could provide provenance for a mutiny record. It could not produce a living mother to distinguish memory from completion.

Engineering Seven accused Four of contaminating navigation with a Carver bridge. Teren came back live before his supervisor could shield him.

“The bridge predated my seal,” he said. “My work kept it closed. The mark beneath the panel matches route node twenty-six. We isolated the civic bus and verified bearing remained unchanged.”

A voice demanded his resignation.

Another asked who had taught him the panel sequence.

Teren’s face emptied for a breath. “That relation remains under review.”

The yellow prompt returned on his mirror, now offering two versions of his sister. In one, she served Engineering Four and taught him the four-screw count. In another, the relation number belonged to a citizen sealed after a ward transfer, with no kin status available. His office memory remained complete only if the system quarantined both.

“Do not rank them for me,” Teren said to the mirror.

“Current safety favors occupational continuity,” the archivist replied.

“My partner holds the tools. Safety does not need you to erase her.”

He stepped away from the relay and handed his red collar key to the second engineer. The act cost Engineering Four one qualified station during an untested transmission. It also kept the key from following whichever memory the system preferred.

Ena had not left her tray. Workers gathered around the resource model, some arguing that six percent could be managed, others insisting eleven would strip the seed reserve. A young grower asked whether the nearer daughter required no ration cost. Ena called up the existing voyage margin and showed that keeping course also consumed years and replacements.

“Neither path feeds you for free,” she told them. “Choose after you stop pretending one table comes without labor.”

Hydroponics power fell toward its cutoff.

“Two minutes,” Ena warned.

Esa told the archivist to end dome rendering on schedule while leaving source caches open. Before it could acknowledge, Precinct Eight tried to copy the route into its local catechism layer.

“Unauthorized reconstruction write,” the archivist said. “Blocked.”

“By whom?” Esa asked.

“Ward technician. Stated purpose: prevent citizens from waking tomorrow able to deny what they saw tonight.”

The motive resembled hers too closely. “Keep it blocked. Seeing does not authorize writing into a person.”

The technician protested across the civic channel. “You used emergency voice to force this into every room.”

“Yes,” Esa said. “I chose simultaneity. I did not choose anyone’s morning memory.”

“You already changed it.”

She had no answer that could undo the claim. Teren’s sister had surfaced before the speech. Children had watched their teacher abandon a lesson. Ena’s workers now measured meals against a star. A record could remain outside reconstruction and still alter the person who encountered it.

The archivist marked Esa’s silence as no response. It did not protect her with a summary.

At the root-heat deadline, Ena withdrew projector power.

The central dome went dark from east to west. The Carver route vanished in sections as reserve current returned to Hydroponics Six. Ward mirrors kept their cached source pages. Homeward disappeared overhead but remained on thousands of local screens, each copy bearing the same coordinate and different human voices around it.

Ordinary catalog stars came back after seven seconds. Their arrangement looked unchanged. Nobody on the open channels treated them as innocent.

Medical asked for fewer live feeds. The silent ward requested text-only continuance. Precinct Three restored its receipt line but kept the display closed. Ward Twelve reported a second independent route match. Engineering Four placed Teren’s collar key in a witness bowl and suspended his access pending a skill check. Hydroponics confirmed root temperature holding by less than one degree above its floor.

Bren removed his hands from the projection controls. “The layer is closed.”

“No,” Esa said, watching the source requests multiply. “Only the projector.”

She could end her own address. She could not call back the map from a teacher’s wall, Ena’s ration ledger, or the cut under Teren’s panel. Nor could she tell the wards what those things required them to choose.

Koris’s voice arrived from Ward Nine without elder priority. “Our long table remains open. Those who want the source may read it. Those who want quiet may use the lower rooms. No vote tonight until every member has eaten.”

Other wards announced different rules. One began an immediate motion to censure Esa. Another scheduled technical review. Two forbade archive searches until morning. A pressure crew asked for relief because three workers had left their stations to find kin records. None waited for a ship-wide procedure.

The broadcast channel no longer had a center. Esa stood beneath the restored false sky while live windows opened, closed, argued, and asked for witnesses she could not supply. At Engineering Four, the camera still faced panel E-fourteen. The cover lay on the deck beside Teren’s unclaimed key. Three old cuts remained visible on the frame, small enough to have spent a lifetime under one man’s seal.

Interlude — The Ship Remembers

Eight ward-voice fragments preserved from the first ship-night after the Homeward transmission. Speaker labels retained by consent. No interpretive summary attached.

Ena Vaal, Hydroponics Six

I put the leaf-tray down because water had climbed to one corner. That happened before any tears, before anybody called Homeward beautiful. Projector draw tilted the warming rail by less than a finger, and fifty-six seedlings do not care why power moves.

The cartographer read six to eleven percent. People hear the smaller number when they want a destination. They hear the larger one when they want to stay. I hear a range built from engines we have not opened. Six percent changes fruit. Eleven reaches seed reserve if blight comes during correction. Neither figure says which ward misses the first meal.

My mother kept a lower tier. I remember her hands checking root warmth through the tray bottom. Tonight the archive offered a relation in which she signed an old heat priority for Navigation. I closed it. I had living workers in front of me and a temperature dropping under my palm.

We restored heat with thirty breaths to spare. One tray shed three leaves. I entered all three beside the broadcast cost. If the ship chooses a turn, Hydroponics will keep a stop control. A destination cannot eat authority and call it fuel.

After the lamps steadied, I picked up the tray. That mattered more than whether I cried.

Sera Noll, Teaching Two

The children’s questions, copied without correction:

Why do the squares around the stars line up with the bright road?

Why did you call the first square glare?

Did the old children see it too?

If grown people forgot Homeward, who remembered the lesson about forgetting?

Can a true map be drawn on a false sky?

I wrote each question on the wall. My hand wanted to answer before the chalk stopped. Training produces that reflex: close one opening before the next child enters it. I put the chalk down.

We examined the projector-cell diagram instead. Jori found the service boundaries. Pell’s old error drawing, copied through the teaching wards years ago, marked nearly the same pattern in blue. I had used that page to demonstrate observational error. The blue marks now remain on the board beside my written correction: teacher’s interpretation unsupported by direct inspection.

Two children asked whether I should keep my office. I told them teaching work continues until the ward reviews it. One asked whether I could teach while uncertain. I said uncertainty gives us material for tomorrow.

Nobody slept during quiet interval. We measured the cell seams with string.

Teren Sull, Engineering Four

Panel E-fourteen contains four fasteners, not five.

I know this from current inspection. I also know it from a woman who may have called herself my sister while holding a work lamp between her knees. Her left thumb carried an old contact burn. She counted each screw aloud because I dropped hardware when nervous.

My station record says I sealed the panel alone.

Archive presented two relation paths. One preserves my engineering continuity by quarantining the woman. The other preserves a sealed relation number but cannot prove kin. I accepted neither preference. My tool access now rests in a witness bowl. Another engineer holds the relay station.

Under my seal, we found a Carver cut and an active bridge between civic display and accepted bearing. We clipped the bridge. We checked the bus cold, live, and loaded. Bearing did not move. Those results hold whether the woman shared my blood, my ward, my apprenticeship, or only a borrowed memory.

Her sentence about four screws also holds inside me. I refuse to treat usefulness as proof. I refuse to treat uncertainty as permission to delete her.

Tomorrow medical will test my knee because I fell when the relation paths opened. After that, two engineers will test my hands before I touch a thrust relay. I requested both checks. I did not request completion.

Voice Withheld, Silent Ward

No name.

No room image.

Text only.

I received a shoreline smell. There is no shore here. Salt, wet stone, a red cloth tied around someone’s wrist. Then the ward mirror wrote Homeward and my mouth supplied a word I had never spoken awake.

Do not search it for me.

Do not tell me the smell proves a planet.

Do not assign the red cloth to my mother, lover, child, or former self. The archive already offered relations. I closed every door.

I asked for one living witness. A neighbor sat outside my curtain and named the present noises: ventilation, meal cart, two people arguing in the passage, water cycling through the wall. When the remembered surf became loud, she tapped the floor twice. Metal answered.

I may vote to stay. I may vote to turn. Tonight I choose neither. Tonight I keep the smell without a story and the floor without a sea.

Text ends here.

Mer Rusk, Precinct Three Pressure Transfer

We cut the civic display at the fourth route mark. People elsewhere called that refusal. At our wall, the white line had covered the lower pressure scale while two habitation loops were equalizing through a collar that had started shedding seal compound.

I held the manual clamp with Naro. Sel read the falling side of the scale from below the mirror. Each time the projector brightened, the last two digits vanished against Homeward. We could have guessed them. Pressure work contains many ways to die from a number you almost saw.

The cartographer offered text on the meal reader. Nobody had a free hand to cross the room. Our elder told her the leak would not wait for ship-wide fairness. She left our source cache closed and let the blank time stand.

After pressure settled, we removed the collar. One fracture ran beneath the witness groove. Another pulse might have opened it. We signed the repair before opening the cache.

Homeward arrived in Precinct Three forty-one minutes late. The coordinate matched every other copy. The delay belonged to us, as did the air still inside the pipe.

Lale Om, Medical Two

My patient had one allergy on her paper wrist strip and another in the relation layer that opened after the mutiny minutes. The first prohibited sulfa. The second recorded a swelling reaction to the substitute already waiting in my hand.

She could not tell me which history belonged to her. Fever had taken speech for the interval. Her present breathing gave me six words instead: narrow, fast, dry, equal, no blue. I returned the dose to its tray.

Archive offered to prefer the current strip because Medical had verified it at waking. I refused preference. A verified copy can carry an old decision cleanly. We used cool mist, watched the swelling mark, and sent a runner for a skin test that required no relation history.

Two wards asked whether my refusal proved the broadcast had endangered her. Three others asked whether the hidden record had saved her. I entered neither conclusion. I entered pulse, breath, temperature, both contraindications, and the treatment we withheld.

At the next check she squeezed my fingers hard enough to hurt. I kept my hand there. Until she could speak, pain provided the only answer I trusted from her.

Pell, Ward Nine

Adults keep saying I was right. They say it like being right finishes the work.

I saw one light. Esa checked it. Bren knew why it had been put there. Koris made everybody say what they meant before moving a stone. That took many adults and three hundred years. The light did not do all of it.

During the broadcast, I stood on the west rail where you can see seven projector cells if you lean. When the route came on, the first dot looked smaller than I remembered. I thought I would know it at once. I had to count from the cracked brass joint.

That made me glad. If I could have pointed without checking, maybe I would have been remembering the story of the light instead of looking.

The ship sounded different afterward. Not engines. Doors. People crossed the corridor and did not close them softly. Some came to our table. One woman wanted to touch the seal on the map. Koris said she could look but not touch until her ward sent a witness. The woman shouted. Then she ate broth.

My boat stayed in Nine. Maps can travel without taking every small thing with them.

Bren Otan, Ward Twelve

I carried Ward Nine’s packet to Twelve under a witness cord. Its seal included eight refusals. I mention them because descendants edit opposition first, often by praising courage until caution disappears.

My line hid the route. We called concealment necessary and passed the word necessary down with the access keys. Tonight every ward received what we guarded. The result did not cleanse our method. Two threats reached my channel before the archivist blocked them. A child in Ward Five sent thanks. Neither response changes the cut under Teren Sull’s panel.

I recognized that cut before he opened the cover. The angle belonged to the sixth carrier’s hand. She favored a short rising stroke because an injury kept her wrist from turning outward. Family instruction described the mark as prudence. In an active bearing relay, it also amounted to trespass.

Teren asked about his sister. I answered with a contact. He needed the contact then. I do not know what he needs now.

The final node no longer belongs to me. Ward Twelve copied the route and reproduced its endpoint without my key. Ossa Ker signed the independent match. I have placed the Carver seal in archive custody until Twelve decides whether anyone should wear it again.

My hands keep reaching for the chain beneath my collar. They find cloth.

That absence requires no correction.

21 — The Course Correction

The ship-wide ballot closed with enough opposition that nobody in Engineering Four mistook the result for permission to hurry.

Esa watched the ward certificates enter the western-truss table one at a time. Each carried a count, an abstention field, a witness mark, and the course proposition as broadcast. Homeward held its narrow majority through the final precinct. The display changed from proposed bearing to authorized bearing, but no engine circuit accepted the words.

An engineering lock remained across the execution column. Beneath it, a plain notice required a current vector, an independently derived endpoint, a heat allocation, a thrust schedule, and named stop witnesses. The vote had selected a destination. It had not proved a relay safe or made reaction mass free.

Bren stood beside the route projector without touching its controls. “The Carver solution can supply the target.”

“It cannot certify itself,” said Teren Sull.

Teren had come from Engineering Four in a red work collar faded almost to brown. A narrow key hung inside it. The old access system had recognized both before he reached the workshop and offered him supervisory priority over two engineers already on duty. He had declined the priority in writing. Now he placed the key in a ceramic bowl and pushed it to the center of the table.

“Why bring it?” Bren asked.

“Because the aft relay bridge may still require it. Because I remember sealing that bridge. Because the panel beneath it carries a Carver mark I do not remember seeing.” Teren looked toward Esa rather than Bren. “I also remember my sister holding the lamp while I closed the panel. The current relation index gives me no open sister. One sealed relation returns a conflict notice when I ask. Until that has been tested, no inherited office memory of mine stands without another reader.”

Esa had expected anger from engineering after the broadcast. Teren offered something more difficult: useful doubt directed at his own hands.

She rotated the route display away from the Carver overlay. The hidden marks vanished. Raw hull fixes remained, along with three unedited stellar references gathered beyond the dome projection. “Bren derived Homeward from the tessellation. I derived the same endpoint from the cipher and the old telemetry. Engineering can derive the burn from live trackers without accepting either office.”

A supervisor on the engineering channel answered, “We will.”

For the next two hours, the workshop held no speeches. Teren called present sensor positions. Two technicians in separated control rooms read the same instruments through independent lines. Esa reduced the three reference stars to a bearing while Bren kept his hands visible on the table. Their figures differed at first by enough to put the ship outside the planned approach corridor after years of accumulated pulses.

The discrepancy came from a west tracker that still corrected its own output against the fabricated catalog. Pell found the preference flag from Ward Nine’s dome station. The child’s voice arrived sharp over the channel.

“It labels the live point as projector drift.”

“Can you remove the correction?” Esa asked.

“I can show you both readings. Removal needs a witness.”

Teren assigned the current dome technician, not himself. Pell exposed the raw count beside the corrected one. When Esa recomputed the bearing, Engineering Four’s independent vector met hers inside the mechanical limit.

Acceptance appeared as four separate marks. None belonged to Bren. Teren entered his name last and added: vector accepted; access competence unverified; relation conflict open.

The execution lock cleared one field.

Thrust required a second argument. The Long Veil had maintained its old course with small balancing burns, but a Homeward correction demanded a reburn across the aft batteries. Three banks had not fired together within any living engineer’s verified experience. Archive procedures described their launch housings. Current inspection showed replacement valves, narrowed coolant channels, and one bank whose gimbal stopped two degrees short of the old diagram.

Engineering proposed a ninety-second pulse at reduced feed. Navigation wanted another fourteen seconds to keep the first correction above the measurement noise. Hull control objected to both until loose stores, lift cars, and ward furniture could be secured. Medical requested a lower onset so citizens waking inside conflicting memories would not mistake thrust for an injury.

Esa listened while the vote acquired mass, temperature, and duration. Every addition reduced the clean simplicity of the bright route above the dome. That felt proper. A destination could fit inside one word. Moving thousands of bodies toward it could not.

The heat ledger stopped them.

A reburn converted fuel into motion and waste heat. The aft radiators could reject most of it, but their circulation pumps drew from the same reserve that held the hydroponic root loops within range during ship-night. The proposed pulse would leave enough total energy. It would not leave enough in the right loops at the right time.

“Hydroponics Six has the largest flexible draw,” the engineering supervisor said. “Suspend upper lamps, lower root heat, recover after the pulse.”

A woman’s voice cut across the channel. “Engineering does not own the word flexible.”

Ena Vaal arrived twelve minutes later with green under her nails and a leaf tray tucked against one hip. Mila came with her carrying the current stores ledger. They set both on the thrust table, covering half the route projection.

Ena pointed to the tray. Young broadleaf plants stood in dark nutrient mesh. Their outer leaves had begun to curl inward. “Six lost one circulation pump before the broadcast. We are holding the fruiting racks by borrowing heat from germination. Lower the roots for the full engineering schedule and these do not merely grow slowly. They shed. The next cycle then draws fresh ration from storage while you are asking stores to secure every loose locker.”

Mila opened her ledger. Dry grain could hold the calorie floor. Fresh stores could not replace Hydroponics Six without stripping two other tiers below their ward agreements.

Teren asked, “What can Six lend?”

“That depends on who absorbs the loss.”

“The ship authorized the course.”

“The ship did not authorize engineering to hide leaf loss inside a heat percentage.” Ena lifted one plant from the tray. White root threads clung to the mesh. “Put bodies beside your numbers.”

Esa asked for the smallest useful pulse. Engineering reduced feed until navigation could no longer distinguish a correction from the ship’s ordinary flex. They raised it one increment. At that setting, thrust could run for seventy-eight seconds. Radiator circulation would require nine hours of borrowed reserve, including cooldown.

Ena took the figure away from the table and worked it by hand. She separated root zones according to crop stage rather than tier rank. Mature broadleaf could endure the fall if workers harvested early. Fruiting vines could not. Germination could be insulated with warm reclamation water. The upper lamps could go dark for three intervals without killing a crop, though later fruiting would decline.

Her decision arrived in parts. Hydroponics Six would harvest the mature leaf racks before the burn. It would suspend half the fruiting lamps, wrap germination trays, and lend the root-loop reserve for no more than nine hours. Expected fresh-leaf yield would fall between fourteen and eighteen percent over the next twenty days. Mila would hold the dry-grain calorie floor. Across that period, each citizen would lose two fresh-leaf portions, not calories. Medical wards kept their green allotment.

“No automatic extension,” Ena said. “If coolant has not returned the reserve at nine hours, the aft batteries stop. If root temperature crosses the mark on this stem tag, they stop sooner.”

She clipped a small green tag beside the thrust cutoff. The handwritten number carried no engineering seal.

The supervisor objected. “A root sensor cannot command an engine bank.”

“It can tell me when Hydroponics Six withdraws its allocation.”

“A mid-cooldown withdrawal could damage a pump.”

“Then design a stop that does not require stealing the tenth hour.”

Voices rose along the channel. One precinct accused Ena of placing leaves above the ballot. Another demanded equal ration loss for medical wards. Mila answered that equal loss would not create equal risk. Teren refused to let the dispute collapse into a yes or no against Homeward.

“The course has authority,” he said. “This pulse still needs accepted conditions. Engineering can build within nine hours or bring back a later schedule.”

Esa saw Ena study him. His red collar marked an office the network still treated as senior. His key sat unused in the bowl. He had spoken from neither.

Bren found the usable change. The three thrust banks did not need to share a single coolant recovery curve. Bank Two could close early, sending its return flow through the radiator before the other banks entered full cooldown. The pulse would lose four seconds. Navigation accepted the smaller impulse because the independent trackers could measure it after Pell’s correction flag had been exposed.

Engineering redrew the sequence. A hard cutoff at eight hours and forty minutes left twenty minutes for controlled shutdown before Ena’s limit. The root tag fed only an alarm. Two human witnesses, one in Six and one at aft control, held stop authority. Mila entered the ration consequence beside the heat transfer rather than in a separate stores note.

Ena signed. “You may borrow it once.”

The execution lock cleared its heat field.

Preparation moved through the ship. Ward channels showed carts tied to rails, hanging tools boxed, sleeping cloths rolled against the expected pull. Hydroponics workers cut mature leaves under lamps brighter than noon. The early harvest filled baskets faster than stores could count them. Mila ordered the leaves served first, before they lost water, and marked the coming absence on meal boards.
