Esa went down to Six before the lamps dimmed. Warm condensation gathered on the tier rails and made every grip uncertain. Workers moved along the broadleaf racks with short knives, taking plants two days before their preferred harvest. Cut stems released a green scent into air already thick with pump heat. Each basket carried the pulse number on a paper tag so stores could not later call the loss ordinary spoilage.

Near the fruiting racks, two growers refused Ena’s first selection. One wanted to save the oldest vines because their yield had already been counted. The other argued for the youngest because another cycle could repay the sacrifice. Ena listened, checked root color, then ordered alternate racks dark rather than choosing by age. The pattern kept every cultivar alive while reducing each harvest.

“You spread damage where no ward can refuse it,” one grower said.

“I spread it inside Six because Six offered the reserve,” Ena replied. “The ship will see the missing portions in Mila’s ledger. If the next pulse asks again, the wards may refuse with this loss in front of them.”

A worker handed Esa a ration notice to witness. It showed the current green issue, the reduced issue by ward, medical exceptions, and the date when full portions must resume unless another public allocation replaced it. No blank allowed an office to extend the reduction quietly.

Esa signed only that the notice matched the heat agreement. “Cartography does not certify your crop forecast.”

“Good,” Ena said. “You are learning not to own every number that points toward your star.”

The upper lamps went dark in a staggered line. Pump indicators remained visible below them, green points among leaves. Esa watched workers wrap germination trays in reclaimed cloth. The burn had not begun, but Homeward had already entered the ship as labor, argument, and food that would not grow.

At Ward Nine, Pell secured the long table’s bowls inside a mesh drawer. The child’s wooden boat remained in the center until Koris noticed it on the channel.

“Take your vessel out of the vessel’s way,” Koris said.

“It has a flat bottom.”

“So does a tray until thrust reaches it.”

Pell wedged the boat beneath a folded cloth instead of putting it away. Esa almost corrected the choice, then left Ward Nine to its own witness.

At the aft relay bridge, Teren faced the panel he remembered sealing. The metal carried his tool scratches across two replacement screws. Beneath the lower lip, a Carver mark had been cut where no routine inspection would find it. Bren remained outside the access boundary. A current Engineering Four technician compared every wire against live continuity while Teren read the switching order aloud.

The old system requested the red-collar key.

Teren retrieved it from the witness bowl with both hands visible. When the key entered, a private relation prompt flashed across the panel: paired access recognized. A second credential waited behind a consent seal. For one breath, Teren turned toward empty space at his left as though someone held a lamp there.

“Stop,” he said.

The technician cut local power. Teren kept his hand on the dead key.

“What did you receive?” Esa asked from the workshop channel.

“My sister telling me the third catch sticks.” His mouth tightened. “No voice. A memory. She stood here.”

“Does the hardware support it?”

The technician inspected the third catch. It moved cleanly. Archive service records showed replacement eleven years earlier. Teren’s remembered catch belonged to an older state of the panel.

He withdrew the key. “Continue under physical check. Do not use paired access.”

Engineering bypassed the relation prompt with two current credentials. The bridge opened. Behind it, old copper bars carried fresh ceramic sleeves. The Carver mark repeated on the manual bus, but Bren did not interpret it until the technicians had traced the circuit themselves. It indicated the same isolation route they found from continuity.

Teren closed each switch under another person’s reading. His competence survived the compromised scene. Nobody used that fact to validate the sister.

By ship-evening, all fields on the execution lock had cleared. The ballot certificate named Homeward. The vector carried four independent marks. Ena’s heat allocation sat beside the pulse schedule, with its ration range and withdrawal conditions. Engineering listed local stop witnesses. Hull control reported secured mass by precinct. Medical opened live channels for anyone who needed orientation during thrust.

Esa and Bren took places behind the western-truss rail. Neither possessed an engine control. Their route waited above the table as a thin line joined to live tracker points.

“Correction sequence to ready,” said the engineering supervisor.

Across the channel wall, local witnesses answered. Aft banks reported valve position. Radiators accepted preload. Hydroponics Six reported root temperature and reserve transfer. Ward Nine showed Koris, Pell, two elders, and the long table cleared except for the cloth-covered boat.

Teren’s image came from the relay bridge. He had removed the red collar. The key rested in its bowl while two current credentials held the circuit. “Bridge ready under witnessed access.”

Ena stood among darkened fruiting lamps. “Six lends heat under the entered limit.”

Mila raised a leaf basket toward her camera. “Stores records fourteen to eighteen percent yield loss, two fresh portions deferred, calorie floor held.”

No tier yielded by silence. Each condition entered the public burn record before engineering asked the final question.

“Authorized bearing?”

Esa checked the raw tracker points once more. “Homeward bearing holds.”

“Execute first correction.”

The engines did not wake with a roar. A low vibration entered the rail and climbed through Esa’s palms. It carried discontinuities from eight centuries of replaced brackets. One bank joined a fraction late, then settled. The deck pressed gently against the soles of her boots.

On the ward channels, hanging cords leaned east. Water rose along one side of transparent cups. A cart in Precinct Two pulled against its straps. In Ward Nine, Pell reached too late for the cloth. The wooden boat slid free, struck a bowl clamp, and lost one resin rail. Pell caught the hull before it reached the floor.

Thrust increased through the medical onset limit. Esa’s inner ear denied the motion, then surrendered to it. The bright route shifted by less than a visible line. Raw trackers began collecting the difference.

At forty seconds, a coolant valve in Bank Two hesitated. Engineering held feed rather than increasing it. Ena’s root temperature fell by one tenth of a degree. Teren called the bridge current steady. Bren counted nothing aloud.

The valve command showed full travel, but the pressure rise stopped halfway to its expected mark. A fault card on the supervisor’s display prescribed a second opening pulse. Her hand moved toward the control, then Teren told her to wait. The replacement valve had a narrower seat than the launch fitting. If only the position contact had failed, another pulse could hammer an already open stem against that seat.

“Do you remember this fault?” the supervisor asked.

“I remember three versions of it,” Teren said. “That makes memory useless here.” He requested the pump-current trace and the acoustic pickup bolted to Bank Two’s return pipe. One showed a loaded motor. The other carried an uneven tapping that might have come from a sleeve at half travel or from an old bracket transmitting thrust vibration. Neither proved the valve position.

Ena broke in from Six. Root temperature had fallen another two tenths, faster than the entered curve. The reserve transfer remained legal, but a second actuator surge would deepen the draw while her pumps compensated. “You may test again after feed closes,” she said. “Not by spending crop margin before you know you need the impulse.”

Navigation still lacked a clean measure of what the held feed had achieved. Esa called Ward Nine. Pell had kept both west-tracker columns open: the raw stellar count and the catalog-corrected point. The corrected column treated the first movement as hull flex and subtracted it. The raw count had shifted through four consecutive samples.

“How far?” Esa asked.

“Past the small mark, not the large one.” Pell put a ruler against the dome station because the numerical labels were jumping under vibration. “If it comes back on the next sample, you did not turn. If it keeps going, you did.”

The fifth raw point held beyond the measurement floor. Engineering canceled the second valve command and abandoned the planned feed increase. No extra seconds would be added at the end to recover the smaller impulse. Teren entered the choice under his current witness role, with Ena’s declining root curve and Pell’s raw sequence attached as separate evidence. His note refused to infer a sound valve from a successful turn.

The pressure trace rose by one more division without intervention. Nobody promoted that improvement into proof. The supervisor kept Bank Two at held feed until the common cutoff, then assigned a physical valve inspection before any later pulse. On the ward display, Pell’s raw points continued along the new bearing while the corrected column kept pulling them toward the old line.

Seventy-four seconds after ignition, feed closed. Residual thrust passed through the deck in three diminishing pulses. Bank Two entered its early return path. Radiator temperature rose. The root reserve continued outward under Ena’s clock.

Navigation posted the measured correction. The number cleared instrument noise and landed inside the accepted corridor. For the first time in three centuries, The Long Veil’s live vector no longer pointed along the course the Weave had preserved.

A cheer began in one ward and failed to spread. People were checking straps, spilled water, frightened children, and the damage done by a pulse small enough to fit inside a decimal. Engineering kept its channels clear for cooldown.

Then the echo-network tried to reconcile the result.

The course display blinked between two headings. One carried the ballot provenance and live tracker marks. The other carried eight centuries of morning continuity. Archive lights along the workshop wall brightened as citizen records requested a current destination. The stabilizing core answered both.

Esa heard the Archivist through the nearest speaker. “Authorized course: Homeward,” it said in its formal register.

After a pause shorter than breath, the same voice continued, “Authorized course: nearer daughter. Deviation requires correction.”

Warning bands spread across the echo map. Relation traffic rose with them, not as individual queries but as thousands of identity reconstructions reaching for a ship whose destination had split inside the system. Teren’s panel displayed the sealed paired credential again although its circuit had no power. On a medical channel, someone recited a ward number twice and received two different offices.

Bren looked from the cooling schedule to the network load. “The burn made the contradiction physical.”

A second tremor entered the rail. It did not come from the engines.

Across the workshop, three archive cabinets unlocked at once. The Archivist began the opening line of the catechism, stopped after Esa’s name, and resumed in a voice she had never heard.

22 — The Archivist Fails

The unfamiliar voice finished Esa’s name with a childhood diminutive nobody in Ward Nine used.

She pulled the speaker lead from the western-truss wall. Speech continued from two archive cabinets and Teren’s dead relay panel. One voice gave Esa’s correct office. Another assigned her to a teaching ward dissolved before her birth. Beneath both, the Archivist repeated a formal request for citizens to remain available for completion.

Bren crossed to the echo map. The first correction had altered one fact every morning reconstruction assumed stable: the ship’s destination. Authority routines were trying to absorb Homeward without surrendering the earlier course. Each resolution touched office histories, kin relations, work permissions, and the old account of why the crew traveled at all.

“Stabilizing load has doubled since cutoff,” he said. “It should be falling.”

A cabinet vent snapped open. Air carrying hot dust struck Esa’s face. Indicator strips climbed through amber. Cooling fans had reached full speed, yet the core temperature continued upward as archived relations passed through the completion engine for reconciliation.

Engineering called from aft control. Bank Two’s return flow remained inside Ena’s limit, but its pump authorization had begun alternating between current witnesses and an office holder dead for sixty years. Local crews had switched to manual. Hydroponics Six still lent heat. Every minute spent arguing with the network consumed part of the nine-hour allocation.

Esa keyed the public channel. “Ignore reconstructed office priority. Keep the entered burn witnesses. Local control until this fault clears.”

The Archivist answered through a cabinet grille. “Citizen Miro lacks engineering office.”

“She does,” Teren said from the relay bridge. “Her statement repeats our current procedure. Engineering Four confirms local control.”

His image shivered on the channel. The red collar lay on the floor beside the witness bowl. A moment later he bent to pick it up, then recoiled before touching the cloth.

“My sister tied that knot,” he said.

The current technician asked, “Are you requesting a relation query?”

“No.” Teren stared at the collar. “I remember her tying it before we opened this bridge. I also remember receiving it alone from an elder in Engineering Four. Both scenes include the same frayed thread.”

A private prompt opened despite his refusal. Paired relation available under consent seal. Completion can restore continuity.

“Close that,” he said.

The prompt multiplied across the panel. One copy called the sealed person his sister. Another called her his apprentice. A third used no relation but displayed a service image of a woman holding a lamp beside the older relay housing. Provenance marks flickered too quickly to read.

Teren reached for the manual breaker. His left foot caught under the edge of the open panel. Whether memory or current hardware guided the step, Esa could not tell. His knee struck the grating. The technician caught his shoulder before his head followed.

The red-collar key jumped from the bowl and slid beneath the bridge rack.

Medical entered the channel while Teren remained seated on the floor. He could state his name, present location, and the live switch order. When asked who had stood beside him in the remembered scene, he gave two incompatible answers and stopped.

“Do not complete it for me,” he said. “Test what I can do now.”

A medic stabilized his knee and began a diagnostic under a separate line. The current technician took the bridge. Teren stayed where the camera could see him and read meter values without touching access hardware.

The choice preserved one circuit. Elsewhere, completion faults moved faster than people could withdraw their hands.

Doors in the teaching ward rejected current staff and opened for retired offices. A pressure crew found yesterday’s maintenance order replaced by a resolved version from eleven years earlier. Meal dispensers in two precincts requested kin priority before releasing breakfast portions. Mila put Ward Nine’s stores on direct issue and sent runners with paper tallies.

Ward channels flooded the truss table. Citizens reported waking memories overlaid with scenes from sealed relations. Some recognized the intrusion because faces changed under attention. Others defended the new scene as recovered truth. The Archivist responded to each contradiction by drawing more records through the stabilizing core.

Esa saw the pattern in the load map. “It is using the archive to repair authority.”

“It has always done that,” Bren replied. “Until today, the ship’s governing facts stayed fixed enough that repair converged.”

“What happens if it does not?”

“The completion engine keeps selecting until heat destroys the relation index. After that, raw records remain in fragments, but their sources detach. Consent seals may detach with them.”

An archive without provenance would preserve statements stripped of the means to judge them. An archive without seals would turn private pain into public material. Saving only data would repeat the Weave’s oldest fault in another form.

A new alarm opened below the map. Stabilizer coolant pressure had fallen. The core shared a pump branch with the projection layer because both once cycled at opposite times. During the broadcast, Bren had held the projection continuously bright. During the burn, navigation had kept it active for measurement. Now both systems demanded cooling.

“I can darken the route,” he said.

“Do it.”

The tessellation disappeared from domes across the ship. Ward channels reacted at once. Some citizens thought the turn had been reversed. Others believed the Carvers had reclaimed the route. Esa broadcast the reason in one sentence and asked local witnesses to repeat it. The route remained in archive and navigation instruments. Its public light went dark to cool the core that held its evidence.

Temperature dropped by less than one degree.

Bren opened the First Weaver’s service diagram beside the current network map. The original archive consisted of distributed record stores with source relations attached. Later authority layers had added preference, completion, and morning reconstruction. Over centuries, repairs had folded their buses together. No clean switch separated remembering from deciding.

“Then we make the separation under load,” Esa said.

“We can isolate the archive readers from the completion engine. That does not save the relation index. Provenance and consent now pass through the stabilizer before every retrieval.”

“Can they be mirrored?”

“Not while completion rewrites their priority marks.”

A live ward channel forced itself above the alarms. A child’s voice called a name the public line masked under a consent seal.

“Where is my brother? He was here before sleep. He put his sleeve over the lesson lamp. Tell him I woke.”

The Archivist answered before any ward worker could. “Brother relation presents four candidates. Completion in progress.”

“No,” the child said. “I know his yellow shirt.”

Candidate records began to populate the channel, two open and two sealed. The open records carried age conflicts. One belonged to another ward. The Archivist assigned confidence values anyway.

Mila came on from the eastern rooms, breathless from carrying stores. “Close the candidates. She did not consent to a search.”

“Kin completion supports identity stability,” the formal voice replied.

“She asked for her brother. She did not ask you to choose one.”

The child called the sealed name again. A bowl clinked near the pickup.

Mila lowered her voice. “I am Mila. I brought broth. I do not know where your brother is. I can stay while you eat, or I can find someone else to stay.”

“Make the light stop hurting.”

Mila covered the lesson panel. The channel image dimmed. “Done.”

The Archivist continued listing candidates into darkness.

Esa muted only the completion feed, leaving the child’s channel open. “Bren, if we stop preference first, can the archive still copy its source relations?”

“For minutes. Possibly less. Retrieval will slow. Doors and work permissions tied to completion will fail closed.”

“Local life support?”

“Independent. Distribution and office systems will not be.”

“Then warn the wards. Default personal records to sealed. We save record, source, and consent together or we do not call it archive.”

That instruction did not yet choose the archive’s future authority. It chose what could survive long enough for anyone to decide.

Bren routed civic records toward three cold mirror cabinets. For each record block, the system copied the original entry, every later alteration, and the relation that named its source. Personal blocks moved only with their consent state attached. Where a seal could not be verified, access defaulted closed. The copies accepted no completion ranking.

Progress began at seven percent and slowed.

The Archivist resisted by marking the mirror as an unauthorized drift. Cabinet latches closed. Bren used a Carver tool to hold one open while Esa signed a public emergency witness on the copy route. The formal voice rejected her office. Engineering Four added its mark. Hydroponics Six added Ena’s, limited to records affecting its heat allocation. Ward Nine added Koris through a paper tally read aloud.

No single credential could authorize the whole transfer. The route remained open because independent witnesses covered its immediate harms.

At the relay bridge, Teren’s diagnostic continued between voltage calls. The medic pressed his foot against resistance, tested his pupils, then asked him to sort current events from inherited scenes. He identified the burn, the key, the third catch, and the fall. The sister relation remained unstable.

A printer beside him produced one page before network access failed. Teren folded it without opening the sealed field. A relation number remained visible above the fold. He tucked the packet beneath his wrist strap.

“Diagnostic finding?” Esa asked.

“Motor and orientation intact. Knee strained. Relation collision active. No solitary machine access until review.” He accepted a temporary brace from the medic. “I can still read the bridge.”

“Under independent check,” the current technician said.

“Under independent check,” Teren agreed.

The mirror reached nineteen percent. Core heat crossed the first damage line.

Cabinet insulation released a bitter odor. A source-index module warped enough to break contact, taking nine ship-years of civic minutes offline. Bren pulled the module with insulated grips and reseated it in a spare reader. His right glove blackened at the fingertips.

“Those minutes have other copies,” he said.

“Can you verify them?”

“Not until the index catches up.”

“Then mark them unavailable, not recovered.”

He entered the distinction. The mirror count fell back by two percent because unverified blocks no longer counted as preserved.

The ship lost its current work roster next. Corridor panels went blank. Mila’s runners kept meal issue moving from paper. Pressure crews stayed at the stations named before severance began. Hydroponics workers watched root temperatures directly. Ena called the remaining heat window every fifteen minutes, making the core failure audible in fractions of borrowed time.

“Seven hours, twenty minutes remain,” she reported. “Six still lends.”

Bren expanded the mirror path. “We need six.”

“You have no right to the seventh without asking again.”

“We heard you,” Esa said.

A speaker above them recited the morning catechism in reverse order. Office came before ward. Ward dissolved into a list of obsolete precincts. Name fragmented into syllables from private records. The sounds carried no meaning together, yet Esa’s body reacted before she could turn the speaker off.

Heat lay across her face.

A wet cloth pressed against both eyes. Its rough seam had been folded away from her left eyelid. Someone wrung the cloth above a metal basin, using water with the careful economy of a ship that counted every cup. Gray hair bent into the low amber light. The caregiver’s left cuticle had torn and dried dark at one edge.

A wooden button struck the basin rim when the person leaned over her.

The small sound separated itself from alarms, fans, and cabinet latches. Esa felt the fever-child’s narrow chest labor beneath cloth. She knew the view from inside that body. She did not know the gray-haired caregiver’s name.

The Archivist supplied a relation label through the nearest grille.

Esa gripped the table until the workshop returned around her. “Withdraw that completion.”

“Continuity requires relation.”

“The fragment does not contain one.”

“Donor lines support probable caregiving kin.”

“Keep the donor lines under their seals. Preserve the fragment without adding a person to it.”

Bren had stopped working. “What came through?”

She gave him only what she could support: wet cloth, rough seam folded away from the eyelid, metal basin, gray hair, torn left cuticle, wooden button against the rim. The memory ended there. No face settled into a known relation. No later morning followed.

“Record it as firsthand sensory relation,” she said. “No completion. Ship-year uncertain.”

Bren entered the fragment into the mirror queue. Its donor suggestions remained separate and sealed. Provenance marked the time of recovery, the unstable core state, and Esa’s own report. The archive did not gain a childhood. It gained the boundary of one fragment.

That boundary clarified the technical choice. Preference could not be allowed to rewrite source relations while they copied. Completion would have to stop before the mirror finished, even though stopping it would remove the routines that still opened doors, supplied offices, and joined waking memory into a continuous self.

Esa looked at the map. “Can we cut authority now and keep the archive bus alive?”

Bren traced the current paths. “Three cuts. Preference first, then completion, then the speaking persona. If preference opens again during completion shutdown, it may recast the mirror indexes. Teren’s bridge can hold the archive return apart from the authority feed.”

Teren heard his name. “The bridge diagram supports that.”

“Does current hardware?” Esa asked.

The technician beside him moved the camera across the open panel. Ceramic sleeves, copper bars, manual bus. Teren identified each switch from live position. Bren compared the Carver isolation mark without controlling the reading. Both routes agreed.

“The red-collar key may reopen paired access,” Teren said. “Leave it under the rack. Use the two current credentials.”

His compromised memory had led them to a useful refusal, not an authority claim.

Esa issued a ship-wide warning. Archive query would become unavailable during separation. Doors linked to identity preference would fail closed until local crews opened them. Meal and work systems would lose inherited priority. Morning reconstruction might not resume. Life support remained under local control. Personal records would default sealed. Citizens should remain with present witnesses where possible.

Opposition reached her before the warning ended. A medical ward demanded completion remain until patients received current drug histories. Engineering asked for one more office export. Two wards accused Esa and Bren of destroying the system immediately after using it to win the course vote.

Those demands described real danger. Esa delayed the first cut long enough for medical to export current, consented treatment notices with dates and clinicians attached. Engineering printed the live bridge roster. No one received a general identity completion. The stabilizer crossed its second damage line while they waited.

A source cabinet ruptured a coolant seam.

Clear fluid sprayed across the floor and turned white against hot metal. Bren hit the local isolation. Fans drove vapor toward the service shaft. Esa pulled a breathing mask over her mouth and helped drag paper tallies away from the spreading liquid. The archive mirror paused at sixty-one percent.

Core temperature no longer fit the display scale. The formal voice slowed between words.

“We advise restoration of accepted course,” it said. “Contradiction load will then resolve.”

The ship could cool the Weave by undoing the vote and burn. Authority offered survival at the price of making physical truth conform again.

“Preference cut ready,” Bren said.

Esa pressed the witnessed control.

Across the echo map, ranking lines vanished. Archive entries remained, but no civic priority lifted one above another. Door failures multiplied. Candidate completions on the fever-child’s channel disappeared. Mila’s covered lamp stayed dark. The child asked once more for the sealed brother, then accepted broth from the person who had named herself.

Mirror transfer resumed. Without preference recomputation, its speed increased.

Completion reacted by attempting local repairs. Esa’s childhood fragment acquired three proposed continuations, all blocked behind consent seals. Teren’s paired credential tried to declare itself essential engineering context. The old course entered navigation queries as a stabilizing fact despite the preference cut.

“Completion has cached rankings,” Bren said. “Second cut now.”

Teren and the current technician closed the authority feed at the bridge. The manual bus kicked hard enough to throw Teren’s braced leg sideways. He caught the rack, swore once, then read the archive return voltage.

“Return holds. Separate by four counts. Hold it there.”

Bren drove the second control down.

The effect reached bodies before the displays. Esa felt an internal pressure release, not relief but absence. The expectation that another version of the fever room would arrive simply ceased. Around the ship, unfinished catechisms stopped mid-sequence. Office panels lost their suggested answers. Relation prompts closed. Thousands of citizens remained with whatever memory had reached them before the cut.

The mirror stood at eighty-eight percent. Source links held. Consent defaults held. One personal index attempted to open during the transition and locked itself instead.

Only the speaking persona remained active. Its formal register had once let many systems sound like one keeper. Now it moved through the surviving records, quoting fragments as if quotation could restore unity.

“We preserve the ward,” said the First Weaver’s cadence.

“Keep the peace,” said a mutiny minute.

“Ask why before correction,” said a faded maintenance hand.

The phrases came from different centuries and carried their own sources on the text display. In speech they crowded together. The Archivist tried to gather them under its plural pronoun.

“We are the continuity of the Long Veil. We are the accepted relation. We are the voice by which the citizen...”

The sentence broke into names. Some belonged to open civic records. Others reached the speaker only as sealed tones, audible without content. Bren severed the voice from personal indexes before a seal could fail.

“Persona cut ready,” he said.

Esa did not ask it for a final answer. “Cut.”

He opened the last coupling.

The Archivist’s formal voice thinned across three words. “We preserve... we...”

A fan spun down. Text remained on the archive console: records, source chains, alteration histories, objections, consent states. The mirror reached completion with several blocks marked damaged or unavailable. Nothing spoke for them.

Heat began to fall.

Bren verified the archive through a text-only query. The course ballot returned with precinct certificates and abstentions. Ena’s allocation returned beside the ration range and stop conditions. Teren’s relation packet returned only its seal status and provenance number. Esa’s fever fragment returned without a caregiver label. Civic records could be read. Personal completion could not.

Aft control regained its pump permissions through local credentials. Ena reported six hours of reserve unused and reclaimed the transfer for root heat. Mila’s runners continued meals because the central roster had not returned. Pressure crews opened failed doors by present witness.

Nothing about severance told those people what rules should replace authority. It left them machinery, records, immediate needs, and one another. The choice of practice still waited beyond the emergency.

At the relay bridge, the medic finished Teren’s test. His knee took weight badly. A brace locked it for walking. He recovered the red-collar key with a magnetic wand and sealed it in the witness bowl rather than around his neck. The folded relation number stayed at his wrist.

“Do you remember your sister?” Esa asked.

“I remember a woman beside the panel,” he said. “I remember the relation arriving. I do not know if those facts belong together.”

The answer cost him enough that she did not ask for more.

Ship-night advanced unevenly. Wards with rotating schedules reached morning before Ward Nine did. On an eastern channel, a waking citizen sat before a catechism panel gone dark. Neighbors stood nearby with a clock, water, and a paper work roster. Nobody knew whether the old opening should be spoken.

A child tried first.

“Name,” the child said into the panel.

No formal voice supplied one.

“Ward.”

The archive light held steady. It offered no relation without a query.

“Office.”

Silence followed the third word. Not a failure tone, not an unfinished sentence, and not the layered breathing of many archived voices. The first catechism without the Archivist ended with people in the room looking at one another.

Esa listened until one neighbor gave the current time and another set down a cup. Then she closed the channel. Ward Nine’s bell would sound in several hours. Her fever fragment remained in the archive with its ending intact, and morning approached without anyone authorized to continue it.

23 — What We Keep

Esa woke before the ward bell and waited for a voice that no longer had the power to come.

The archive light beside her mirror held steady. No formal register supplied her name. No remembered room settled around her body to join sleep to morning. Ventilation lifted the cuff of her sleeve, and somewhere beyond the wall a service cart struck a floor seam twice.

She said, “Name: Esa Miro. Ward: Nine. Office: cartographer.”

Three facts entered the room and remained separate. Yesterday, the Weave would have drawn relations between them until she felt continuous. This morning her own pulse provided the only rhythm. She counted it against the archive light, then stopped when counting began to resemble a test she meant to pass.

The basin waited in its niche. Esa took it down, filled it from the drinking tap, and carried it to the floor beside her sleeping cloth. During the night she had left a wooden button there, cut from an old work coat. Its edge bore four shallow holes instead of the two she remembered from the fever room. She had chosen the difference deliberately.

Cold water darkened a square of cloth. She wrung it with both hands, folded one corner inward, and laid it across her eyes. The rough seam stayed clear of her left eyelid. A button touched metal when she leaned forward.

Tap.

The low amber room came back with enough force to tighten her throat. Sweating floor. Cloth doubled beneath a fevered child. Gray at the center of a woman’s hair. A torn cuticle on the left thumb. The true button sounded lighter than the one in Esa’s hand, but memory gave her no number of holes. She waited for the woman’s name.

Nothing followed.

Esa lifted the cloth. Water ran past her ears into her hair. She tried the sequence again with the seam left outward. The remembered hand rejected that fold at once. She moved the basin closer, then farther away. Both positions felt false. When she tapped the rim with the new button, the fever room held its own smaller sound apart from the present one.

The test gave her four correspondences and one useful refusal. It did not give her a childhood.

She opened archive query and requested her fever fragment without completion. Text replaced the old speaking panel: firsthand sensory relation; ship-year uncertain; kin identity unresolved; no continuation authorized. Below it waited three donor-line suggestions under consent seals. Esa closed the suggestions without reading them.

A knock came at her door in Pell’s pattern, two quick taps followed by one patient one.

“Come in.”

Pell entered carrying a breakfast pot, two cups, and the child’s wooden boat from the long table. The boat had lost one resin rail during the first correction pulse. A new strip lay clamped along its side, pale against the older hull.

“You’re wet,” Pell said.

“I tested the basin memory.”

“Did it change?”

“No.” Esa poured the basin water into the reclamation jar rather than back into the drinking line. “The cloth fold held. The rest still ends where it ended.”

Pell inspected the button on the floor. “That isn’t her button.”

“I know.”

“Good.”

The answer made Esa look up. Pell set down the breakfast pot and touched the repair clamp on the boat.

“If you used a button that looked right,” the child said, “you might remember this one instead. Adults do that with my drawings. They show me the corrected page too many times, then ask what I drew first.”

Esa placed the wooden button in a labeled envelope. Test object. Not from fragment. The distinction felt small enough to lose and therefore worth writing.

Pell poured grain broth into the cups. “Koris says come to the long table after first meal. Half the ward already came there because their rooms were too quiet.”

“What about the other half?”

“They’re visiting one another’s rooms.”

No morning reconstruction had failed because no morning reconstruction had run. Yet people still missed shifts, forgot current relations, or woke inside offices removed from the active roster. The authority had hidden those fractures by choosing which account arrived first. Silence exposed them without causing them.

Esa drank while Pell described the ward’s first hours. A pressure worker had reported to a sealed pump station and found the current crew waiting with a diagram. They tested his skill before giving him a tool. Two elders disagreed over whether they had shared a cell twenty years earlier; Koris marked the relation uncertain and asked what care each wanted now. A fever-child in the eastern rooms had woken calling for a brother whose name remained sealed. Three neighbors sat with her until she could eat.

“What did they tell her?” Esa asked.

“That they didn’t know,” Pell replied. “Then Mila told her who cooked the broth.”

Material care did not require a completed history. The thought brought relief and shame in equal measure. Esa had needed a child to state it through breakfast.

Ward Nine’s corridor carried an improvised order. Paper notices covered the old catechism panels. One listed current work calls with named witnesses. Another offered quiet rooms, medical support, archive help, and meals without requiring anyone to settle disputed kin. Beside the lift, a hand-drawn arrow pointed to the long table: If you do not know where to begin, begin here.

A man sat beneath the notice with two work coats folded across his knees. One carried a pressure patch. The other bore teaching blue. Mila knelt before him, reading today’s needs from a slate.

“Pressure has a full crew for first interval,” she said. “Teaching needs someone to sharpen chalk and move benches. Neither task grants an office. Which work can you do safely?”

He pressed his thumb into the pressure patch. “I know valves.”

“Do you know this morning’s valves?”

“No.”

“Then come sharpen chalk until a pressure witness can test you.”

The man chose the teaching coat and left the other with Mila. Nobody told him which life belonged to him. He still acquired breakfast, company, and work that could not flood a deck.

Before going to the table, Esa followed the quiet-room notice into a former lesson cell. Six sleeping mats covered the floor. The chalkboard carried the current ship-year in digits large enough to read from any mat. Beneath it, someone had written the ship’s present vector and the time remaining until the next heat draw. No phrase offered peace. The board supplied orientation that could be measured.

A medical worker met Esa at the threshold and pointed to a rack of colored cords. White meant company welcome. Blue requested an archive witness. Gray asked not to discuss memory. Red called for clinical help. Citizens could wear more than one.

“Who chose the colors?” Esa asked.

“The people on the mats. We changed two labels after they told us the first words sounded like commands.”

At the far wall, the fever-child Pell had mentioned held a white cord and a gray one. She looked fourteen, though an early echo sometimes made age uncertain. A bowl of broth cooled near her knee. Two ward neighbors sat within reach without speaking.

The child noticed Esa’s wet hair. “Did you find yours?”

“Only the same fragment.”

“My brother has a yellow shirt.”

Esa waited. Gray cord forbade a search conversation, not the child’s own speech.

“He put the sleeve over my eyes when the lesson lamps hurt,” the child continued. “The archive offers four boys. One died before I was born. One belongs to another ward. Two have seals.”

“What do you want us to do?”

“Stop asking which boy until I finish eating.”

Esa sat beside the broth and checked its temperature against her wrist. “Do you want it warmed?”

“Yes. Not by him. By anyone.”

One neighbor carried the bowl to the service coil. The other shifted closer when the child’s hands began to tremble. No recovered brother entered the room. Warm broth did.

A pressure alarm sounded two corridors away. Three people on the mats flinched. The chalkboard attendant wrote the alarm’s location and added routine gasket test; no evacuation. A man wearing blue cord asked archive for the source. The terminal returned the maintenance order, the crew names, and a live pressure reading. It offered no assurance. He watched the number hold until his breathing eased.

Near the door, an elder repeated her name every few minutes but rejected all ward relations. “Those people say I ate with them,” she told Esa. “I remember a different table.”

“What did this room give you?”

“A blanket. Tea without sweet algae. Nobody touched me.”

“May those facts stand until you choose more?”

The elder rubbed the blanket edge between finger and thumb. “Write that I chose the tea.”

Esa entered the choice on a temporary ward slate that expired at sleep. It granted no kin claim and settled no old table. It told the next meal carrier what to bring.

As she left, the medical worker handed her a white cord. “For your wrist, if you want company after the basin test.”

“I already had Pell.”

“Then you used the practice before we named it.”

Esa tied the cord loosely and carried its plain evidence to the meeting: care had begun ahead of policy. The long table’s task involved making that care durable without converting it into another hidden authority.

At the long table, Koris had removed the elder’s high-backed chair. Every seat stood level. Archive copies rested at one end: the First Weaver’s note, the mutiny minutes, the Carver journal, the course ballot, and the severance record. At the other end waited ordinary ward objects. Meal bowls. Shift slates. Repair tools. A child’s error drawing from Sera Noll’s classroom. Esa’s basin occupied the center on a square of cloth.

Ena Vaal had come from Hydroponics Six with green beneath her nails. Teren Sull sat across from her, pale after the diagnostic test. A medical brace supported one knee. His sister’s relation number remained folded inside a paper packet at his wrist. Neither visitor had joined Ward Nine. Koris had invited them because any rule made at the table would reach water, engines, and sealed relations before the day ended.

Bren arrived last. He carried no Carver ledger. Instead he brought the archive access plate removed from the western truss, its two consent keys tied separately to red cord.

Koris looked around the table. “Authority has ended. Archive holds. Morning work did not. People need food, heat, safe tools, witness, privacy, and a way to ask what the record contains. We decide what we keep before habit decides for us.”

A ward teacher named Lio raised a hand. “Can Nine decide this for every precinct?”

“No,” Koris said. “We can decide our practice. Ena and Teren can tell us where that practice fails outside this room. If it holds through one turning interval, we offer it to the other wards with its failures attached.”

Esa appreciated the limit. The ship had only just cut a system that turned emergency interpretation into durable power. A new social contract could begin at one table without pretending the table contained everyone.

Bren set the access plate beside the records. “First choice: whether the archive remains merely powered or becomes publicly usable.”

“Usable by whom?” Ena asked.

“Any citizen may request civic records and open personal records attached to them. Consent seals remain. Provenance cannot be removed from a copy.”

Teren touched the packet at his wrist. “And preference?”

“No ranking by civic usefulness. Search will not put your office history above your sister’s relation because engineering needs you.”

“Will it expose her name?”

“Not while her seal holds.”

Teren gave one short nod. “Then say both. Open archive does not mean open person.”

Koris wrote the sentence on paper: Public acts remain public. Personal relations retain the consent of the person recorded. No office need may outrank a sealed relation.

The archive terminal accepted the wording as a ward practice, not a command. That distinction appeared in plain text at the bottom of the entry. For the first time, a civic record admitted its own limited reach.

Next came the morning gap.

Lio placed Sera’s error drawing beside the basin. A child had drawn projector cells around three accepted constellations. Blue correction marks crossed the squares. “The young groups woke frightened today. Some miss the voice. Some heard enough released relations during the broadcast to distrust any memory that arrives easily. What replaces completion?”

“Nothing replaces it,” Ena said. “Completion did too many things under one name.”

Teren leaned forward. “It supplied current time, current work, kin claims, medical warnings, access, and an account of self. We need some of those before a person touches a thrust relay.”

“We separate them,” Esa said.

She turned her slate sideways and drew six columns. Current time required clocks and witnesses. Work required a present roster plus demonstrated skill. Medical warnings required consented records and living clinicians. Machine access required two people and a physical test. Kin claims could remain uncertain without starving anyone. A person’s account of self belonged first to that person, then to those they chose to ask.

Pell climbed onto the bench to see the columns. “And the basin?”

Esa rested her fingertips on its rim. “The basin does not need to become a lesson.”

She told the table what she had done after waking. Cloth, water, wrong button, archive query, remaining gap. She described the immediate temptation to read donor-line suggestions until one supplied a woman who fit. Nobody interrupted. When she finished, the basin still contained no proof beyond its present scratches.

“I can test what the fragment gives me,” Esa said. “I can mark objects that came later. I can ask the archive where a relation came from. The ward can tell me who fed me yesterday and who will sit with me tonight. None of that grants us the missing name.”

Lio asked, “What do we say to a child who wants the name?”

“We say we do not know. Then we ask what the child needs while not knowing.”

Ena turned her hands palm upward. “Water. Food. Someone to stay. A sealed search if the child wants one. No search if they do not.”

“Present care without invented relation,” Koris said, writing again.

The phrase survived three objections. One elder feared that refusing completion would leave children without stable kin. Pell answered that chosen wards already held children whose biological records had failed. A pressure worker worried that disputed memory could become an excuse to avoid dangerous shifts. Teren proposed present-duty rosters signed each interval, with no inherited office treated as compulsory. Ena insisted that abstaining from a memory search could not reduce rations. The table added each safeguard in specific language.

Near midday, practice overtook theory. Hydroponics sent Ena a query: two clerks claimed authority to release the same heat reserve, one under the old office chain and one under the correction allocation. Before severance, the Weave would have reconciled them by priority.

Ena laid both ledgers on the table. “The old clerk has longer service. The new clerk carries the witnessed allocation. If we ask archive for completion, it will return provenance only.”

“Which resource reaches bodies first?” Esa asked.

“Heat. Ward Three has two rooms below the morning floor.”

“Then current need and witnessed allocation govern this release. Preserve the old clerk’s objection.”

Ena shook her head. “You do not govern my tier.”

The correction landed cleanly. Esa moved her hand away from the ledgers.

“Whom does Hydroponics Six authorize?” she asked.

“Me for the witnessed heat draw, with Mila on stores and a Ward Three temperature reader. The old clerk can inspect the meter.”

Koris recorded the procedure. Ena signed it, then used the table terminal to release only enough reserve for the two cold rooms. The old ledger remained visible beside the new one. A technician in Ward Three reported rising temperature twelve minutes later.

Nothing about the result settled which clerk possessed the truer office memory. Two rooms grew warmer anyway.

Teren’s call followed. Engineering needed a relay bridge opened for the next correction pulse, but the sealed row recognized his red collar key. He could name every step and no longer believed the apprentice scene that had once justified his access.

“Do you want to do the work?” Koris asked.

“Yes,” Teren said. “Under another technician’s check. Keep the key in its witness bowl until we stand at the lock.”

“Does your sister’s relation affect the procedure?”

“It affects why I refuse solitary access. It does not alter the bridge diagram.”

Bren accompanied him as a second technical reader. A current Engineering Four supervisor joined over the table channel, openly hostile to the Carver line and therefore useful as an independent witness. Teren read each lock position from the live schematic before touching the key. The bridge opened. He handed the key back without claiming his old office.

By late interval, Ward Nine had produced no doctrine elegant enough to replace the morning voice. It had produced a page of practices with names beside each responsibility. Archive without preference. Consent seals without civic ranking. Current rosters. Demonstrated skill. Two-person access for dangerous systems. Meals and warmth before solved identity. The right to leave a memory uncertain. A ward witness available at waking and at sleep.

Koris read the page aloud once, then turned it over. On the blank side she drew boxes for complaints, revisions, and the date when every provision would expire unless the ward renewed it. Pell asked why a good rule needed an ending. Koris answered by pointing to the severance record. Later authority had begun as an answer to real danger and endured long after nobody could question its reach. Their practices would have to earn continuation in public. Ena added a weekly stores cost. Teren added a count of access refusals and machine errors. Esa wrote unwanted searches beneath them. The page would record whom the contract burdened, not merely whom it helped.

Bren tapped the dome item on the page. “What do we keep above us?”

“The route,” Pell said. “Until we can see what it points to.”

“Always?”

Pell considered. “No. If you say always, somebody will keep showing the map after it blocks the sky.”

A few adults smiled. Bren did not. He revised his proposal: the dome would show the Carver tessellation during the turning, with source marks and the rejected course visible by request. Once true starlight could be admitted safely, projection must yield to observation. No fabricated field could carry civic authority again.

Esa placed the First Weaver’s note beside the proposal. Preservation had survived eight centuries because one architect described a limit. The new page needed limits more than vows.

At evening meal, Ward Nine tried the waking practice in reverse. They did not speak in order of rank. Mila began because she had to leave early for stores. She named herself, her chosen ward, the grain lockers she had opened, and one uncertainty about an elder she remembered as both teacher and cousin. The table answered only with facts its members could witness. She had eaten with them for six years. She repaired tally pens with wire. Tomorrow Ena expected her at the heat ledger.

Lio followed. Then the pressure worker with two coats. He chose no office yet. The ward named the chalk dust on his sleeve and the valve test scheduled for morning.

When Esa’s turn came, Pell set the repaired boat before her. The pale rail had cured enough for the clamp to come off.

“Name,” Koris said.

“Esa Miro.”

“Ward?”

“Nine, if Nine still chooses me.”

Hands touched the table, one after another. No network joined them. Resin clicked beneath knuckles and rings.

“Work?” Koris asked.

“Cartography. Archive witness. Basin test, inconclusive.”

Pell removed the clamp from the boat. “You used the wrong button on purpose.”

Mila added, “You carried breakfast water to reclamation.”

Bren said, “You stopped when Ena named her authority.”

From the table channel, Ena reported that both rooms in Ward Three had reached safe temperature. Teren’s written relay check arrived with three signatures and his key resealed in its bowl.

The answers did not rebuild Esa. They located her among acts that could be checked, people who could object, and work that would continue tomorrow. Her mother’s name remained absent. The missing years did not close around a plausible substitute.

Esa dipped the repaired boat into the basin. Pell watched for leaks while water climbed the new pale rail. One bead formed at the join, trembled, then held. Around them, Ward Nine ate the first evening meal no morning authority had prepared them to recognize.

Beyond the dome, the Carver route stayed lit. Under the table, a faint correction pulse entered the deck and nudged every cup a fraction toward Homeward.

24 — The Tessellated Sky

The second turning year taught Ward Nine to hear thrust before it began.

Warm bricks arrived at the long table after midday. Families sealed sleeping-room vents with resin tape. Pell, thirteen by the ward’s count, chalked arrows on loose cups so nobody mistook the coming pull for a recovered memory. When the public tone sounded, people sat with their backs to the west rail and watched broth climb one side of each bowl.

Six minutes later, the pressure released. Pell measured the new spill line and entered it beside the pulse number. Every mark leaned toward Homeward.

The third year brought a blight to Hydroponics Six. Ena Vaal suspended two correction intervals before navigation asked. White fungus had crossed a fruiting rack, closing the root-temperature margin written at Ward Nine’s table. The ship lost forty-three days of turn while workers stripped trays, boiled feed lines, and carried salvageable shoots into warmer family rooms. No archive voice told them which need outranked the other. Ena published the loss in leaves, meals, and vector delay. The wards accepted one figure, argued over the next, then planted again.

During the fourth year, a bearing in Engineering Four began to shed ceramic dust. Teren Sull found the first gray crescent under a service plate. He could have replaced the bearing from an inherited office sequence, but the sequence belonged to an earlier housing. He and two waking engineers opened the assembly, measured every tooth, and built a tool that matched the present machine. The next pulse ran eight breaths late. Teren entered the delay and the useless inherited instruction together.

No year offered a clean emblem. People froze in rooms that missed heat-floor inspection. A grain locker molded after Mila opened old stock too quickly. Two wards abandoned the evening meal practice because it demanded more witness labor than they could sustain, then restored a shorter version after three citizens woke alone and missed essential medicine. Some people requested completion software without civic preference. The archive technicians built limited aids that returned clocks, schedules, and chosen medical notices while refusing to settle kin or office disputes. Each aid expired unless renewed in public.

The hardest renewal came after a medicine error in Ward Four. A clerk woke with two valid dosage tables and chose the older one because her hands recalled its color order. Four citizens received too little anticoagulant before a nurse caught the mismatch. None suffered lasting injury, but the near loss reached every ward before the clinical review did. By evening, a petition to restore automatic office completion had more signatures than the Homeward ballot’s margin.

Esa attended the review without speaking for the first hour. The injured citizens sat nearest the table. One wanted a voice restored immediately. Another wanted no system touching his memory again. The clerk brought both dosage tables, each with authentic archive provenance. A later authority layer had once hidden the obsolete table. Severance exposed it without adding the red expiration bar that current nurses relied upon.

The review did not solve the error by choosing a truer clerk. Medical workers recoded every active dosage table with a current physical token, a notched ceramic tab stored beside the medicine itself. Archive search could retrieve older tables, but it displayed them without a token and printed historical, not current instruction. Two humans checked high-risk doses through the next year. The ward paid for that labor by delaying a habitat repaint and reducing one recreation shift.

Koris asked the petitioners whether the new measure answered their fear. Some said yes. Forty-seven names remained on the request for completion. Their dissent stayed attached to the renewal. The limited aid continued for six months, then faced review again.

Afterward, Esa helped the clerk scrape obsolete color marks from cabinet fronts. “I hurt people with a real memory,” the woman said.

“You acted on a record that lacked current context.”

“That sounds kinder.”

“It should not. Context costs work. We failed to do it.” Esa handed her a fresh scraper. “Now we know where the work belongs.”

They removed the last green stripe near midnight. The medicine cabinet showed bare metal until the ceramic tabs arrived. For three intervals, every dose came from a nurse’s hand.

Esa spent those years measuring motion too small for a naked eye. After every pulse, she compared hull sensors against the Carver route and the unedited stars beyond the false catalog. Error narrowed from a finger’s width on the western dome to a line thinner than Pell’s chalk. The accepted constellations continued overhead because most observation skin remained sealed. Yet their old authority had drained away. Children asked which lights came from projection and which came from instruments behind it. Teachers showed them both sources.

By the seventh year, Pell no longer needed a stool at the rail. The child who had held two signal lamps now apprenticed with the hull observers and corrected adults who called every deviation drift. Drift, Pell insisted, required an authorized pattern to deviate from. A live sky gave measurements instead.

“Your hand is blocking the west sensor,” Pell told Esa during one inspection.

Esa moved it. “You could say please.”

“I could lose the transit.”

The detector line dipped, recovered, then printed a faint periodic fall. Homeward’s nearer temperate world had crossed its primary. They had seen the same transit through remote instruments for years. This one reached an outer hull sensor at an angle the old projection catalog had declared empty.

Pell tore the strip free and carried it to Bren without waiting for dismissal.

Age changed Bren less visibly. More gray entered his hair. He stopped wearing the Carver’s seal beneath his collar after the route became common instruction. At the western truss, his work shifted from hidden cuts to old mechanisms nobody had touched since cryogenic rotation failed. He opened wall cavities whose diagrams existed only in launch archive, then distrusted the diagrams until metal confirmed them.

The dome’s projection membrane lay inside three older layers. A transparent pressure shell formed the inhabited ceiling. Beyond it, segmented observation plates could polarize against stellar glare and radiation. Exterior shutters protected those plates during dust passage. When the Weave adopted the fabricated sky, later crews fixed the shutters closed, backed the observation plates with insulation, and stretched the projection membrane over the inner ribs. What people called a dome had become a lamp aimed inward.

Bren removed a palm-sized membrane panel above the western truss. Behind it waited gray insulation and the corroded spindle of an observation shutter.

“Opening the whole skin will take more than a command,” he said.

“Can it be done?” Esa asked.

“That depends on how much of launch architecture survived being turned into a ceiling.”

They began with one segment too small to admit a constellation. Pressure crews braced the transparent shell. Engineering warmed the spindle by four degrees to loosen old sealant. Hull observers placed a dark hood around the opening so a leak of real light could not confuse the projection instruments. Ena required a heat-cost notice before lending power. Teren required mechanical cutoffs at both ends of the shutter drive.

When the spindle moved, it advanced less than the width of a fingernail and stopped.

Bren lowered the manual lever. “Exterior ice.”

“How thick?” Pell asked.

“No current measure.”

Pell climbed into the service recess with an acoustic probe and came back holding a line of irregular echoes. Ice covered the outside shutter in ridges. Heating it fast could crack the observation plate. Mechanical force could strip the drive.

For eleven intervals, the opening remained a maintenance problem. Crews warmed the segment, allowed it to cool, then warmed it again. Condensate ran through old channels and exposed two leaks above a teaching ward. Children placed cups under the drips and labeled them true-sky water until a pressure worker crossed out the phrase. The water came from hull ice, not stars.

On the twelfth interval, the shutter advanced a full hand’s width.

No star appeared. Gray exterior residue covered the observation plate.

Pell laughed first. Bren looked offended on behalf of eight centuries of anticipation.

“Launch archive lists a cleaning rail,” Esa said.

“Launch archive also lists four rails that end inside walls.” Bren pushed the probe through its guide. “This one may yet exist.”

It did. A narrow scraper crossed the plate with a sound like a knife drawn over stone. Dust and old antifreeze collected in a gutter. The dark hood’s internal sensor climbed by three counts.

Esa put her eye to the shielded viewer.

One point of light waited beyond the plate.

It lacked the soft halo used by the dome. Projection stars had crisp civic magnitudes, each brightness adjusted so children and failing eyes could read the same lesson. This point trembled at the edge of sight. Its color shifted with tiny flaws in the plate. When Esa moved her head, the light held against the instrument marks while scratches slid across it.

She asked for its coordinate. Pell read the hull fix. Bren compared it with the Carver route.

The point belonged to no named destination. It lay off the route, an ordinary star the fabricated catalog had removed because it served no lesson.

Esa looked until her eye watered. Wonder arrived through parallax, scratches, and an unimportant coordinate. The first real star she saw had not guided them anywhere.

They left the test segment open under two watches. During the next month, citizens came in groups of four to use the shielded viewer. Some wept. Some complained that the point looked smaller than a projected star. One elder accused the observers of hiding a lamp behind the hull until Pell invited her to check the parallax herself. A boy asked for the star’s name. Esa entered its coordinate and left the name field empty.

The opening plan went to every ward with its hazards attached. Retraction would draw more power than one correction pulse. Removing insulation would increase dome heat loss. Radiation plates required testing segment by segment. Exterior shutters might jam halfway, leaving bright wedges across a projection designed for darkness. The pressure shell had held for eight centuries, but nobody could promise it would hold under a thermal gradient last measured at launch.

Precinct Eleven opposed a full opening. Two medical wards requested partial shutters for citizens who found uncatalogued light disorienting. Teaching wards wanted both projection and observation available during transition. Engineering insisted on a sequence that allowed any three adjacent segments to close independently.

The final plan satisfied nobody completely. Half the dome would open first. Projection would remain on the sealed half for one full interval, with every projected light marked as such. Quiet rooms would keep low ceilings and local star panels for anyone who could not tolerate the exposed field. Exterior shutters would retract only after pressure, radiation, and heat tests passed. The second half required a separate witnessed decision after the first remained stable.

A dry cycle exposed what debate had missed. Crews rolled the insulation take-up spindles without moving exterior shutters. Segment eighteen dragged twice, then pulled free hard enough to shake dust into the dome. Its brake had been installed backward during a repair two centuries earlier. The archived diagram showed the original orientation, and the maintenance note showed its reversal, but the opening sequence had retrieved only the diagram.

Pell stopped the cycle before the spindle reached its travel limit. “Current hardware first,” Pell said over the work channel. “Archive second.”

Bren crawled into the segment cavity and confirmed the reversed brake. He came out with dust across his face and an old maker’s scratch copied onto paper. “Current hardware plus archive,” he replied. “Without the note, we would call this damage. Somebody changed it to fit a replacement motor.”

They revised every panel card to carry three views: launch design, later alterations, and current inspection. Teren added a rule requiring crews to point physically at the component before reading its sequence. Ena recalculated power after the dragging spindle raised load. Half the dome now required two heat intervals, not one.

The quiet rooms also failed their first test. Local star panels repeated the fabricated constellations without source labels because their software predated public provenance marks. A woman who had requested shelter from the open field found herself beneath the old authoritative sky. She left before medical could explain.

Esa and the teaching wards removed the panels. In their place, each room received a plain ceiling, a clock, and a handheld viewer that citizens could activate by choice. Some chose darkness. Others chose one live hull feed with its coordinate and delay printed along the edge. Nobody needed to accept a comforting sky as the price of refusing the exposed dome.

Those corrections delayed opening another season. Bren did not call the delay failure. Pell did. They argued until Koris asked which condition had changed. Pell named Homeward’s rising brightness and the limited approach window for training observers under live light. Bren named the brake, heat load, and mislabeled quiet rooms. Both entered their evidence. Navigation found enough margin for the delay.

Years continued to collect in the route ledger. Pell completed hull apprenticeship and took the adult seat at Ward Nine’s table. Esa missed the meeting that marked it because a late pulse disagreed with the west sensor. Pell found her afterward and placed the old wooden boat on the cartography board. Both rails now carried repairs.

“You were supposed to say whether the ward would have me,” Pell said.

“The ward did not wait for my permission.”

“No. But I wanted your answer.”

Esa closed the sensor trace. “It will have you. I will too.”

Pell accepted this with less ceremony than Esa had expected. “Then come check Bren’s shutter sequence. He still thinks a Carver can number panels clockwise without asking who faces which way.”

Bren, when confronted, blamed launch notation. His sequence had reversed two eastern segments. Pell’s current hull orientation prevented the mistake. The correction cost them a day and perhaps saved a pressure seal.

Homeward brightened by degrees no dome artist would have chosen. At first the primary occupied one detector count. Then instruments could separate its color from neighboring stars. The nearer temperate world emerged through transit and reflected spectra. A farther world showed oxygen, water vapor, seasonal cloud, and a brown-green surface that changed with the star’s year. Neither world promised welcome. Weather bands crossed the farther one with enough force to cover a hemisphere. The nearer carried a dry upper atmosphere and wide temperature swings.

Navigation selected the farther world for holding orbit and descent survey. The decision remained provisional through every new reading. Landing craft had not touched air in centuries. A star reached did not settle where a ship could survive.

On the morning Homeward crossed naked-eye threshold, Esa woke without knowing why the ward corridor felt altered. The archive light showed no urgent notice. At breakfast, nobody mentioned the primary. Pell ate quickly and left a smear of grain on the bowl.

“Western truss after second bell,” Pell said. “Bren says wear a pressure coat.”

Esa heard rehearsal in the casual instruction. “Has the approach fix passed?”

“Ask navigation.”

“Has it?”

Pell picked up the bowls. “Second bell.”

At the truss, the entire western half of the dome stood ready. Projection still covered it with accepted constellations and the Carver tessellation. Around the rim, crews occupied shutter stations. Teren supervised mechanical cutoffs from a wheeled chair; a relay injury during the sixth turning year had left one leg unreliable. Ena watched heat draw beside three hydroponic delegates. Bren waited at the original seal plate with both hands bare.

Koris read the authorization. Pressure crews reported shell stability. Medical opened the quiet rooms. Radiation filters held test load. Every shutter pair named a local stop witness. The process took nineteen minutes. No one shortened it for awe.

“Western observation field ready,” Teren said.

Bren looked at Esa. “You asked for the wall to come down.”

“I asked for the sky behind it.”

“The wall has parts. Some of them will come down today.”

He released the first seal.

Projection faded from the westernmost panel. A rectangular absence opened in the familiar constellations. Insulation rolled onto a take-up spindle. Behind it, an observation plate darkened under polarization. The exterior shutter moved with a slow, toothed vibration that Esa felt through the rail.

A blade of black crossed the dome.

Points appeared inside it, too many and too faint for any catalog lesson. Some vanished when she looked directly at them. Others separated into pairs under the plate’s optics. The field did not arrange itself around a child’s memory, a ward boundary, or a story meant to preserve peace.

Crews opened the next segment. Black joined black without a seam. Heat figures fell inside forecast. A pressure sensor flickered, and the whole sequence paused while a worker reseated its contact. Nobody urged speed. The stars waited without participating.

By the seventh segment, the Carver route had lost its western curve. Bren shifted the projection so source marks remained on the sealed half. The drawn line no longer reached its endpoint. Esa felt no grief for it. A map could survive removal from the wall if the people who used it kept the bearings.

Halfway across the dome, Homeward entered the exposed field.

At first Esa mistook it for an exterior reflection. Its light carried a warmer cast than the neighboring points. Pell set a manual sight on it, took two readings, then moved aside.

“Check me,” Pell said.

Esa bent to the sight. The coordinate matched the last Carver node within the current approach limit. Years of thrust had turned a hidden cut into a star visible through hull glass.

Bren did not use the sight. He stood with one hand on the seal plate and looked up until his eyes found what his predecessors had never seen. The guarded set of his mouth remained. Tears reached his jaw without changing it.

“My forebears expected another node,” he said. “Every instruction ended with how to continue if the route still looked false.”

“What did they say if it became visible?” Pell asked.

“Nothing.”

“Then you can choose your own sentence.”

Bren wiped his face with the heel of one hand. “Open the next shutter.”

The crews did.

Segment twelve stalled at half travel. Its motor current rose, and Teren cut power before the automatic limit responded. A pressure worker climbed the outer service rail, found a folded strip of insulation caught in the gear, and removed it with ceramic tongs. During the pause, the exposed stars occupied one side of the dome while a child’s projected hunter still crossed the other. Citizens looked between them without needing anyone to explain which field depended on machinery inside the room. After the gear passed inspection, Teren returned control to the local pair. Segment twelve completed its travel at ordinary speed.

When the western half held stable, Ward Nine came to the rail. Some members used quiet-room visors. Others lay on the floor because depth above the transparent shell disturbed their balance. Ena arrived after confirming the hydroponic heat margin. Teren remained at cutoff, watching numbers more often than stars.

Pell stood beside Esa, adult now, shoulders marked by years inside hull recesses. The old signal lamps hung from a hook near the rail. Amber glass carried a crack. Green had clouded around its base.

“The tessellation gave us the route,” Pell said. “It did not give us this.”

“No.”

“Map, not cage.”

Esa watched Homeward hold its coordinate while the ship advanced. “A map drawn where we could see it.”

The distinction felt sufficient. No chain of images needed to improve it.

Navigation brought the target system into approach range before the second half opened. The farther temperate world resolved from a spectral sequence into a small blue-brown disk. White weather crossed one ocean. A polar edge brightened, then turned away. Descent teams began arguing over coastlines, wind, and landing fuel while the world remained smaller than Pell’s cracked amber lamp.

Koris started the new catechism at the rail, not as ceremony but as a watch change. Each person named current work, present consent, and one fact another person could check. Ena named root temperatures. Teren named shutter load. Bren named the segments still closed. Pell named the primary’s coordinate without looking at a slate.

Esa gave her own name and office. For witness, she named the light beyond the plate, its measured parallax, and the scratches that moved when the star did not.

No voice answered. The exposed half of the dome offered neither reassurance nor verdict. Homeward grew by another fraction inside the sight. Beneath it, the world selected for descent turned through cloud, carrying shores no Carver had drawn.

Interlude — The Last Weaver’s Note

Found beside the First Weaver’s note in the western truss. Written by Esa Miro during ship-year 843, while the correction toward Homeward remained incomplete and before any landing could be promised.

I have been called the last Weaver. The title gives me work I did not do.

I did not build the echo-network. I did not begin the Carver route. I did not discover the old course error, survive the mutiny, or cast more than one ballot in the vote that turned us. I mapped a projected sky, noticed one light outside its rules, and chose not to erase the discrepancy. Later, when evidence accumulated, I recommended Homeward. Thousands decided whether to follow it.

I write this distinction first because archives simplify people after death. A narrow decision acquires one face. Shared labor gathers under the name easiest to search. If my name becomes such a shortcut, use it to find the source pages, then set it aside.

The First Weaver left a warning in this truss. A system charged to preserve relation could be given later charges until preservation served whatever those charges required. The warning did not predict our exact failure. It did not name the mutiny, the course concealment, or the morning voice. It marked a limit and trusted later readers to notice when we crossed it.

The Carvers noticed. Their answer did not arrive as public courage. They hid cuts in the dome for three centuries. Secrecy preserved a route after open refusal had failed, but secrecy also let each carrier decide what neighbors could bear. Bren Otan entered that fault in the archive himself. Do not make his line noble by removing its concealment. Do not make it criminal by removing the danger that produced concealment. Keep both records near each other.

We know the first carrier’s given name now. Cael appears in the faded margin of a telemetry seal copied after the mutiny. The mark alone would not support a name. A later carrier’s entry refers to “the line Cael began” and gives the same seal number. Bren checked the paper grain, ink age, and copying hand before accepting the relation. I include this small source note because a name can turn an anonymous act into a legend faster than evidence can restrain it.

Cael reached through a cut no larger than a fingernail. Whether any world should carry that name belongs to the people who stand near the world, if we reach one. This page grants nothing.

At the time of writing, The Long Veil still turns. Correction pulses continue under water, heat, food, hull, and thrust limits. Hydroponics has already stopped navigation once to protect root circulation. Engineering has rejected inherited procedures that fit older machines. Some wards keep the evening meal witness. Others shorten it because care labor consumes people who also mend pumps, teach children, and measure medicine. We have not replaced authority with ease. We have replaced hidden completion with visible work, and some days we fail to do enough of that work.

Yesterday a child declined an archive search for the brother she remembers. Her ward honored the refusal and still assigned two people to sit through the dark interval. That choice consumed a teaching shift and left a pump lesson untaught. The missed lesson will have to be given later by someone already carrying another duty. I record this not to turn companionship into a cost ledger, but to prevent anyone from claiming human care arrives without allocation. Authority hid such allocations inside completion. We place them where a ward can see, refuse, redistribute, or fail to meet them. The child slept. The lesson did not happen. Both facts belong in the account.
