Four citizens received the wrong medicine this year after a clerk used an authentic but obsolete table. They recovered. The error strengthened those who want automatic completion restored. Their argument deserves a record without ridicule. A voice that selects current instruction can prevent injury. The same mechanism once selected which sister a relay technician could remember, which destination a crew could name, and which childhood a fevered cartographer could claim. Useful selection can grow into authority while every addition still answers a real danger.

Therefore, if you build aids after us, make each function plain. Let a clock give time. Let a current roster assign present work with consent. Let a medical notice carry the clinician, date, and table that authorized it. Let archive search return provenance and uncertainty. Do not bind those functions into a speaker that tells a waking person what all the records mean together. If you believe such a speaker has become necessary, state its powers where every ward can contest them. Give each power an ending date.

My own test remains incomplete. During the severance of authority, I recovered a fever-room fragment. A woman cooled cloth in a metal basin, folded the rough seam away from my eyelid, and spent scarce water on me. A wooden button touched the rim whenever she bent. I do not know her name. I do not know whether she remained until morning. The archive offers possible kin relations under consent seals, but no relation carries enough provenance to close the gap.

Ward Nine sits with me when the gap hurts. Pell brought breakfast after the first silent waking. Koris records what our table can witness. This care does not prove who held the cloth. It does not need to.

I ask whoever reads this to resist two opposite errors. Do not call the fragment meaningless because it lacks completion. Do not expand it into the childhood you think I deserve. Preserve the wet cloth, folded corner, gray hair, torn cuticle, and button against metal. Preserve the empty space after them. Uncertainty recorded honestly carries more of a person than a merciful invention.

The same rule applies to Homeward. We possess a route, a reachable primary, two temperate candidates, and growing measurements. We do not possess a shore. Spectra can tell us about water vapor, oxygen, cloud, and heat. They cannot promise that landing gear will hold, that soil will feed us, or that weather will spare the first shelter. If this note survives a failed approach, do not edit its hope into prophecy. We turned toward evidence, not guarantee.

I have three requests, though no request on this page binds a future ward.

Keep the archive usable. Public decisions should remain open with their sources, objections, costs, and later consequences attached. Personal relations should retain consent seals. Open archive must not become open person. When a record carries uncertainty, copy the uncertainty with it.

Keep some practice by which people tell one another what happened during the day. Our long table works for Ward Nine because the table already held meals, repairs, disputes, and children’s boats. Another ward may need a different practice. Preserve the function, not our furniture. A self should have living witnesses who can say what they saw without claiming ownership of everything the person remembers.

Keep observing beyond whatever instrument becomes familiar. The Carvers used a false sky because the false sky occupied the one surface everyone shared. Their cuts converted that surface into evidence. If later generations come to trust one catalog, one lens, or one civic explanation without checking what lies outside its frame, the failure can recur under different names. No wall needs to call itself a sky in order to limit sight.

These requests can conflict. Full records can injure privacy. Witness practices can become compulsory. Observation can consume resources needed for food or shelter. Do not preserve my sentences by ignoring those conflicts. If a future ward rejects one request after public review, attach its reasons to this page. An unanswered instruction hardens into ritual. A challenged instruction remains accountable to the living.

I withheld the first anomalous coordinate from the archivist. I lied about a sensor error. Those acts opened the inquiry and also repeated the Carvers’ private certainty. Later I carried the recovered evidence to Ward Nine, then asked the ship to hear it in full. The broadcast caused terror, conflicting relations, work failures, and pain that no disclaimer could prevent. I still believe staged disclosure would have preserved the suppression too long. Belief does not refund the cost.

I recommended the turn. I accepted responsibility for that recommendation and refused responsibility for the choice citizens made after hearing it. The boundary remains important. It also remains imperfect. My office gave my words reach. My measurements shaped what the ship considered possible. A person can avoid command and still influence thousands. Preserve the ration range beside my speech. Preserve Ena Vaal’s suspension conditions, Teren Sull’s independent handoff, Precinct Eleven’s refusal, the abstentions, and the count against Homeward. Otherwise the archive will turn recommendation into destiny.

No one person woke this ship. The First Weaver left a limit. Mutiny survivors preserved enough of their error to expose it later. Carvers cut a route. Children noticed projector cells and wandering lights. Workers carried the broadcast. Wards argued. Engineers moved mass. Hydroponics lent heat and light under conditions it could revoke. Citizens who opposed Homeward kept the vote honest by remaining visible after they lost.

If arrival comes, those facts should come with us.

I once believed cartography meant giving every light a place. The projected catalog rewarded that belief. Each point had a civic name, lesson, and approved relation. The first real star I saw through an opened test segment had no destination value. It trembled behind scratched hull glass at a coordinate the old lessons omitted because it helped nobody remember the approved sky. I left its name field empty.

Perhaps another cartographer will name it. Perhaps no one will. The light does not require our use.

The First Weaver’s note and mine lie together because later hands placed them so, not because I answer the architect across eight centuries. I cannot know what answer the First Weaver would accept. I can say what we attempted: archive without completion, care without invented kin, work without inherited entitlement, a course chosen after evidence and objection reached the same room.

We may arrive. We may fail within sight of the target. In either case, do not write certainty backward into this year.

My name is Esa Miro. Ward Nine has chosen me more than once. I mapped a false sky and helped read a route hidden inside it. At this writing, Homeward remains ahead of us. The engines will pulse again after the next heat review. Pell has left a repaired wooden boat on my worktable, and one pale rail still leaks when the basin water reaches its top.

That is where the record stands.

Esa Miro, sky-cartographer

Ship-year 843

26 — Homeward

The landing craft rejected the first shore before anyone left orbit.

Its terrain model marked a broad pale shelf beside the western ocean. From the open dome, the shelf looked level enough to hold every ward on The Long Veil. Radar found channels beneath the surface, each covered by a crust no landing strut could trust. A craft that settled there might remain upright for minutes, then push through into salt mud.

Pell removed the site marker from the descent board. Years in hull observation had broadened the child’s shoulders and sharpened the direct voice into something crews obeyed before remembering how young it had once sounded.

“Site Three goes,” Pell said. “No argument from orbit can thicken the crust.”

A pilot objected that Site Four lay eighty kilometers inland and required crossing a mountain wind. Pell placed the radar section beside the atmospheric plot.

“Wind gives us a bad descent. Three gives us a false landing. Choose the difficulty that ends when the engines stop.”

Esa watched the room accept the distinction. Adult Pell no longer needed to point at a light until someone older agreed to see it. The board changed under Pell’s hand.

Site Four occupied a basalt terrace above a narrow estuary. It had stable rock, liquid water below, and a slope gentle enough for cargo sleds. Cloud moved across it every nine hours. The survey could not predict how local organisms would affect them. Habitable described pressure, temperature, water, and chemistry within human tolerance. It did not grant permission to remove a helmet.

Bren stood at the back of the descent room with both hands inside an open guidance cabinet. He had aged through the turning years without losing the habit of distrusting finished panels. Where Pell read surfaces and loads, Bren traced the old choices hidden behind them.

“Craft Two still accepts archive preference on final approach,” he said.

Teren Sull’s voice came from the orbital relay. “We severed that bus.”

“You severed it on the ship. This controller copied its own landing profile before authority separation.”

“Can it issue command?”

Bren withdrew a narrow crystal strip. “Not now.”

The strip carried a green status light even after removal. Its stored profile favored the nearer dry world, selected centuries before Homeward returned to public memory. Had the craft entered atmosphere with that preference intact, guidance could have interpreted every deviation toward Site Four as error and trimmed against the pilots.

Pell held out an evidence sleeve. “Log it before you break it.”

“I know archive practice.”

“You know concealment practice better.”

Bren’s face closed. Esa expected an old quarrel. Instead he placed the strip in the sleeve.

“That,” he said, “is why you witness me.”

Pell sealed it. Their agreement contained no affection, but trust had acquired working parts.

Two landing craft had survived eight centuries in vacuum cradles. One could fly after nine years of repair. The other had donated seals, guidance gyros, and half its heat tiles. Craft One would carry twelve people: two pilots, four surface engineers, a medical pair, Koris as civic witness, Pell for hull and terrain, Bren for legacy systems, and Esa for cartography. The first cargo consisted mostly of things they hoped not to need: pressure shelter, lift bags, sealant, heat blankets, water assay equipment, medical isolation, and fuel for immediate ascent.

No seed crossed in the first load. Ena had refused every request to carry a ceremonial tray.

“We do not plant into chemistry we have measured from orbit,” she had said. “Bring me water, soil, spores, and weather. Then I will decide what living thing risks the second descent.”

Hydroponics packed six empty sample boxes instead.

During the last orbital interval, navigation needed a name for the world’s common chart. Homeward Two had begun to sound permanent. Koris called a ship-wide naming witness, not yet a binding ballot, and asked each ward to submit names with reasons and source marks.

Esa went to the archive margin she had cited in her ship-year 843 note. The telemetry seal occupied a paper sheet no wider than her palm. Mutiny clerks had copied engine values across its center. Along the lower edge, a faded hand had written: Cael retains the rejected bearing. Ask why before correction. The main record classified the line as an obsolete maintenance comment.

A seventh Carver entry, made generations later, referred to “the line Cael began under seal 44-C.” Fiber comparison linked that entry to a copy of the same sheet. No surname survived. No office could be proven. Cael may have objected during the mutiny, inherited another person’s numbers, or discovered the bearing afterward. The records supported a name and an act of retention. They did not support a founder’s life.

Bren read the margin through the archive viewer. “My family turned Cael into the first Carver because every line prefers a beginning.”

“Do you reject the relation?” Esa asked.

“No. I reject the person we built around it.” He magnified the faded sentence. “This hand kept a bearing somebody ordered removed. That much holds.”

Pell leaned over the viewer. “Cael’s Reach.”

Esa looked at the planet below. “For the world?”

“For the act. Reaching does not promise arriving.”

Bren considered the words longer than either of them. “Enter the source beside the proposal.”

Esa submitted Cael’s Reach with the margin image, the seventh entry, and Bren’s objection to any fuller legend. Other wards proposed Second Water, Open Sky, Wardfall, and names drawn from the nearer daughter they had not chosen. The binding vote would wait until the first landing report. A world should not receive a public name before anyone could report what the name touched.

Launch restraints closed around Esa’s shoulders shortly before local dawn at Site Four. The craft cabin faced inward. No window showed the planet. Each seat carried a small instrument panel and a pressure hood. Pell occupied the hull station opposite Bren’s legacy console. Koris sat between two sample boxes with the naming proposals strapped beneath her feet.

“Descent authority?” the senior pilot asked.

“Pilots execute,” Koris answered. “Any technical station may call abort. Civic witness preserves decisions and does not fly.”

“Archive guidance?”

“Read-only maps. No preference trim.”

“Surface claim?”

“None. We inspect, stabilize, sample, and report.”

The pilot closed the checklist. “Release Craft One.”

Clamps opened with four impacts. Weight vanished. Esa’s harness held her against the seat while maneuver jets pushed the craft clear of The Long Veil. For several minutes, vibration provided the only evidence of motion. Then the primary pilot rolled them toward atmosphere and the cabin gravity shifted from absent to uncertain.

Pell read hull temperatures. Bren read guidance disagreement. Both spoke in numbers until upper air reached them.

First contact sounded like dry grains dragged across the hull. The noise deepened. Heat rose along the craft’s underside and entered the cabin through the seat frames. Esa watched a row of tile sensors turn amber. One at the aft quarter climbed faster than its neighbors.

“Aft thirty-two diverging by six degrees,” Pell said.

“Within old limit,” Bren replied.

“Old limit assumes original adhesive.”

The surface crew had replaced that tile twice. Pell called for a shallower angle. The pilot checked the trajectory cost.

“Shallow adds four minutes in heating.”

“Peak drops nine degrees. Thirty-two needs peak relief.”

Bren opened the replacement record. “Adhesive batch came from cradle seal stock. Long heat performs worse than peak.”

Pell looked across the cabin. “You signed that substitution.”

“I did.”

“Which failure comes first?”

“At current rise, peak separates the tile in ninety seconds. Shallow exposure softens adhesive in three minutes.”

The pilot had less than a breath for their answer.

“Lower peak for two minutes, then steepen,” Pell said. “Watch the bond sensor.”

Bren entered the hybrid profile manually. “Concur.”

Craft One lifted its nose by three degrees. Heating spread across more tiles. Aft thirty-two slowed, then held. After two minutes the pilot steepened. The bond sensor stayed green by one count.

Air thickened into force. Esa’s chest pressed against the harness. Every breath required intention. The cabin panels shook too quickly to read until vibration filters caught up. Outside, ionized flow blocked the ship relay. For seven minutes, the landing party possessed only local instruments and one another.

A warning tone cut through the noise.

“Pressure shell?” Koris asked.

“Exterior antenna loss,” Pell said. “Expected in hot flow. Internal shell holds.”

The tone repeated because the old controller expected archive acknowledgment. Bren reached toward mute, stopped, and asked the pilot for permission. The pilot gave it. He silenced sound without clearing the event.

When plasma faded, the atmosphere began to speak in other ways. Rain struck the forward hull. Crosswind rolled the craft hard enough to drive Esa’s shoulder into the seat frame. Radar found Site Four through cloud, lost it behind a mineral-rich downpour, then found it again twenty kilometers east of prediction.

Mountain air descended across the terrace in pulses. The craft compensated with fuel reserved for ascent. Each correction shortened the margin for immediate return.

“Abort remains possible for forty seconds,” the junior pilot said.

Pell studied terrain velocity. “Terrace stable. Wind cycle repeats every eleven seconds.”

“Three cycles measured,” Koris said.

“Enough to forecast, not enough to trust.” Pell marked the lowest interval. “We descend during the lull. If it shifts by more than two seconds, climb.”

Bren checked the controller for hidden trim. “Manual response clean.”

The fourth lull arrived late. The pilot held altitude. The fifth came at eleven seconds. Craft One dropped through rain.

Esa felt the landing gear deploy beneath her feet. Four green marks appeared. The left aft strut turned amber.

“Latch incomplete,” Pell said.

“Recycle?”

“No time. Load it last.”

The pilots tilted the craft by less than a degree so three confirmed struts would touch first. Basalt rushed up on the radar display. Crosswind struck once more, weaker near the surface. Engines fought it sideways.

First contact drove a hard blow through Esa’s spine. A second impact followed as the forward struts compressed. The right aft touched. Left aft remained unloaded while the craft leaned above it.

“Weight transfer stable,” Pell said. “Hold thrust at twelve percent.”

Outside engines threw rain and stone across the terrace. Surface radar mapped the incomplete strut. Its foot hung a hand’s width above a shallow depression.

Bren had already unlatched his harness. “Manual jack can extend it.”

“Not while you’re sitting inside a craft on three legs,” Pell said.

“The jack controls sit in the outer gear bay.”

“I know where they sit.”

Pell ordered two lift bags deployed through the cargo floor. Surface engineers fed them beneath the left quarter using rigid poles, then filled them from landing gas. Only after pressure transferred into both bags did Bren enter the outer bay in a sealed crawl suit. He found a bent latch pin, drove it clear, and extended the strut seven centimeters.

“Foot contact,” he reported.

Pell watched the load cells settle. “Transfer two percent at a time.”

The lift bags softened by measured steps. Four struts accepted weight. Engines throttled down.

One system still kept them isolated. The exterior antenna lost during hot flow had not deployed its backup after landing. Without it, the craft could transmit only through a narrow emergency beam that required The Long Veil to cross directly overhead. Orbit would carry the ship below the terrace horizon in twenty-six minutes.

Pell pulled up the hull camera. The backup mast lay folded beneath a skin panel warped by heat. “I can reach it from the upper service eye.”

Bren examined the panel fasteners. “The eye opens beside tile thirty-two. Its adhesive has not cooled.”

“I do not need to step on the tile.”

“You need to cross its load frame.”

Pell drew a path through the camera image. “Tether here, knee on the antenna rib, left hand on the cold rail.”

“The rib carried launch vibration, not a suited adult.” Bren called up the original section and the later repair. “Its inner bracket lost one bolt when they donated parts to Craft Two.”

“Then brace the bracket from inside.”

They built the procedure together while the ship moved toward the horizon. Bren crawled into the upper equipment bay and set a jack beneath the weakened rib. Pell entered the service eye in a pressure suit, keeping all weight off the hot tile. Esa saw only fragments on the hull camera: one boot, a tether clasp, rain breaking into steam against ceramic.

“Panel pin fused,” Pell said.

“Rotate a quarter turn toward your right,” Bren answered.

“My right or the diagram’s right?”

“Your right. Current body before archive.”

The pin moved. Pell lifted the warped panel enough to free the mast. Wind caught it before the hinge locked. Bren’s jack took the load. Pell hauled the mast back through its arc and drove the manual latch with a tool tied to the wrist.

Signal bars rose on Koris’s board. Teren’s voice reached them through static. “Craft One, orbital relay confirms surface carrier.”

Pell sealed the service eye and came inside with one glove scorched at the knuckle. Bren lowered the jack. Neither praised the other. They entered the missing bolt, hot-tile route, and manual latch force into the landing record before removing their helmets.

Silence arrived badly. Cooling metal clicked. Rain hissed on the hull. Pumps continued because no crew trusted a quiet machine immediately after landing.

The senior pilot kept one hand on ascent. “Craft stable. Fuel margin below immediate-return plan by eight percent. We can still reach orbit if wind permits.”

Koris entered the number. “No speech yet. Complete surface checks.”

Esa had imagined arrival through a dome, a shore filling the view. Instead she watched pressure figures while sweat cooled under her collar.

External chemistry probes extended first. Air pressure matched the survey. Oxygen could sustain unassisted breathing, but aerosol samples contained complex spores and reactive salts. Medical prohibited open helmets. Rainwater carried organic structures unlike ship algae. None penetrated suit filters during the test.

A surface camera unfolded beneath the craft. Basalt occupied most of its view, black and ridged. Low silver growth clung in cracks where rainwater ran. Beyond the terrace, the estuary reflected a strip of pale sky. Waves moved inland against the wind.

Pell pointed to the repeating line. “Tide.”

Navigation models had predicted one minor moon. The observed tide carried more range than that moon explained. Something else pulled the water, perhaps a second small body hidden in cloud or a resonance from the nearer world.

“Does it reach the terrace?” Esa asked.

“Not on current marks. We have one partial cycle.”

The landing site had changed again. Stable rock did not mean stable access to the estuary. Pell added tide stakes to the first surface plan.

Koris authorized the outer hatch after one hour of sampling. Nobody stepped directly onto the world. Surface engineers sent a tethered crawler down the ramp. Its first wheel slipped on wet basalt. Bren adjusted the ramp feet. Pell placed traction mesh from the hatch to the nearest ridge.

Esa descended fourth. Her suit made each step deliberate. Rain struck the helmet hard enough to hide the craft lights when she looked down. At the foot of the ramp, she set one boot on basalt and waited for the rock to answer through pressure.

It gave her weight back.

No catechism entered her mind. No archive marked the contact complete. She checked the suit seal, named her position to Pell, and moved aside so Koris could descend.

Work divided at once. Medical established a clean line around the ramp. Surface engineers anchored the pressure shelter to cracks rather than drilling unknown silver growth. Bren opened a cargo latch that had warped during heating. Pell walked the terrace edge placing tide reflectors and load markers. Koris set a folding table beneath the craft’s lee and began the first local record.

Esa carried the survey baseline to a basalt rise twenty meters from the ramp. The instrument legs sank into mosslike material, so she moved them to bare stone. Her first sight line ran from the landing craft to the estuary bend. The second fixed a mountain notch above cloud. The third returned to the craft and missed by four millimeters because wind flexed the mast.

She measured again.

Bren joined her with the recovered guidance strip sealed at his belt. “Still making the world fit a triangle?”

“Making the triangle admit wind.”

He looked toward Pell, who had driven three red tide stakes into the lower path. “Cael never reached this.”

“No.”

“My line did not reach it either. We kept a bearing. Other people built the arrival.”

Esa leveled the instrument. “Say that at the naming witness.”

“I intend to object to the name.”

She turned from the sight. “You entered your support in orbit.”

“I supported the source. Down here, the word makes my family too large.”

Bren pointed to the landing craft, the pressure shelter, and Pell’s markers. “None of that came from us.”

The objection did not surprise her. It made the proposal more honest.

They carried samples to Koris’s table as local dusk approached. Air remained suit-safe but unapproved for breathing. Water required isolation. Silver growth responded to light by folding narrow fronds into rock cracks. The tide reached the lowest red stake two hours earlier than orbital prediction, then stopped. Craft One could not launch until wind dropped, which models placed after dawn.

No first night feast occurred. The landing party ate sealed grain paste inside the pressure shelter while rain pressed its roof inward between ribs. One heater failed. Bren repaired it with a contact stripped from the obsolete guidance profile. Pell maintained tide watch and refused relief until Esa carried a second chair to the hatch.

The naming witness opened over delayed relay. Wards aboard The Long Veil received the landing report before any proposal. Koris listed the bad shore, tile heat, fuel loss, incomplete strut, spores, tide error, heater failure, stable basalt, breathable chemistry, liquid water, and living silver forms. No one heard only the word habitable.

Esa presented the Cael margin and later Carver reference. Bren followed.

“Cael retained a rejected bearing,” he said. “My family carried that retention and also hid it. This landing belongs to pilots, repair crews, wards, growers, voters, and people who opposed the course but maintained it after losing. If you choose Cael’s Reach, let reach name an unfinished action shared by those people. Do not make Cael our founder.”

Pell spoke next from the hatch. “I support it because reach includes missing. We missed the first shore. We missed the weather timing. We reached this terrace after changing the plan.”

Other names remained on the ballot. Voting took one full tide interval. Cael’s Reach carried without unanimity. Precinct Eleven preferred Second Water. Hydroponics split between Cael’s Reach and Open Soil, though Ena entered a note that nobody had yet proven the soil open to anything.

Koris closed the count. “Homeward names the primary. The farther temperate world enters the public chart as Cael’s Reach, with source record, objections, and landing report attached.”

The new name appeared on Esa’s survey board. It did not change the rain.

Near dawn, cloud broke above the estuary. Homeward cast a warm line across the water. Real stars emerged around it, shifted into constellations no projected lesson had prepared. The Long Veil crossed overhead as a moving point, dome open, thousands of people waiting to learn whether a second craft should descend.

Ward Nine gathered by relay at Koris’s folding table. Pell came in from tide watch with silver mud on both boots. Bren brought the repaired heater contact. Esa stood beside her survey mast, one gloved hand on the instrument that had finally closed its triangle.

“My name is Esa Miro,” she said. “Ward Nine. Cartographer of The Long Veil. Surface surveyor of Cael’s Reach for this interval, if the ward consents.”

“The ward consents for this interval,” Koris answered.

Pell laughed. “You made the office expire.”

“We learned to date authority.”

Bren named himself as legacy-systems mechanic, not Carver. Pell named hull observer and landing witness, then reported the next tide in forty-three minutes. The ward answered each with work it had seen and limits it expected them to keep.

Esa did not claim wholeness. The fever room still ended after the button touched metal. Her early years remained partly borrowed. On this shore, nobody could author those years into certainty. They could hold a survey mast against wind, check her seal, bring a chair to the watcher, and correct her when four millimeters mattered.

Behind them, silver fronds opened as Homeward rose. Ahead lay shelter tests, microbial study, water treatment, another landing, and the decision of whether any permanent settlement should stand on the terrace. Hard work did not make arrival provisional. It made arrival exact.

Esa looked from the open dome moving overhead to the adult Pell beside the tide stakes, then to Bren warming both hands around a cup repaired twice. The Carver route had ended. Their responsibility had not.

Esa said, “We are home. And we are who we chose to be.”

A new tide touched the lowest red marker. Pell noted the time. Esa turned her survey book to the next blank page.
